Chapter 01 - Yeyar Kon Kalikkaama Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam.

Thiruchitrambalam. Sekkizhar Peruman's Periya Puranam, Part 839, Vampara Vari Vandu Sarukkam, Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar Puranam. In the Periya Puranam, in the Vampara Vari Vandu Sarukkam, following the Puranam of Thirugnanasambandha Peruman, is the Puranam of Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar. It contains 409 verses. The Puranam of Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar is connected to the Puranam of Sundaramoorthy Swamigal. Therefore, the middle part of Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's Puranam is contained within the Puranam of Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar. At the beginning of this Puranam, Sekkizhar Peruman sings about the country and city of Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar. In about 20 verses at the end of this Puranam, Sekkizhar Peruman connects the Puranam of Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar with the Puranam of Sundaramoorthy Swamigal.

The Puranam of Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar is presented. In the intervening approximately 380 verses, the middle part of Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's sacred history is contained. At the beginning of the Periya Puranam, in the Thaduthatkonda Puranam, we contemplated the sacred birth of Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, the arranged marriage of Sundaramoorthy Swamigal with the daughter of Puthur Sadangavi Sivachariyar, the history of Lord Shiva Himself coming and taking Sundaramoorthy Swamigal as His own, and the marriage of Sundaramoorthy Swamigal with Paravai Nachiyar. This was the initial part of Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's sacred history. Now, in the Puranam of Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar, we find the middle part of Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's sacred history. It contains many divine events performed by Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, including his visit to Thiruvottiyur and his marriage to the divine mother Sangili Nachiyar. All these important parts of the Puranam are contained within this Puranam of Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar. Subsequently, in the later part of the Periya Puranam, in the Puranam of Kazharitrarivar Nayanar, the continuation of Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's sacred history appears. Finally, in the Vellana Sarukkam, the completion of Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's sacred history is presented. Therefore, although this is titled Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar, the majority of Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's sacred history and its central part are located here. The sacred history contained in about 69 Thiruppathigams (hymns) composed by Sundaramoorthy Swamigal is this Puranam of Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar. Therefore, we shall now begin to contemplate the Puranam of Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar. We know that the Thiruthondar Thogai (collection of hymns) composed by Sundaramoorthy Swamigal is a compendium, the Thiruthondar Thiruvandhadhi (anthology of hymns) composed by Nambiandar Nambigal is a type of work, and the Thiruthondar Puranam, also known as Periya Puranam, composed by Sekkizhar Peruman, is an elaborate work. Therefore, in the Thiruthondar Thogai, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal sings about Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar as follows: "Eyarkon Kalikkaman Adiyarkkum Adiyen" (I am a servant to the servants of Eyarkon Kalikkaman). This is what Sundaramoorthy Swamigal sang about Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar in the Thiruthondar Thogai. "Eyar" is a family name, and "Kon" indicates the head of that family. It also refers to the head of the army, the commander-in-chief of kings.

Therefore, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal sings in the Thiruthondar Thogai, "Eyarkon Kalikkaman Adiyarkkum Adiyen," meaning, "I am a servant to the servants of Kalikkaman, who lived as the head of such an Eyar lineage." The "um" (also) in "Adiyarkkum" implies that Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar is also a servant to the devotees praised previously in the Thiruthondar Thogai. We shall see in this history the connection and friendship between Sundaramoorthy Swamigal and Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar, which later came about through divine grace. Therefore, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, even while composing the Thiruthondar Thogai, sings in this manner, guided by divine grace, about the history that is yet to unfold. Thus, we have contemplated the part concerning Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar in the compendium. Now,

In the Thiruthondar Thiruvandhadhi, a type of work, Nambiandar Nambigal praises Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar in the 35th verse. That verse is as follows: "Kottaththiral Enthai Thanthai Than Thanthai Engoottamellam Thettrachchadaiyaai Neenatha Adiyen Thigazh Vanthondanē Matra Ippini Thavirppaan Endru Udaivaal Uruvi Annoi Setru Thavir Kalikkaman Kudiyeyar Seerkudiyē." This verse is found in the Thiruthondar Thiruvandhadhi. It means, "My victorious and strong father, his father, and his father, and all our lineage, are Your servants, O Lord with matted locks. This shining Vanthondan (Sundaramoorthy Swamigal) is the one who will remove this affliction." Saying this, he drew his sword, ripped open his stomach, and removed the disease. That Kalikkaman's lineage is the glorious lineage of the Eyars. Thus, Nambiandar Nambigal has sung about the Puranam of Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar so concisely. We are now going to elaborate on this in the Puranam of Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar. This Puranam, which is part of the Periya Puranam and titled Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar Puranam, is the second Puranam in the Vampara Vari Vandu Sarukkam, following the Puranam of Thirugnanasambandha Peruman. This section narrates the history and character of Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar. In the Thiruthondar Thogai, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal sang, "Vampara Varivandru Mannara Malarum Madhumalarnar Kondraiyaan Adiyalaal Penaa Empiraan Sambandhan Adiyarkkum Adiyen" (I am a servant to the servants of our Lord Sambandhan, who worships only the feet of the Lord with the fragrant Kondrai flower, where bees with striped wings hum). In accordance with this, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal sang about Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar as "Eyarkon Kalikkaman Adiyarkkum Adiyen." This Puranam is the second one in the Vampara Vari Vandu Sarukkam of the Periya Puranam, which serves as an explanation for the verse of the Thiruthondar Thogai that begins with "Vampara Varivandu."

Sekkizhar Peruman explains this Puranam in 409 verses. Among them, verses 1 to 8 describe the country, city, lineage, family, servitude, and other glories of Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar. They also narrate the anger of Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar and his criticism of Aarooran (Sundaramoorthy Swamigal) for sending Lord Shiva as a messenger. Following this, from verse 9 to verse 382, Sekkizhar Peruman describes the history of Sundaramoorthy Swamigal. After composing the Thiruthondar Thogai, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal worshipped many holy places, went to Thiruvottiyur, swore not to leave, married Sangiliyar, then remembered Thiruvarur, and because he broke his vow, he lost his eyesight. On the way, he regained his left eye in Kanchipuram, reached Thiruvarur, regained his other eye, but was unable to enter Paravaiyar's mansion. Paravaiyar was in a state of 'oodal' (a lovers' quarrel). Sekkizhar Peruman describes the history up to the point where Sundaramoorthy Swamigal sent the Lord as a messenger to resolve this 'oodal'. After this, from verse 383 to verse 409, Sekkizhar Peruman narrates the history of Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar's anger towards Sundaramoorthy Swamigal for sending the Lord as a messenger, the onset of a severe stomach ailment (Sulai Noi) for Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar by divine command, the Lord's instruction to Sundaramoorthy Swamigal to cure the ailment, Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar's act of stabbing himself in the stomach with a sword to end the disease and his life upon learning this, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's decision to end his own life with the same sword upon seeing this, and then, by divine grace, Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar's revival and his grasping of the sword, leading to their reconciliation and friendship. With this, Sekkizhar Peruman concludes this history. Through this, we can understand the peaceful resolution of Kalikkama Nayanar's history and its continuation in the history of Kazharitrarivar Nayanar. Thus, the introduction to the Puranam of Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar is presented. Now, we will continue to elaborate on the Puranam of Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar in the extensive Periya Puranam. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 02 - Yeyar Kon Kalikkaama Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Sekkizhar Peruman's Periya Puranam, Part 840, Vampara Vari Vandu Sarukkam, Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar Puranam. We have seen the parts where Sundaramoorthy Swamigal praised Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar in the Thiruthondar Thogai, and Nambiandar Nambigal praised him in the Thiruthondar Thiruvandhadhi. Therefore, after these two, the "type work" and the "collection work," we now begin to contemplate the Puranam of Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar in the elaborate Periya Puranam. The first verse in the Puranam of Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar is composed by Sekkizhar Peruman as follows: "Needu Van Pugazh Chozhar Neernattu Ida Nilavum Maadu Ponkozhi Kaveri Vadakarai Keezhpaal Aadu Poongodi Maadam Neediya Aninagarthan Peedu Thangiya Thiru Perumangala Peyarthal."

This verse, the first one, Sekkizhar Peruman presents the royal lineage of the Cholas, the prosperity of the Chola country, the glory of its waters, and the name of the holy place called Perumangalam, all at once. When beginning a Puranam, Sekkizhar Peruman customarily starts by singing about the holy place where the Nayanar praised in that Puranam was born, the holy land, the glory of its waters, and the glory of its city. Following this custom, in this Puranam as well, Sekkizhar Peruman begins by singing about Perumangalam, the holy place where Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar was born, the Chola country where that place is located, the Kaveri river flowing in that Chola country, the water prosperity that the Chola country gains from it, and the beauty of the holy city called Perumangalam, all in this first verse.

Sekkizhar Peruman begins this Puranam. The Chola country is a land rich in water, a country of Cholas with enduring strength. In that Chola country, to the east of the areas on the northern bank of the Kaveri, lies the beautiful city of Perumangalam, a city full of beautiful mansions adorned with dancing flower creepers. Sekkizhar Peruman refers to this as "Peedu Thangiya Thirupperumangalam" (Thirupperumangalam, which possesses glory). "Peedu" means glory. What kind of glory? It is a holy place that possesses eternal, unchanging Shiva-auspicious glory. Therefore, its name, Perumangalam, is a descriptive name. This city is imbued with that glory. "Thangiyiruthal" (being imbued) means that even when its leader, Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar, passed away, the auspiciousness remained unchanged. Sekkizhar Peruman refers to this here in this way. Such a place bears the name Perumangalam. Therefore, it also possesses qualities befitting its name. In this Puranam, Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar's consort plays a very important role. Before this, in the Periya Puranam, the Puranam where Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar appeared was the Manakkanyara Nayanar Puranam. We have already contemplated that. Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar married the daughter of Manakkanyara Nayanar. In the Manakkanyara Nayanar Puranam, on the wedding day of Manakkanyara Nayanar's daughter and Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar, Lord Shiva came in the form of a great ascetic (Maaviratha Kolam) and asked Manakkanyara Nayanar to cut off his daughter's hair. Manakkanyara Nayanar, on his daughter's wedding day, cut off her hair and gave it to the Lord who came in the form of a great ascetic. By the grace of Lord Shiva, his daughter's hair grew back. After that, Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar came to Manakkanyara Nayanar's wedding, and the marriage of the Nayanar's daughter and Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar was happily concluded there, as we saw. Therefore, although Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar's consort had her flower-like hair cut off on her wedding day, it grew back by divine grace. Similarly, here in the Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar Puranam, when Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar ended his life, his consort was about to lose her auspiciousness, but because Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar revived, the divine mother regained it. Therefore, Sekkizhar Peruman, with all these historical references implicitly contained, refers to that holy city as "Thirupperumangala Peyarthu," a city where unchanging Shiva-auspiciousness always resides. Now, the second verse is a verse that explains the glory of the city. "Inji Soozhvana Enthira Panthisoozh Gnayil Manju Soozhvana Varaiyana Uyarmamani Maadam Nanjusoozhvana Nayaniyar Nalina Melladi Sempangu Kaalaiyar Kunjiyin Parappu" is the verse. In this verse, we should take "Gnayil Inji Soozhvana Maadam Manju Soozhvana Kunjiyin Parappu Panju Soozhvana." "Inji" means a wall, and "Gnayil" means the structure on top of that wall. Therefore, it says, "Inji Soozhvana Enthira Panthisoozh Gnayil." Around the holy city of Thirupperumangalam, there are large walls. On top of these walls, various types of machines are placed. To prevent enemies from crossing the walls and entering the city, various types of machines are placed on the walls. These machines include fire-emitting machines, machines that can kill hundreds of people, and many other types of machines. All these are fitted on various sides of the fort walls. Now, "Maadam Nanjusoozhvana," meaning there are very large houses with mansions in this city. Clouds gather and settle on the tops of these mansions. Now, the next part is "Nanjusoozhvana Nayaniyar Nalina Melladi Sempangu Kaalaiyar Kunjiyin Parappu." In Thirupperumangalam, lovers, the heroine, the beloved, are in a state of 'oodal' (a lovers' quarrel). To resolve this 'oodal', the heroes (Kalaikal) are referred to as "Nanjusoozhvaniyar." This indicates that the beloved's eyes are filled with anger due to the 'oodal'. The implication is that the disease of love is revealed by the gaze of the eyes. Even in Thirukkural, there is a couplet that says, "One look is a look of disease." Therefore, the heroine is in 'oodal'. To appease her anger, the hero lies at the feet of his beloved. As a result, the red paste (Sempangu Kuzhambu) on the beloved's feet touches the hero's hair, making his hair appear red. Thus, this second verse is structured. Here, "Kunchi Gnayil" indicates the glory of the country and city, "Maadam" indicates the prosperity of the city, and "Nayaniyar Kaalaiyar" indicates the happiness and prosperity of the people there. Sekkizhar Peruman continues to describe this in the next verse as well. The third verse is "Vizhavu Aradhan Vilangu Oli Mani Ne Nedum Veethi Muzhavu Aradhan Moi Kuzhaliyar Nada Arangam Mazhavu Aradhan Mangalam Poli Mani Muntril Uzhavu Nalvalathan Oongu Erungudigal" is the verse. In the beautiful streets of Thirupperumangalam city, festivals are always taking place. Plays and performances are always happening on the stages in every street. The dancing halls of women with dense hair are always filled with the sound of drums. Performances are always happening in the dancing halls, and the sound of the mridangam (drum) is always heard to accompany them. Children are always present in the courtyards, never leaving. Boys and girls are playing in the courtyards of the houses. As Thirukkural says, "Mangalam enba mana maatchi matrum adhan nankalam nan makkal peru" (Auspiciousness is the glory of a home, and its good adornment is the birth of good children). Therefore, good domestic life prevails there. Good children are playing in the streets of that city. Prosperous great families are always engaged in farming. Sekkizhar Peruman thus describes the flourishing of agriculture, hinting that the Nayanar of this Puranam, Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar, is a Vellalar (farmer). Sekkizhar Peruman sings that the unparalleled excellence of the prosperity derived from agriculture and the absence of defects in other industrial wealth prevail there, with good prosperity abounding everywhere. Now, the fourth verse is "Neerinil Poli Sadaimudi Netri Naattathu Kaarinil Thigazh Kandar Tham Kaathalor Kuzhumi Paarin Mikkathor Perumaiyaal Paramar Thazhparavum Seerin Mikkathu Sivapori Enathagum Sirappaal." This verse is structured in this way. Lord Shiva is one who has matted locks adorned with the river Ganga. He has a third eye on his forehead. He has a dark throat that shines like a cloud. Thirupperumangalam is distinguished by the gathering of those who have great love for such a Lord Shiva. Thirupperumangalam is greatly glorified by the exclusive worship of the sacred feet of the supreme light of Shiva. Therefore, this city itself becomes worthy of being called Sivapuri (City of Shiva). Sekkizhar Peruman sings, "Neerinil Poli Sadaimudi" to indicate the glory of its water resources, referring to the river Ganga. Sekkizhar Peruman states that in this holy place, the glory of "Kaathalor Kuzhumi Paarin Mikkathor Seer" (the gathering of lovers, a glory greater than that of the earth) prevails. In this history of the Puranam, Sekkizhar Peruman sets the stage for the later verses, such as "Empiraan Entha Thanthai Thanthai Em Kootamellam Thampiraan Neere Endru Vazhi Vazhi Saarnthu Vaazhum Imbarin Mikka Vaazhkai" (Our Lord, my father, his father, and all our lineage, saying "You are the Lord," live a life greater than this world, adhering to Him generation after generation). With such glory, among all other glories in this world, this glory alone is the highest. The people there are distinguished by their worship of only the sacred feet of Lord Shiva. It says, "Paramar Thazh Paravum." If we separate it as "Thaalgalaiye" (only the feet), it implies that they worship only the sacred feet of Lord Shiva, without worshipping anyone else. This idea is conveyed here. In the previous two verses, Sekkizhar Peruman sang about the worldly prosperity of the country and the city. Continuing from that, in this verse, the Shiva devotion, which is the state that brings good to the soul, flourishes here. Therefore, Sekkizhar Peruman sings that this holy place is worthy of being called Sivapuri. We will continue to contemplate. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 03 - Yeyar Kon Kalikkaama Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Sekkizhar Peruman's Periya Puranam, Part 841, Vampara Vari Vandu Sarukkam, Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar Puranam. The fifth verse is: "Inna Vaazhpathi Adhan Idai Eyarkon Kudithaan Manniya Neediya Valavar Senaapathikkudiyaam Thonmaimēviya Thodarchiyaal Nigazhvathu Thooya Ponni Naattu Velaanmaiyil Uyarntha Porpinaal." Sekkizhar Peruman has been singing about the glories of Thirupperumangalam. This holy place is currently near Vaidheeswaran Koil, a short distance north of Thiruppunkur. There is a Shiva temple here called Vanthondar Koil. Sekkizhar Peruman, who sings about the prosperity of this holy place, says that in this prosperous region, an "Eyarkon Kudi" (lineage of Eyarkon) is established and has been flourishing for generations. This is the lineage that serves as the commander-in-chief for the Cholas. The position of commander-in-chief for the Cholas has been inherited by this lineage through generations. This lineage has been continuously flourishing from ancient times. This lineage is distinguished by its excellence in agriculture in the pure Kaveri region. Agriculture is the glory of a country, it is the wealth of farming. This lineage is distinguished by its excellence in such agriculture. The Nayanar of this Puranam, Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar, is a Vellalar (farmer). Therefore, Sekkizhar Peruman thus glorifies the Nayanar's lineage. Now, the next verse, the sixth verse: "Angan Mikka Akkudiyinil Avathariththu Ullaar Gangai Vaazh Mudiyaar Thondar Kalikkamar Enbaar Thangal Naayagar Adipanivaar Adichchaarnthu Pongu Kaathalin Avar Pani Potruthal Purinthaar." In that glorious lineage, was born the Nayanar named Kalikkamar. Kalikkama Nayanar was a devotee of Lord Shiva, who has the Ganga residing in His matted locks. He not only served Shiva but also eagerly performed the services of Shiva's devotees with even greater love. Sekkizhar Peruman sings, "Pongu Kaathalin Avar Pani Potruthal Purinthaar" (He performed their service with overflowing love). "Ponguthal" means to increase more and more. "Potruthal" means to perform with eagerness. "Purithal" means to continuously think, speak, and do. Therefore, with that ever-increasing desire, the Nayanar continuously thinks, speaks, and does the service of the Lord's feet and the service of Shiva's devotees. This implies that Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar was not only devoted to Shiva but also excelled in Jangama Bhakti (devotion to wandering ascetics), and among these two, he particularly excelled in serving the devotees. We are going to learn through his Puranam that he excelled in serving Shiva's devotees generation after generation. Now, the seventh verse: "Puthiya Naal Mathichchadai Mudiyaar Thiruppunkurukku Athigam Aayina Thiruppani Aneganum Aavana Neeru Uganthaar Kazhal Endru Thuthiyinaal Paraviththozhuthu Inpuruginraar." Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar performs many, many sacred services to Thiruppunkur, the holy place of Lord Shiva, who has matted locks adorned with the newly sprouted crescent moon. He performs many, many sacred services not only to the Lord residing in that place but also to the place itself. Once, when the country was suffering from a drought, Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar prayed to the Lord. He sought a boon, wishing for abundant rain. He offered 12 'veli' (a unit of land) of land to the Lord and prayed for the boon. Seeing this, by the grace of the Lord, heavy rain fell, causing floods everywhere. The rain continued to fall without stopping. Therefore, to stop the heavy rain, he again offered 12 'veli' of land and prayed to the Lord, and the heavy rain stopped. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal has sung about this sacred history in his Thiruppathigam: "Vaiyaga Mutru Maamazhai Maranthu Vayalinneerilai Maanilam Tharugom Uyya Kolga Matra Engalai Enna Oligol Venmugilaai Paranthu Engum Peyyum Maamazhai Peru Vellam Thavirthu Peyarthum Panniru Velik Kondarulum Seigai." Thus, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal has sung about this sacred history. Therefore, Sekkizhar Peruman refers to this as "Athigamaayina Thiruppani" (many, many sacred services) because he first offered 12 'veli' of land for the rain to fall, and then another 12 'veli' of land for the rain to stop. In addition to this, there is another history. It is said that at one time, to cure a disease he suffered, Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar offered 12 'veli' of land to the Lord of Thiruppunkur. This history is found in the words of Sundaramoorthy Swamigal: "Etha Nannilam Eeraruveli Eyarkon Utra Virumbi Thavirthu." Moreover, Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar worshipped the Lord of Thiruppunkur, whose name is Sivalokanathar, as his personal deity (Aanmaartha Moorthi). It is also heard that he performed many good sacred services in this way. We also saw that Thiruppunkur is a holy place near Thirupperumangalam, the Nayanar's place. Thus, Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar, who performs many sacred services to the Lord of Thiruppunkur, praises and worships the sacred feet of Lord Shiva, who desired the sacred ash, as his wealth, and finds joy in it. Sekkizhar Peruman sings, "Nithiyam Aavana Neeru Uganthaar Kazhal Endru Thuthiyinaal Paravi Thozhuthu Inpuruginraar" (He praises and worships the feet of Him who desired the sacred ash as his wealth, and finds joy in it). This is the goal that Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar embraced and lived by: "Aiyaran Adithalame" (The feet of Aiyaran alone) which destroys and creates great wealth, as stated in Thiruviruttam. We see in the histories of Meypporul Nayanar, Enathinatha Nayanar, Pugazhchola Nayanar, and others that the path of sacred ash is the path of Shiva. Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar, who desired and wore the sacred ash, and who possessed the ability to follow the path of sacred ash, considered the feet of those who desired the state of servitude to the sacred ash as true wealth, and lived happily by embracing that state of servitude. Now, Sekkizhar Peruman composes the next verse, the eighth verse. In this verse, Sekkizhar Peruman briefly describes the Nayanar's history and its structure. Sekkizhar Peruman composes this verse as: "Naavalur Mannar Naathanai Thoodu Vittu Adhanukku Yaar Isseyal Purinthanar Endru Avar Ezhappa Devar Thampiraan Avar Thiram Thiruthiya Adharku Mēva Vantha Asseyalinai Vilambuvaan Utren." The king of Thiruvarur, Nambi Aaroorar, sends Lord Shiva as a messenger to Paravai Nachiyar. Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar criticizes Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, asking who would perform such an act. Lord Shiva, the Lord of the Devas, performs a divine act to correct this opinion of Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar. Sekkizhar Peruman says, "I have come to narrate that event." Through this verse, Sekkizhar Peruman provides a brief summary, like a preface, to this sacred history, indicating that this history revolves around the sacred history of Sundaramoorthy Swamigal. Therefore, because Sundaramoorthy Swamigal sent Lord Shiva as a messenger to resolve Paravai Nachiyar's 'oodal' (lovers' quarrel), the Puranam of Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar is about to unfold. Therefore, Sekkizhar Peruman will first narrate the sacred history of Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, who is the cause of this, and then, in the later part of this history, he will narrate Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar's anger, the divine acts performed by Lord Shiva to appease it, and so on.

Therefore, the sacred history of Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, which is the basis for the Puranam of Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar, is the sacred history from the time Sundaramoorthy Swamigal composed the Thiruthondar Thogai to the time Lord Shiva went as a messenger to resolve Paravaiyar's 'oodal' in Thiruvarur. Earlier, at the beginning of the Periya Puranam, in the Thirumalai Sarukkam, in the Thaduthatkonda Puranam, we saw Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's history from his birth to his composition of the Thiruthondar Thogai in Thiruvarur. From that point, Sekkizhar Peruman now begins to narrate Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's sacred history in this section. Therefore, from the point where Sundaramoorthy Swamigal composed the Thiruthondar Thogai and resided in Thiruvarur, up to the point where Paravai Nachiyar's 'oodal' was resolved by sending Lord Shiva as a messenger, this history is narrated. Sekkizhar Peruman then begins, elaborates, and concludes this history by stating that upon hearing the news of Nambigal sending the Lord as a messenger, Kalikkama Nayanar became angry and harbored enmity towards Nambigal (Sundaramoorthy Swamigal). This is the structure of this history, the structure of this holy Puranam. The king of Naavalur is Sundaramoorthy Swamigal. Nambi Aaroorar, who was born in Thirunaavalur, is Sundaramoorthy Swamigal. He grew up under Narsinga Munaiyaraiyar, the king of Thirunaavalur. Therefore, Sekkizhar Peruman refers to Sundaramoorthy Swamigal as the king of Naavalur for that reason. Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar criticizes Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, the king of Naavalur, saying that our Lord, the Lord of Thiruvarur, would perform such a lowly act. Sekkizhar Peruman begins to sing, saying that he will narrate the divine act performed by Lord Shiva between Sundaramoorthy Swamigal and Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar to change that opinion. Now, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's sacred history is about to continue. From this point, the ninth verse: "Thiruthondar Thogai Aruli Thirunaavalur Aali Karutthu Ondru Kaathalinaal Kanagamathil Thiruvarur Oruththar Kazhal Muppozhuthum Urugiya Anpodu Paninthu Peruththu Ezhum Mey Anbinaal Priyaathu Angu Uraiyum Naal." This verse is structured in this way. The verse begins with "Thiruthondar Thogai Aruli Thirunaavalur Aali." After composing the Thiruthondar Thogai, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, the ruler of Thirunaavalur, with a unified mind and great love, worships the feet of the Lord residing in Thiruvarur, the golden city, three times a day with melting love. With ever-increasing true love, he worships without separation and resides there. Love is something that happens between a hero and a heroine. The love mentioned here refers to the excellence of the love for Lord Shiva, the spiritual hero. Such a Lord is the Lord of Thiruvarur. Sekkizhar Peruman praises him as "Thiruvarur Oruththar." "Oruththar" refers to Lord Shiva. "Oruvan Ennum Oruvan Kaanaga" (See the one who is called the One) is from Thiruvachagam. The supreme being, who is said to be one of the Vedas, is Lord Shiva. The Vedas praise God as one, "Eka Brahma." The meaning of this is that the Lord, who is called the ultimate reality (Pathiporul), is only one. Such a "Oruththar" (One) resides as Thiyagesa Peruman in Thiruvarur. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal worships the feet of such a "Oruththar" three times a day with melting love, with the love that causes the mind to melt. "Peruththu Ezhum Mey Anbinaal" means with ever-increasing true love. Because of that, the mind became one with love, causing it to melt and worship. Therefore, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal resides in Thiruvarur, worshipping three times a day without separation. What happens on such a day of his residence? Sekkizhar Peruman continues to sing about it. We will also continue to contemplate. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 04 - Yeyar Kon Kalikkaama Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Sekkizhar Peruman's Periya Puranam, Part 842, Vampara Vari Vandu Sarukkam, Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar Puranam. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal is residing in Thiruvarur, praising the Lord. During this time, by the grace of the Lord, a divine and miraculous event is about to take place. Sekkizhar Peruman sings about it. The tenth verse is: "Thaalannmai Uzhavu Thozhil Thanmai Valam Thalaisirantha Velaalar Kundaiyur Kizhavar Enum Methakkor Vaazhaar Venmathiyaniynthaar Maraiyaavarai Vazhakkil Venru Aala Kondavar Thazh Adainthu Anbaal Ozhuguvaar." Near Thiruvarur, there is a village called Kundaiyur, near the holy place known as Thirukkuvalai or Thirukkozhili. In that village lived a very distinguished Vellalar (farmer). His name was Kundaiyur Kizhavar. This great person, through his diligent efforts in farming, acquired abundant wealth. "Thaalmai" means perseverance. "Thaal" means effort, and "aanmai" means the quality of ruling. Sekkizhar Peruman refers to this as "Thaal Uzhavu Thozhil Thanmai." "Thaal Uzhavu Thozhil Thanmai Valam" means that farming is a profession that requires great effort. Compared to other professions, it involves more physical exertion and labor. Such a farming profession, through great effort, brings good wealth, such as paddy, vegetables, fruits, and other riches. Kundaiyur Kizhavar was a great person who possessed an abundance of these riches that come through great effort in farming. "Thaalatrintha thantha porulellaam thakkaarkku velaanmai seithal poruttu" (All the wealth earned through diligent effort is for the purpose of helping the deserving) is a couplet from Thirukkural in the chapter on "Oppuravu Arithal" (Knowing Benevolence). Here, "Thaalatrintha thantha porulellaam" means all the wealth earned through great effort, according to Parimelazhagar. Therefore, "Thaal" refers to effort. So, the meaning of this couplet is that great people, who are worthy, would use all the wealth earned through great effort to help others. Here, Kundaiyur Kizhavar is a Vellalar, belonging to the farming community. Sekkizhar Peruman refers to this great person as Kundaiyur Kizhavar. "Kizhavar" has become "Kizhaar" in common usage. Sekkizhar Peruman refers to this great person by his village, Kundaiyur Kizhavar, without mentioning his individual name, to emphasize the glory of that lineage. Furthermore, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal has glorified the village of Kundaiyur by including it in his Thevara Thiruppathigam. Following this, Sekkizhar Peruman also refers to him as Kundaiyur Kizhavar. Kundaiyur Kizhavar was a devotee of Lord Shiva, who wears the luminous crescent moon. Lord Shiva came as a Brahmin, won a legal dispute, and took Nambi Aaroorar (Sundaramoorthy Swamigal) as His own. Kundaiyur Kizhavar, having taken refuge at the feet of Nambi Aaroorar, lived with great love for him. We have contemplated this sacred history in the Thaduthatkonda Puranam, where Lord Shiva came and won a legal dispute and took Sundaramoorthy Swamigal as His servant. This implies that Kundaiyur Kizhavar, being involved in that sacred history, lived with great love for the feet of Sundaramoorthy Swamigal. What sacred service did he perform as a sign of his overflowing love? Sekkizhar Peruman reveals this in the next verse. The eleventh verse is: "Sennellum Ponnena Sezhum Paruppum Thengarumbin Innallamuthum Muthal Ennil Perum Pala Valangal Manniya Seer Vanthondarkku Amuthaaga Valuvaamal Panedunaal Paravaiyaar Maaligaikku Padi Samaiththaar." This verse is structured in this way. In this verse, Sekkizhar Peruman praises the sacred service performed by Kundaiyur Kizhavar. Kundaiyur Kizhavar, for the purpose of preparing sacred food for Vanthondar, Nambi Aaroorar, provided various riches such as red paddy, golden-like rich lentils, sweet sugarcane, and many other abundant provisions, without fail, for a long time, to Paravai Nachiyar's mansion. It says "Padi Samaiththaar." "Padi" means a measure, a daily offering of sacred food. He performed that sacred service. The twelfth verse is as follows: "Aanasaiyil Anbin Varum Aarvaththaal Magizhndhu Aatra Vaana Murai Vazhangaamal Maanilaththu Valam Surunga Ponaga Nel Padi Niramba Eduppatharku Potha Maanavali Kolgaiyinaal Manamayangi Varunthuvaar." He performed that act of daily offering sacred food with great joy, out of intense desire born of love. While he was doing so, the rain failed to fall as it should have, causing the agricultural produce to diminish. Rain should fall regularly, three times a month. The sky failed to provide rain in this manner, causing the agricultural produce to diminish. As a result, it became impossible to provide the paddy and other provisions for the sacred food. Therefore, Kundaiyur Kizhavar became very distressed. Sekkizhar Peruman refers to this as "Maanam Azhi Kolgaiyinaal Manamayangi Varunthuvaar" (He became distressed with a confused mind due to the loss of his honor). "Maanam" means honor. Here, it refers to the intense feeling of commitment to one's duties concerning life. It should not be interpreted as the ordinary worldly pride, which is related to attachment. Therefore, Kundaiyur Kizhavar is distressed, his mind is confused. Next, verses 13 and 14 are as follows: "Vanthondar Thiruvarur Maaligaikku Nel Edukka Indru Kurai Aagindrathu En Seigēn Ena Ninainthu Thundru Perum Kavalaiyinaal Thuyar Eythi Unnaathe Andru Iravu Thuyil Kolla Anganar Vanthu Arul Purivaar Aarooran Thanakkunpaal Nel Thanthom Endru Aruli Neerurum Sadaimudiyaar Nithi Komaan Thanaiyeva Perur Matrum Adhan Ellai Adangavum Nel Malai Pirangal Kaarurum Nedum Visumbum Karakka Nirainthu Oongiyathaal." These verses are structured in this way. Kundaiyur Kizhavar is confused, thinking, "Today, there is a shortage in providing paddy for Vanthondar's Thiruvarur mansion. What shall I do?" Due to the reduced rainfall, agricultural produce has decreased. Therefore, there was a shortage of provisions. As a result, he is unable to fulfill the regular service he had undertaken. He is distressed, thinking, "What shall I do?" With this anxiety, he goes to sleep that night without eating. Not eating food is a sign of mental distress. We contemplated in the Puranam of Thirunavukkarasu Peruman that when the Thiruppazhaiyarai Vadathali temple was occupied by Jains, Thirunavukkarasu Peruman, seeing this, fasted, saying, "If this temple does not appear and You do not bestow grace, I will not eat." Similarly, here, due to his mental distress, Kundaiyur Kizhavar sleeps without eating. At that time, Lord Shiva appears in his dream. Lord Shiva, seeing Kundaiyur Kizhavar's distress, who considered the shortage in preparing sacred food for Vanthondar as his own shortcoming and slept without eating, bestows grace in his dream. Kundaiyur Kizhavar's anxiety is a great anxiety concerning a devotee. Therefore, Lord Shiva Himself comes directly to resolve it and bestows grace. Thirukkural says, "Thanakkupamaillaathan thaazh sernthaarkku allaal manakkavalai maatral arithu" (It is difficult to remove mental distress for anyone other than those who have taken refuge at the feet of the incomparable One). Thus, Lord Shiva comes in Kundaiyur Kizhavar's dream and says, "Aarooran thanakku unpaal nel thanthom" (We have given you paddy for Aarooran). Lord Shiva bestows grace, saying, "We have given you paddy for Aarooran to prepare food." Having bestowed grace in the dream, Lord Shiva then commands Kubera. He is the Lord of wealth, the king of Alagapuri. Kubera is also a friend of Lord Shiva. As one of the Ashtadikpalakas (guardians of the eight directions), he is bound by the rule to provide the necessary wealth to devotees wherever Lord Shiva commands. At this point, we can recall the histories of Ilayankudi Mara Nayanar and Kungiliya Kalaya Nayanar. Lord Shiva commands such a Kubera. Kubera brings paddy in the form of a mountain to Kundaiyur. Paddy heaps are piled up like mountains throughout Kundaiyur. This is done by Kubera at the command of Lord Shiva. That paddy mountain reaches up to the sky. The paddy mountain covers the entire Kundaiyur. Lord Shiva bestowed grace, saying, "Aarooran thanakku unpaal nel thanthom" (We have given you paddy for Aarooran). He could have given it directly to Sundaramoorthy Swamigal. But instead, He gives it to Kundaiyur Kizhavar, implying that the Lord bestows grace in this way to relieve Kundaiyur Kizhavar's mental distress and to fulfill his sacred service. Therefore, the paddy mountain accumulates throughout Kundaiyur, covering the entire village boundary. The fifteenth verse is as follows: "Avviravu Pularkaalai Unarnthu Ezhuvaar Athu Kande Evvulagin Nelmalai Thaan Ithu Endre Athisayiththu Sevviya Ponmalai Valaiththaar Thiru Arulin Seyal Potri Kovvaivaai Paravaiyaar Kozhunaraiye Thozhuthu Ezhuvaar." Kundaiyur Kizhavar, having been enlightened by the Lord in his dream, wakes up at dawn and rises. This is a dream at dawn, which is significant. He rises, worshipping Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, the husband of Paravaiyar. The act of worshipping and rising happens simultaneously. Like Thirukkural says, "Kozhunan thozhuthu ezhuvaal" (She rises worshipping her husband). Having risen, he is amazed. He is amazed at the paddy mountain. He is filled with wonder, thinking, "From which world did this paddy mountain come?" He praises the divine act of Lord Shiva, saying that it is indeed the divine act of Lord Shiva, who bent the golden mountain (Meru) as a bow. He rises, worshipping Sundaramoorthy Swamigal. The fact that he saw the paddy mountain with his eyes indicates the immense pride that arises from something happening that is beyond comprehension. Therefore, he stands amazed. He praises it, saying that it is indeed the divine act of Lord Shiva, who bent the golden mountain (Meru) as a bow, and rises, worshipping Sundaramoorthy Swamigal. We will continue to contemplate. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 05 - Yeyar Kon Kalikkaama Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Sekkizhar Peruman's Periya Puranam, Part 843, Vampara Vari Vandu Sarukkam, Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar Puranam. Kundaiyur Kizhavar sees the entire Kundaiyur filled with paddy mountains. The sixteenth verse is as follows: "Naavalur Mannanaarkku Naayanaar Aliththa Nel Ingu Yaavaraal Edukkalaagum Isseyal Avarkku Sollappovan Yaan Endru Ponthaar Pugunthavaaru Aruli Seythu Devar Tham Perumaan Eva Nambiyum Ethir Senraar." Kundaiyur Kizhavar is amazed. He thinks, "This paddy was given by Lord Shiva out of compassion for Nambi Aaroorar, the king of Thirunaavalur. So, who can carry it from here? No one can." The one who gave this paddy mountain is Lord Shiva, and He is very great. To whom did He give it? To Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, and he is also very great. What did He give? This gift, the paddy, is piled up like mountains. Therefore, the giver, the recipient, and the gift are all great. So, no one else can carry this paddy mountain in this world. No one can carry this paddy mountain in this world except by the grace of Lord Shiva, the Lord. Thinking this, he says, "I will go and tell Nambi Aaroorar about this deed," and sets off towards Thiruvarur. At the same time, the Lord had already informed Nambi Aaroorar that Kundaiyur Kizhavar was coming and commanded him to go and meet him. The grace of the Lord, the devotion of Kundaiyur Kizhavar, the greatness of Nambi Aaroorar, the simplicity with which the Lord bestows grace, and the strength of Paravaiyar, all came to light simultaneously for the world to understand. Nambigal prayed to the Lord to compose a Thiruppathigam (hymn) about this, and the Lord's grace was bestowed in such a way that the world could witness and understand. Sekkizhar Peruman continues to explain this. "Aatpaal Avarkku Arulum Vannamum Aathi Maanpum Ketpaan Pugil Alavillai Kizhakka Vendaa" (If one were to ask about the manner in which He bestows grace upon His servants and His ancient greatness, there is no limit; there is no need to explain). This is a Thiruppaasuram (sacred verse). The seventeenth verse is: "Kundaiyur Kizhavar Thaamum Ethirkondu Kothil Vaaimai Thondanaar Paatham Thannil Thozhuthu Veezhndhu Ezunthu Nindru Pandellaam Adiyen Seytha Pani Enakku Indru Mutta Andartham Piraar Thaame Nelmalai Azhiththaar Endru Manitharaal Edukkum Ellaiththu Andru Nelmalaiyin Aakkam Ini Ennaal Seyyal Aagum Pani Andru Ithu Ena Kettu Pani Mathi Mudiyaar Andre Parinthu Umakku Azhiththaar Nel Endru Iniya Mozhinthu Thaamum Kundaiyur Eytha Vanthaar." These are the seventeenth and eighteenth verses. Kundaiyur Kizhavar comes to meet him. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal also sets off from Thiruvarur towards Kundaiyur. Kundaiyur Kizhavar meets Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, prostrates at Nambi Aaroorar's feet, falls to the ground, rises, and stands. Kundaiyur Kizhavar bows at Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's feet, saying, "When the service I, your servant, had been performing all this time, was interrupted today, Lord Shiva Himself, the Lord of the celestials, created a mountain of paddy." He further says, "The quantity of this paddy mountain is immense. It is not something that can be carried by humans. Therefore, this is not a task that I can do. Carrying all this paddy mountain to Thiruvarur is not something I can do. No other human in this world can do it." He appeals to Nambi Aaroorar's feet. Hearing this, Nambi Aaroorar says, "Did not Lord Shiva Himself bestow this paddy upon you?" and with sweet words, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal comes near Kundaiyur. Kundaiyur Kizhavar also comes along. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal says, "The Lord Himself gave you the paddy. If He had given it for my sake, He would have given it in Thiruvarur. But instead, He gave it in Kundaiyur and bestowed grace upon you. Therefore, He gave it for your love." Saying this, they both reach the boundary of Kundaiyur. The nineteenth and twentieth verses are as follows: "Vinninai Alakkum Nellin Veppinaai Nambi Nokki Annnalai Thozhuthu Potri Athisayam Mikavum Eythi Ennil Seer Paravaiyillaththu Innellaai Edukka Aalum Thannilavu Aninthaar Thaame Tharil Andri Onnaathu Endru Aalida Vendik Kolvaar Arugu Thiruppathiyaana Kozhiliyil Tham Perumaan Koyilinai Vanthu Eythi Vaanalavu Kanmadavaal Varunthaame Enum Pathigam Moola Varum Kaathaludan Mun Thozhuthu Paaduthalum." These two verses are arranged in sequence. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal sees the paddy mountain that reaches up to the sky. He worships the glorious Lord Shiva and is greatly amazed. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal thinks that this paddy cannot be taken to Paravai Nachiyar's mansion, which possesses immeasurable glory, unless Lord Shiva Himself provides the people to carry it. Therefore, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal prays for people to carry the paddy. He goes to Thirukozhili, a holy place near Kundaiyur, and enters the temple of Lord Shiva there. With overflowing love, he bows down and sings the Thiruppathigam (hymn) that contains the phrase and meaning "Vaanalavu Kanmadavaal Varunthaame" (Let not the woman with eyes like the sky suffer). Thirukozhili is a holy place located a short distance directly south of Kundaiyur. Kundaiyur is not a major holy place. It can be reached from Thiruvarur by going southeast, passing through Thiru Ema Perur, Thirukkarayil, and Thiruvalivalam. It is also possible to consider that Nambigal went to Thirukozhili, which is a nearby holy place and one of the Sapthavidanga Sthalams (seven sacred places where Lord Shiva performed the cosmic dance), and sang a hymn. Since the paddy mountain was piled up to the boundary of Kundaiyur, preventing anyone else from entering Kundaiyur, it is also possible that he went to the nearby Thirukozhili and sang a hymn. We have already contemplated the special features of Thirukozhili in the Puranams of Thirunavukkarasu Peruman and Thirugnanasambandha Peruman. In such a holy place of Thirukozhili, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal sang a Thiruppathigam before the Lord, praying for people to carry the paddy. The Thiruppathigam, which begins with the first verse "Neela Ninainthu Adiyen Ummai Nitthalam Kaithozhuven Vaazhana Kanmadavaal Aval Vaadi Varunthaame Kozhili Emperumaan Kundaiyur Sila Nellu Petren Aal Ilai Emperumaan Avai Attiththara Paniye" (I, your servant, will always remember and worship you. Let not the woman with beautiful eyes suffer. O Lord of Kozhili, I have received some paddy in Kundaiyur. O Lord, I have no people to carry it; please command them to carry it), is the 20th Thiruppathigam in the 7th Thirumurai, set to the tune of Nattaragam. This Thiruppathigam was sung by Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, praying for people to carry this paddy to Thiruvarur so that Paravaiyar would not suffer. In the first verse, he says, "Aal Ilai Emperumaan Avai Attiththara Paniye" (O Lord, I have no people to carry it; please command them to carry it). In the sixth verse, he says, "Paravai Pasi Varuttham Athu Neeyum Arithiyanre" (Is not Paravai's hunger and suffering known to you?). In the eighth verse, he says, "Paravai Val Vaaduginraal" (Paravai is suffering). In the tenth verse, he says, "Nellida Aatkal Vendi" (Praying for people to carry the paddy). Thus, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal has composed this Thiruppathigam. Now, the twenty-first verse: "Pagal Pozhuthu Kazhinthu Adhanpin Paravai Manaiyalavu Andri Mika Perugum Nel Ulakil Vilangiya Aaroor Niraiya Pugappeythu Tharuvana Nam Boothangal Ena Visumbil Nigappu Ariyathu Oru Vaakku Nigazhnthathu Ninmalan Arulaal." As soon as Sundaramoorthy Swamigal sang this Thiruppathigam, by the grace of Lord Shiva, an ethereal voice arose in the sky. After the day had passed, the voice said that not only would the paddy, which was greatly abundant, fill Paravaiyar's house, but it would also fill Thiruvarur, which is renowned in the world, and our Bhoothaganas (celestial attendants) would bring and add it. An ethereal voice, incomparable, arose in the sky. The actions of the Shivaganas (attendants of Shiva) are invisible to human eyes. Therefore, the situation was that the entire paddy mountain would be transferred from Kundaiyur to Thiruvarur within ten Nazhigai (a unit of time). The divine voice appeared, saying that the Shivabhootha Ganas would do it after the day had passed. The ethereal voice proclaimed that this entire paddy mountain could not be stored in Paravaiyar's mansion alone; it would be stored throughout Thiruvarur. The voice said, "Our Shivaganas are the Bhoothas with a deep voice." We have contemplated the glories of the Shivabhootha Ganas at the beginning of the Periya Puranam. We have seen in the sixteenth verse, "Needu Devar Nilaigalum Vendidi Naadum Aim Perum Boothamum Naattuva" (If the positions of the long-lived gods are desired, the five great elements will also be created). Now, the twenty-second verse: "Thampiraan Arul Potri Tharainmisai Vizhunthu Ezunthe Omparaal Unarvu Ariya Thiruppaatham Thozhuthu Eththi Sempon Ner Sadaiyaarai Pirappathiyum Thozhuthu Poy Nambar Aaroor Anaiththaar Naavalanar." Sundaramoorthy Swamigal praises the grace of Lord Shiva, falls to the ground, rises, and worships the sacred feet that are incomprehensible even to the gods. He praises and worships the Lord of Thirukozhili. He then visits other holy places like Thiruvalivalam, Thirunellikka, Thirukkarayil, and Thiru Ema Perur, and then returns to Thiruvarur. The twenty-third verse: "Poongoyil Magizhndharulum Puraathanarai Pokku Irai Neengaatha Perumagizhchiyudan Eththi Puramponthu Paangaanaar Pudaisoozhndhu Potrisaikka Paravaiyaar Oongu Thiru Maaligaiyin Ul Anainthaar Aaroorar." Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, who resides in the Poongoyil (flower temple) in Thiruvarur, where the ancient Lord who took refuge in an anthill resides, praises the Lord with unending great joy. Nambi Aaroorar, who returned to Thiruvarur after going to Kundaiyur and singing the Thirukozhili Thiruppathigam, does not go directly to his mansion. He first enters the Poongoyil and worships, and only then goes to his mansion. All Saivites should note this tradition well. The nature of a Jeevanmuktha (liberated soul) who does nothing without His grace is like this. Every time Sundaramoorthy Swamigal comes to Thiruvarur, he demonstrates this to us. Thus, after praising the Lord in the Poongoyil, he comes out, surrounded by his attendants and others who praise him, and then Nambi Aaroorar enters Paravai Nachiyar's glorious mansion. Now, the twenty-fourth and twenty-fifth verses are as follows: "Kovai Vaai Paravaiyaar Thaam Magizhumbadi Kuri Meviyavar Thammōdu Mika Inpurru Irunthatharpin Sevin Mel Umaiyōdum Varuvaartham Thiru Arulin Evalinaal Avviravu Boothangal Mikku Ezunthu Kundaiyur Nelmalaiyai Kural Padai Kavarndhu Vandulaang Kuzhal Paravai Maaligaiyai Niraiviththu Andarpiraan Thiruvarur Adanga Nel Malaiyaakki Kandavar Arputham Eythum Kaatchi Pera Amaiththanaal." These two verses are arranged in sequence. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal narrates the events of that day to Paravaiyar, who has lips like the Kovai fruit, making her happy. After that, he enjoys great happiness with her. By the command of Lord Shiva, who comes on the bull with Uma Devi, the Shivabhoothas (celestial attendants) rise that night. The Bhoothaganas take the paddy mountain from Kundaiyur and fill Paravaiyar's mansion, which has hair where bees hum. Then, they turn the entire Thiruvarur, the city of the Lord of the celestials, into a paddy mountain. This scene was arranged so that all who saw it were filled with wonder. The Shivabhootha Ganas first brought the paddy to Paravaiyar's mansion. Only after filling Paravaiyar's mansion to its capacity did they fill the entire Thiruvarur. Therefore, paddy was piled up like mountains in all the streets of Thiruvarur. All who saw that paddy mountain were filled with a sense of wonder. The reason for their wonder was that the paddy was piled up like mountains, and they were filled with a sense of pride that arose from not knowing how this paddy came here, and other such inexplicable wonders. We will continue to contemplate. Thiruchitrambalam.

Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 06 - Yeyar Kon Kalikkaama Nayanar

Sekkizhar Peruman, by divine grace, continues to compose the verses 844, 26, 27, and 28 of Periya Puranam, Vampara Vari Vandu Sarukkam, Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar Puranam. "That night, as dawn broke, the people living in Thiruvarur saw the mountains of paddy and wondered, 'From what world have these paddy mountains come?' They said, 'Nambi has given these to Paravaiyar, whose eyes are like a doe's, for this world to live.' Seeing the insurmountable mountains of paddy, and realizing it was difficult to pass through them, they returned to their homes. They praised Paravaiyar, who is the embodiment of good fortune, saying, 'It is difficult to find a place to move this paddy.'" "Today, all the paddy mountains within the boundary of your houses, take them into your golden mansions," he announced with the drum of victory. We saw that the Shiva Bhuta Ganas (divine attendants of Shiva) filled the whole of Thiruvarur with paddy mountains overnight. As that night ended and dawn broke, the people of Thiruvarur were astonished to see the mountains of paddy piled up there. They wondered, "From what world have these paddy mountains come?" The people said that these paddy mountains were given to Paravaiyar by Nambiyarurar, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal. When they came out of their houses, they saw paddy mountains piled up all over the streets, leaving no room to go out. So, they went back into their houses and praised Paravaiyar, who is the embodiment of Shiva's good deeds, saying that it was difficult to find a place to move this paddy.

Paravai Nachiyar, who possessed great fame, was very happy to see the paddy given to her by Vanthondar, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal. In addition, she made an announcement by beating a drum. She made an announcement to the people of Thiruvarur that all the paddy within the boundaries of their houses could be taken into their respective mansions. This highlights the greatness of Paravaiyar, who announced this with a drum to the people of Thiruvarur. This also clarifies that the mistaken notion held by some ignorant people, that Sundaramoorthy Swamigal sang and received gold and other things for Paravai Nachiyar, is completely wrong. It is because Paravai Nachiyar spoke these sweet words, saying that everyone could take the paddy that was within the boundaries of their respective houses, that Sekkizhar Peruman praises her as "Kovai Vaay Paravaiyar" (Paravaiyar with coral-like lips) and "Kovvai Vaay Paravaiyar" (Paravaiyar with beautiful lips), due to the nature of her words flowing with grace. We must consider that the act performed by Paravaiyar during a time of drought, when people suffered without paddy, was a great act of kindness done at the right time. The 29th verse: "By the announcement made with the drum in the streets, people filled their houses with paddy, built granaries everywhere without any delay, and the city rejoiced. Paravaiyar bowed to Vanthondar, who wore a garland of gems on his chest." We saw that the streets were filled with paddy mountains, making it impossible for people to walk. To remove that situation, a path was made through the paddy mountain for people to walk, and this was announced by beating a drum. Therefore, according to that, people took the paddy that was in front of their houses, filled their houses with it, and also built granaries to store the paddy. The people of the city were happy. They praised Paravaiyar and Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, believing them to be the cause of this happiness. Now, the 30th verse: "Nambiyarurar, in Thiruvarur, on the days he desired to speak, praised the abundant honey that resided in the anthill, the flourishing deity, with great devotion, drinking it with his consciousness, to the astonishment of both earthly and heavenly beings." In Thiruvarur, the Lord Shiva, who is the God of gods, is known as Vanmeekanathar, the honey that resides in the anthill. He is the one who has taken the anthill as his abode.

This verse praises the Lord who resides in the anthill, Vanmeekanathar, as abundant honey. The Lord is called honey because honey is not only sweet but also beneficial to the body. Similarly, the Lord is the one who brings joy to the soul, sweetening it with bliss. Therefore, He is abundant honey. The Thiruvachagam praises the Lord as "the honey that resides in the pot in Idaimaruthu." In many other places, the Thirumurai praises the Lord as honey. Just as one should drink honey, one should experience the Lord, who is honey, with one's consciousness. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, with great devotion, bowed and drank with his consciousness the abundant honey that resided as the Lord of the anthill, worshipping Him daily. The Devas (celestial beings) have gathered in Thiruvarur, sitting in the Devasiriya Mandapam, to worship Thyagesar and receive blessings. The people of this world are also gathered there. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, with devotion, bows and praises Thyagesa Peruman, who is worshipped by both earthly and heavenly beings. At this time, Kotpuli Nayanar arrives in Thiruvarur to have a darshan (vision) of Sundaramoorthy Swamigal. Sekkizhar Peruman composes the 31st verse: "Kotpuliyar, of great fame, requested that the renowned Arurar come to his city. He bowed at his feet, and the steadfast Vanthondar agreed. Then, he, of praised character, returned to his city." Kotpuli Nayanar is one of the 63 Nayanmars. We will see the Puranam of Kotpuli Nayanar in a later section of Periya Puranam.

Such a Kotpuli Nayanar was of great fame. He came to Thiruvarur, worshipped Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, and requested Sundaramoorthy Swamigal to visit his city, Thirunattiyathankudi. He fell at the feet of Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, explained all his shortcomings, and expressed his wish. He considered it a shortcoming that Nambiyarurar had not yet visited his city, and he expressed that wish, remaining prostrate at the feet of Sundaramoorthy Swamigal. Kotpuli Nayanar, who possessed good character, returned to his sacred city, Thirunattiyathankudi. Now, Sekkizhar Peruman continues to sing the history of Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's visit to Thirunattiyathankudi. The 32nd and 33rd verses are as follows: "The Lord of Thiruvarur, who rules alone, guarded by the Devas and crowded by the devotees, bowed at his feet. Nambiyarurar, having received his grace, desired to worship and sing of the Lord with the Goddess as half, in other sacred places. He went to many places that are difficult for Brahma and Vishnu to comprehend, and worshipped them. Kotpuliyar, of great fame, adorned his Natthiyathankudi in a fitting manner. He also came forward, worshipped him sweetly, and brought him into his beautiful gem-studded mansion with great affection." The Thiruvarur temple is a temple where Devas wait. The Devas wait outside the temple, awaiting the command of Thirunandhidevar to enter the temple. But the devotees, having received divine grace, are allowed to enter without any hindrance, pushing aside these Devas. Although this applies to all sacred places, it is a special characteristic of Thiruvarur. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, the Lord of Thirunavalur, bowed at the feet of the Lord who resides in the anthill, who guards and rules alone in such a Thiruvarur. He received permission from the Lord of the anthill and went to Thirunattiyathankudi, the city of Kotpuli Nayanar, worshipping many other sacred places of Lord Shiva. On the way from Thiruvarur to Thirunattiyathankudi, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal visited and had darshan of places like Thiruvalamar, Thiruyemaperur, Mavoor, and others that were in between and nearby, and then reached Thirunattiyathankudi. When Sundaramoorthy Swamigal reached Thirunattiyathankudi, Kotpuli Nayanar had decorated the entire city in a grand manner. He welcomed Sundaramoorthy Swamigal with great joy, bowed and worshipped him, and brought Nambi into his beautiful gem-studded mansion. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal also entered the sacred home of Kotpuli Nayanar. Now, the three verses, 34, 35, and 36, are arranged in sequence. In these verses, Sekkizhar Peruman sings how Kotpuli Nayanar welcomed, served, and worshipped Sundaramoorthy Swamigal. Those verses are as follows: "He seated him on a golden throne, washed his lotus-like feet with pure water, and sprinkled that pure water, which had touched his feet, all over his radiant mansion, filling his heart with joy. He performed all the prescribed worships naturally. He offered a unique blend of fragrant sandalwood paste made with cool flower dew, fragrant agarwood paste, musk paste, fragrant civet oil, pure green camphor, and betel nut, all presented in countless gem-studded vessels. He brought various types of beautiful garlands and many unparalleled gem-studded ornaments. He arranged countless types of garments in order, bowed before him, and offered boundless worship to the devotee of the Lord who wears the Ganga." These verses are composed. Kotpuli Nayanar, having brought Sundaramoorthy Swamigal to his sacred home, first seated Nambi on a golden throne inlaid with pure gems. He then washed Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's feet, which were like red lotus flowers, with pure water. He sprinkled that holy water, which had touched Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's feet, over himself and then over the entire mansion, making it radiant. Kotpuli Nayanar naturally performed all the prescribed worships with great devotion. He offered a unique blend of fragrant sandalwood paste made with beautiful, cool flower dew, fragrant agarwood paste, musk paste, fragrant civet oil, and camphor. He brought all these items, including betel nut, in countless beautiful plates and vessels, each in its own separate container. He brought various types of garlands for decoration.

He also brought many unparalleled gem-studded ornaments. He systematically arranged countless types of garments. Having arranged all these, he stood before him, bowed, and made the devotee of Lord Shiva, who wears the Ganga, accept the boundless worship. In this verse, "Poonthan Panineer" refers to cool dew collected from flowers. "Maan Madham" refers to musk. "Pugai" refers to fragrant items that produce smoke. "Naavi" refers to civet. "Adaikkai Amudhu" refers to betel nut. In ancient inscriptions, betel nut was referred to as "Adaikkai Amudhu." He brought all these in various plates and vessels, separately, and arranged them before the Lord. "Maarillatha Mani Thiru Aabharana Varkkam" refers to ornaments made of gold and gems, which are of various types. These were offered as ornaments to Sundaramoorthy Swamigal. "Parivattam" here refers to garments like upper garments. It is our tradition to refer to garments offered to the Lord and devotees as "Parivattam." He arranged all these systematically and performed the puja to Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, making him accept the puja. We will continue to contemplate. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 07 - Yeyar Kon Kalikkaama Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Sekkizhar Peruman, by divine grace, continues to sing the Periya Puranam, Part 845, Vampara Vari Vandu Sarukkam, Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar Puranam. Kotpuli Nayanar worships Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, having brought him to his sacred mansion. Sekkizhar Peruman continues to sing about this. The 37th verse: "Kotpuliyar, the commander, who was entitled to the grace of the righteous king, with friendship, made our Lord, the king of Thirunavalur, partake of sacred food in his house, worshipped him, and then, like a surging ocean, showed great love and praised him." Kotpuli Nayanar belonged to a lineage that served as commanders for the Chola kings. The "righteous king" refers to the Chola king. Kotpuli Nayanar was entitled to the grace of such a Chola king, having inherited the right to be a commander through generations. It was customary in those days for the king to choose commanders from his family. Such a Kotpuli Nayanar, with friendly affection, made our Paramacharyar, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, the king of Thirunavalur, partake of sacred food in his palace and stood worshipping him. Our Lord, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, is our Paramacharyar of the Saiva religion. He is also the king of the city of Thirunavalur. Kotpuliyar is a commander. Therefore, Sekkizhar Peruman, with divine poetic grace, composes this verse, stating that Kotpuli Nayanar, the commander, worshipped Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, who is our Lord and the king of Thirunavalur.

Having offered the sacred food, he praised Sundaramoorthy Swamigal with immense love, like an ocean. The next verse, the 38th verse: "With that great desire, he brought his two daughters, Singadiyar, whom he had lovingly begotten first, who wore garlands of honeyed flowers, and Vanappakaiyar, who had deer-like eyes, and made them bow at the lotus-like feet of Vanthondar, and he too bowed and spoke." Kotpuli Nayanar had two daughters. The first daughter was named Singadiyar, and the second was named Vanappakaiyar. He brought these two ladies, his daughters, Singadiyar, whom he had lovingly begotten first, and Vanappakaiyar, who had appeared after her, and made them bow at the lotus-like feet of Vanthondar, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, and he too bowed and spoke. In this verse, Sekkizhar Peruman refers to Singadiyar as "Thenaar Kothai Singadiyar" (Singadiyar who wears garlands of honeyed flowers) and Vanappakaiyar as "Maanar Nokkin Vanappakaiyar" (Vanappakaiyar with deer-like eyes). "Singadiyar who wears garlands adorned with honey" and "Vanappakaiyar with deer-like, startled eyes, like a goddess of wealth" are the meanings. Sekkizhar Peruman describes them in terms of their physical beauty and their inner grace. Both these meanings can be applied to both of them. Since they were women, they would naturally be shy and would remain inside, not coming before Sundaramoorthy Swamigal. Therefore, Kotpuli Nayanar brought these two daughters himself and made them bow at the feet of Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, and he too bowed. Now, the 39th verse: "He said, 'These are my children, your servants. Please accept them as your own, and grant them the grace to worship your fragrant lotus feet and be saved.' Paravaiyar's husband accepted them as his own pure children, as if they were his own daughters." Kotpuli Nayanar bowed and made a request to Sundaramoorthy Swamigal. "These two are my children. Please accept them as your servants, and grant them the grace to worship your fragrant lotus feet and be saved." This means that he requested Sundaramoorthy Swamigal to marry them and accept them as his own. Kotpuli Nayanar made this request to Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's feet because he wanted his two daughters to always worship Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's feet. Kotpuli Nayanar made this request for two reasons: that his daughters should be accepted as servants and that they should worship Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's feet. Hearing this, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal blessed them. Sekkizhar Peruman states in the verse, "He accepted them as his own pure children, as if they were his own daughters." As soon as Kotpuli Nayanar made the request, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal accepted them, saying, "These two are my pure children." He accepted them as if they were his own daughters. Sekkizhar Peruman refers to them as "Thodisér Thalirkaai Ivar" (these with tender hands adorned with bangles). "Thodi" refers to an ornament worn on the hand, a type of bangle. "Thalir" refers to a tender shoot, like a deer's tender shoot. The verse describes them as pure children with hands like tender shoots adorned with bangles. This description indicates that these two women possessed both natural beauty and cultivated grace. Furthermore, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal accepts them as "pure children." "Pure" here means "children of good character," as stated in the Thirukkural, "If one begets children who are free from evil in seven births and whose character is blameless." He accepted both these women as if they were his own children. Although Kotpuli Nayanar intended to give them in marriage, the gift is complete only if the recipient accepts it. However, the recipient here accepts them not as wives but as daughters. The Lord states that he accepted them exactly as Kotpuli Nayanar had intended when he made the request. The verse says, "He accepted them as his own pure children, as if they were his own daughters." Kotpuli Nayanar requested him to marry them, but the verse says that Sundaramoorthy Swamigal accepted them as daughters, exactly as Kotpuli Nayanar wished. This is because the two reasons Kotpuli Nayanar had in mind for marriage were that his daughters should serve Sundaramoorthy Swamigal and that they should always worship his holy feet. These two reasons are fulfilled even if they are accepted as daughters. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, who accepted them in this manner, was the husband of Paravaiyar. He had already become the husband of Paravaiyar. Therefore, Sekkizhar Peruman has composed this verse in such a way as to imply that Sundaramoorthy Swamigal was not in a position to accept this request according to Kotpuli Nayanar's intention. Now, the 40th verse: "He kept the women with braided hair on his lap, and with overflowing love for his daughters, he embraced them, kissed their foreheads with tears falling, gave them whatever they desired, and then Nambi, Thambiran Thozhar, went to the Lord's temple." Sundaramoorthy Swamigal kept the women with braided hair on his lap. He accepted them as daughters and placed them on his lap. With great love for his daughters, he embraced them, kissed their foreheads with tears falling, and blessed them. His heart melted, and tears of love flowed, falling on their foreheads as he kissed them. He also gave them whatever they desired and blessed them. Then, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal went to the temple of Lord Shiva in Thirunattiyathankudi. The 41st and 42nd verses are as follows: "He bowed at the sacred gopuram (temple tower) of the Lord, who raises the victorious white bull, and with a focused mind and folded hands, he entered, bowed, and sang a Thiruppathigam (hymn), praising Kotpuliyar, who was worthy of the Lord's grace. In that Thirukkadaikkappu (concluding verse), he mentioned Singadiyar, and thinking of himself as her father, he named himself Singadiyappan, and sang with great joy, receiving abundant grace." Sundaramoorthy Swamigal bowed at the sacred gopuram of Lord Shiva, the pure one, who raises the victorious white bull on his flag. He entered the temple with a focused mind and folded hands, bowed, and sang a Thiruppathigam. This Thiruppathigam begins with "Poonan." It is a Thiruppathigam sung by Sundaramoorthy Swamigal for Thirunattiyathankudi. Thirunattiyathankudi is near Thiruvarur. The Lord is Manikka Vannar, Rathnapurishwarar, Karinalleshwarar, Natthiyathu Nambi. The Goddess is Mangalambigai. The sacred waters are Surya Theertham and Kari Theertham.

In the temple history, it is said that when brothers had to divide their inheritance, they could not agree on the valuation and division of the gems. They appealed to the Lord, and the Lord came as a gem merchant and divided them. Hence, He is known as Rathnapurishwarar. The temple history describes the divine event of Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's visit here. When Sundaramoorthy Swamigal was staying at Kotpuli Nayanar's house, he went to worship at the Thirunattiyathankudi temple. He was surprised not to find the Lord and Goddess there. He asked Ganesha, who, without speaking, pointed towards the northeast. When Sundarar went in that direction, he saw the Lord and Goddess in the field, working as a farmer and his wife, planting paddy. Seeing this, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal sang, "Enough of planting today, we can plant the rest tomorrow. The mud is still there. Enough of planting, come out, Nambi of Natthiyathankudi." Hearing this, the Lord and Goddess disappeared from there and entered the temple. When Sundarar followed them into the temple, a snake blocked the entrance. Then, he sang the Thiruppathigam starting with "Poonan Aavadhor" and had darshan. This is how the temple history describes Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's singing of the Thiruppathigam. The Thiruppathigam sung by Sundaramoorthy Swamigal is the 15th Thiruppathigam of the 7th Thirumurai. It begins with "Poonan Aavadhor Aravu" and is set to the tune of Thakkaragam. "I am not afraid of seeing a snake that is an ornament, nor do I despise seeing the dance in the cremation ground. Even if I am not born, I will realize your greatness. Even if I do not see you, I will see you in my mind. Even if you do not think of me, I will think of you. I will not stop singing your feet, Nambi of Natthiyathankudi." He begins with this first verse and sings ten verses. In this Thiruppathigam, in the tenth verse, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal concludes by singing, "The Lord of Natthiyathankudi, whose ancient fame is known throughout the land, who defeated the hostile kings in battle, the Lord of Thiruvarur, who is always remembered, sung by Vanthondar, the son of Sadaiyan, the father of Vanappakai, the Lord of Thirunavalur, who is like a fragrant garden." In this Thiruppathigam, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal has highly praised Kotpuli Nayanar. The reason Sundaramoorthy Swamigal sang about Kotpuli Nayanar's greatness in the Lord's Thiruppathigam, due to divine grace, is that he was worthy of the Lord's grace. We have seen how Aludaiya Pillaiyar praised Thirunilanakka Nayanar in the Thiruchathamangai Thiruppathigam and Siruthondar Nayanar in the Thiruchenkattankudi Thiruppathigam. We have also seen how Thirunavukkarasu Swamigal praised Appoothiyadigal Nayanar in the Thiruppazhanam Thiruppathigam. Similarly, here, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal praises Kotpuli Nayanar in the Thirunattiyathankudi Thiruppathigam. In addition, in the Thirukkadaikkappu (concluding verse), he inscribes his name as "Singadiyappan," remembering Singadiyar as his own father. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal sings this, considering Singadi Nachiyar and Vanappagai Nachiyar as his own daughters, even though he is not their biological father. He sings as if he were their biological father. Just as there are ways for children to be related to their father through adoption, fostering, or acceptance, here, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal sings, referring to himself as "Singadiyappan," having boldly accepted them as if he were their biological father. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, who sang this Thiruppathigam in this temple, which is the 118th temple on the south bank of the Cauvery in the Chola country, continues to visit other temples. Sekkizhar Peruman continues to sing about this. The 43rd verse is as follows: "From there, with a joyful heart, he went to Thiruvalivalam, where the Lord with the red third eye resides, worshipped him, and composed a Tamil garland of verses, each ending with 'I saw him in Valivalam,' and adorned it with music." From Thirunattiyathankudi, having received permission from the Lord, with boundless love and a joyful heart, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal went to Thiruvalivalam, where Lord Shiva, who has a red third eye, resides. There, he worshipped the Lord, who has Uma as half of his body, and composed a Tamil garland of verses, each ending with "I saw him in Valivalam," and adorned it with music. Thiruvalivalam is a temple located on the way from Thirunattiyathankudi to Thiruvarur. We have contemplated the special features of Thiruvalivalam in the Puranam of Thirugnanasambandha Peruman. The Thiruppathigam sung by Sundaramoorthy Swamigal for Thiruvalivalam is the 67th Thiruppathigam of the 7th Thirumurai. It is set to the tune of Thakkési. In the fifth verse of this Thiruppathigam, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal praises Thirugnanasambandha Peruman and Thirunavukkarasu Peruman. Sekkizhar Peruman mentions this in the 44th verse: "Having danced in between, he went to Thiruvarur, the great city, and bowed at the sacred feet of the Lord of the temple." In this, Sekkizhar Peruman states that in the fifth verse of the Thiruppathigam for Thiruvalivalam, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, with joy, praises and worships the Lord, saying, "You were pleased to hear the sacred verses of Thirugnanasambandha and Thirunavukkarasu, who bring forth goodness." Accordingly, the fifth verse of this Thiruppathigam is as follows: "My servant's heart melted, and you showed me your sacred feet, cutting off my sufferings. You stood, worshipped by the mighty celestials. I saw you in Valivalam, who sang the good songs of Gnanasambandhan and Navinukkarasar." "Nallisai" refers to the Thiruppathigam. The meaning is that the Lord is very pleased and rejoices in the sacred verses of the great saints, Thirugnanasambandha Nayanar and Thirunavukkarasu Nayanar. We have contemplated these truths very well in their Puranams. It is a great honor that Sundaramoorthy Swamigal highlights and praises them here. We realize that this honor also applies to Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's own sacred verses. Having sung thus, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal goes to Thiruvarur, where the Lord resides. He refers to it as "Mandrur." In reality, the Lord of the dance, Ambazhakoothar, resides in Chidambaram. But here, Thyagesa Peruman resides in Thiruvarur. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal has sung about him as "the one who dances in the Mandram." Therefore, it is understood here that Thyagaraja and Nataraja are one and the same. In the Thaduthathkondar Puranam, we also remember that when Sundaramoorthy Swamigal had a darshan in Thillai, the Lord said, "Come to us in Thiruvarur." Similarly, when Sundaramoorthy Swamigal came to Thiruvarur, the Lord of Thiruvarur gave him the first line for the Thiruthondar Thogai Thiruppathigam, "Adiyen to the devotees of Thillai," thus starting with Thillai. Furthermore, historically, there is a record that Thillai Nataraja Peruman once resided in Thiruvarur. Therefore, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, having visited Thirunattiyathankudi and the temples in between, arrives in Thiruvarur. We will continue to contemplate. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 08 - Yeyar Kon Kalikkaama Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Sekkizhar Peruman, by divine grace, continues to sing the Periya Puranam, Part 846, Vampara Vari Vandu Sarukkam, Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar Puranam. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, having visited the temples from Thirunattiyathankudi, has a darshan of the Lord who resides in the anthill in Thiruvarur. He worships the Lord, and then resides in Paravai Nachiyar's mansion with great pleasure. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal was taken as a servant by the Lord and had attained the path of penance. Therefore, he did not have a specific village or place for himself; he considered the Lord's places as his own. Thus, in Thiruvarur, he considered Paravaiyar's mansion and the temple as his own abode. We see this throughout Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's sacred life.

During those days when Sundaramoorthy Swamigal was residing in Thiruvarur, he visited temples like Thiruvalamar, Thirumalumanagai, Thiruchathamangai, Thiruppalliyin Mukkoodal, and others near Thiruvarur. He also had darshan of the Lord of Thiruvarur and resided there. Even though he visited nearby temples every day, he would return to Thiruvarur on the same day and worship Thyagesa Peruman and the Lord who resides in the anthill without fail. During these days, the Thiruppanguni Uthiram festival arrives in Thiruvarur. It was customary for Paravai Nachiyar to provide whatever was needed for the devotees who came to Thiruvarur for this festival, and to give them gold, clothes, and other necessary items every year. Therefore, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal thought of needing gold to provide these items to the devotees. So, he went to Thiruppugalur to worship the Lord, and the Lord granted him gold there. Sekkizhar Peruman begins to sing this sacred history. The 46th verse: "As the Thiruppanguni Uthiram festival in Thiruvarur approached, Nambi thought of obtaining abundant gold for Paravaiyar's charity and for the festival's needs. He went to Thiruppugalur to worship the feet of the Lord there and arrived." This is the 46th verse. As Nambi worshipped the Lord who resides in the anthill and was enjoying himself there, the Thiruppanguni Uthiram festival approached very closely. The Thiruppanguni Uthiram festival in Thiruvarur is very special. It is celebrated for 48 days. During this festival, there are separate celebrations for Ganesha, Subramaniyar, Chandrasekarar, and even Chandesar. On the Panguni Uthiram day, Sri Thyagesa Peruman, as Veethividangar, gives darshan of Rudrapatham and goes on a procession in the great chariot, which is a very special event.
