The western Kongu region is characterized by such landscapes, with many mountains, forests, and flash floods. Rivers like Amaravathi, Nankanchi, Chiththaru, and Kulaganaru flow there. Having crossed these regions, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal visited sacred places such as Moolanur, Thennilai, and Karur, and then arrived at the sacred place of Venjama Koodal. This is a temple located in the southern part of the Kongu Nadu. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal composed a Thiruppathigam at this sacred place. It is the 42nd Thiruppathigam in the 7th Thirumurai, set to the tune of Kolli Kavaandam Pann.

The first and last verses of this Thiruppathigam are as follows:

Thiruchitrambalam. "The small river, whose waters are filled with burning rays, falling pearls, cardamom, young cloves, takkolam, and ginger, flows and surges, eroding its banks. On its eastern bank, where fresh leaves, large trees, bent Punnai, Gnazhal, and Kurukkaththi trees grow, and where cuckoos sing, O Vikirtha of Venjama Koodal, where wild elephants roam, do You also desire me, Your servant?"

"O Vikirtha of Venjama Koodal, where many beautiful women with slender waists like vines, with peacock-like grace, and with beautiful spear-like eyes come and worship, do You also desire me, Your servant? The ten beautiful Tamil verses spoken by Vanthondan, the king of poets of the fertile land, who desired and did not destroy, are sure to lead those who master them to Shiva's abode." Thiruchitrambalam.

Thiruvenjama Koodal is the fifth of the seven sacred places in Kongu Nadu sung about in the Thevaram. It was the capital of the demon king Venjan, hence its name. Traces of the fort are still visible. The town is now a mound of earth, and the temple is situated in a depression, which is one of the signs of its antiquity. The Lord's name is present in every verse of the pathigam. The small river Kulaganaru flows to the west of the temple. The Lord's name is Vikirutheswarar, and the Goddess is Pannin Nermozhi Ammai. The sacred water is Kulaganaru. This sacred place is located on the road from Karur to Aravakurichi, to the southwest. These are the special notes about this sacred place.

Having worshipped at Venjama Koodal, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal crossed many places and traveled a long distance to reach Thirukkarkudi Malai in the south.

The 93rd verse: "Having worshipped the eminent Lord in this Karkudi, which grants liberation, and with great desire, he sang a Thiruppathigam with a full mind. With his devotees, he visited and worshipped many places, and then reached Thiruarai Metrali, which the two seekers (Brahma and Vishnu) could not find."

Sundaramoorthy Swamigal worshipped the eminent Lord in this Karkudi, which grants liberation, falling prostrate to the ground. With great desire and a mind filled with devotion, he sang a Thiruppathigam. The Thiruppathigam composed by Sundaramoorthy Swamigal for Thirukkarkudi is the 27th pathigam in the 7th Thirumurai, set to the tune of Nattarai Pann.

The first and last verses of this Thiruppathigam are as follows:

Thiruchitrambalam. "O Lord with the bull as Your banner, O You with the fragrant Konrai flower, O You with the white axe, O Supreme One, O Paramparam, O Lord who resides in Thirukkarkudi, surrounded by fragrant groves, do not say to me, Your servant, 'Fear not!'"

"Those who master the garland of verses spoken by Singadi Appan, the good one with a bow-like forehead, about Thirukkarkudi, the Kalpaka (wish-fulfilling) tree, where beautiful festivals take place, surrounded by cool, flowing waters, and where great ascetics reside, will rule the three vast worlds." Thiruchitrambalam.

Thus, he worshipped Thirukkarkudi. We have discussed the Sthala Puranam of Thirukkarkudi in detail in the Puranam of Thirunavukkarasar Peruman. Having worshipped at Karkudi, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal visited sacred places such as Thiruchirappalli, Thiruverumbiyur, Thiruvengur, Thirukkattupalli, Thiruvalampuzhil, Thiruppoonduruthi, Thirukkandiyur, Thiruvedikudi, Thiruththandangurai, Thiruppullamangai, Alanthurai, Thiruchakkarappalli, Thiruppalaithurai, Thiruchelur, and Thirunallur, and then reached Thiruarai Metrali. From Thiruchirappalli, a path runs along the southern bank of the Kaveri and Kudamurutti rivers. This path is small from Arasankudi to Thirukkattupalli and then becomes a larger road. This is a shorter, direct route. Therefore, it is believed that Sundaramoorthy Swamigal might have traveled this way. Having visited all the aforementioned sacred places, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal reached Thiruppurambayam, also known as Araimetrali. We will continue to reflect on this. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 15 - Yeyar Kon Kalikkaama Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Sekkizhar Peruman's Periya Puranam, Part 853, Vampara Vari Vandu Sarukkam, Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar Puranam. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal arrives at Thiru Arai Metrali. The 94th verse: "Having bowed to the feet of the Lord in Thiru Arai Metrali, the Lord who bent the golden mountain as a bow, and having rejoiced daily, he received grace to visit many other places praised by the celestials, and then arrived at the town of Thiru Innambar, where people live." Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, having bowed to the feet of Lord Shiva, who bent the golden mountain as a bow, in Thiru Arai Metrali, rejoiced daily. He then received divine permission to visit many other sacred places and arrived at the town of Thiru Innambar. The Thiruppathigam (hymn) composed by Sundaramoorthy Swamigal for Thiru Arai Metrali is not available to us due to our lack of merit. As guided by divine grace, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal visits many places worshipped by the celestials. These places are believed to be Thirushakthimuttam, Thirupattiswaram, Thirupazhaiyarai, Thirupazhaiyarai Vadathali, Thiruvalanjuzhi, and others. Until now, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal had been traveling eastward. Here, he crosses the Kudamurutti and Cauvery rivers and proceeds northward. Now, the sacred places that Sundaramoorthy Swamigal is about to visit, Thiru Innambar and Thiruppurambayam, are located to the north. Therefore, this indicates a northward journey. We can see this as internal evidence in Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's Thiruppathigam itself. "Having come from Thiru Arai Metrali, where the Vedas are recited, and having stayed in Innambar, we are not sure what it is. The Lord's heart, which is praised by the celestials who keep watch at night, will proceed to worship Thiruppurambayam, riding on the great bull." In the first verse of the Thiruppathigam that comes when he arrives at Thiruppurambayam, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal sings about this travel route. Thus, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal arrives at Thiru Innambar. The 95th and 96th verses are as follows: "Having worshipped the feet of the Lord who rejoices in the beautiful Innambar, and having praised Him with abundant love, he resided there with endless grace. With a heart filled with intense desire to worship Thiruppurambayam, where the Lord who flayed the elephant resides, he sang a true Thiruppathigam and proceeded. Starting with 'Angam Othi Or Arai Melthali,' with fervent love, he composed a sweet Tamil Thiruppathigam with the refrain 'will proceed to worship Thiruppurambayam,' and then arrived at Thiruppurambayam, where the Lord who wears the moon resides." Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, having worshipped the feet of the Lord who rejoices in the beautiful Innambar, praised Him with abundant love and resided there with endless grace. With a heart filled with intense desire to worship Thiruppurambayam, where the Lord who flayed the elephant resides, he sang a Thiruppathigam and proceeded towards Thiruppurambayam. When one travels towards a sacred place, singing praises and expressing the desire to reach it shows the intensity of one's devotion to that place. We can recall here the Thevara verses like "Mudhukunru Adaivome" (we will reach Mudhukunru), "Sottruthurai Sendru Adaivom" (we will reach Sottruthurai), and "Kali Sermine" (join Kali).

Sundaramoorthy Swamigal composed the beautiful Tamil Thiruppathigam, rich in both poetry and music, set to the Kolli tune, with perfect rhythm and structure. As he journeyed towards Thiruppurambayam, he composed this Thiruppathigam with the refrain "will proceed to worship Thiruppurambayam" in each verse. Starting with "Angam Othi Or Arai Metrazhi," and with fervent love, he composed the sweet Tamil Thiruppathigam with the refrain "will proceed to worship Thiruppurambayam," and then arrived at Thiruppurambayam, where Lord Shiva resides. The Thiruppathigam composed by Sundaramoorthy Swamigal on his way to Thiruppurambayam is the 35th Thiruppathigam of the 7th Thirumurai, set to the tune of Kollippenn. The first and last verses of this Thiruppathigam are as follows: Thiruchitrambalam. "Having come from Thiru Arai Metrali, where the Vedas are recited, and having stayed in Innambar, we are not sure what it is. The Lord's heart, which is praised by the celestials who keep watch at night, will proceed to worship Thiruppurambayam, riding on the great bull." "These illusions, which cause one to die, be born, excel, and be confused without end, will be overcome by those who, with a pure heart, worship the Lord of Thiruppurambayam, the father of Ooran, with Tamil verses. Those who can speak these words without deceit will rule the vast heavens." Thiruchitrambalam. Thiruppurambayam is the 46th sacred place on the northern bank of the Cauvery in the Chola country. We have discussed the special features of this place in the Puranam of Thirugnanasambandha Peruman. The 97th verse: "The devotees residing in that town, recalling how the peerless, unique Brahmin had taken him as his own by showing the ancient palm leaf in Thiruvannainallur, exclaimed, 'What penance have we performed for him to come to this town?' and welcomed him. He then arrived at the temple entrance of the Lord who burnt the three cities." As Sundaramoorthy Swamigal approached Thiruppurambayam, singing a Thiruppathigam, he arrived at the sacred place of Thiruppurambayam. At that time, all the devotees in that town gathered together, welcomed and worshipped Sundaramoorthy Swamigal. They spoke among themselves, praising and welcoming Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, saying, "What penance have we performed for Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, who was taken as his own by the peerless, unique Brahmin Lord by showing the ancient palm leaf in Thiruvannainallur, to come to this town?" With great joy, they welcomed him. As they welcomed him, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal arrived at the entrance of the Thiruppurambayam temple. The 98th verse: "He bowed before the lofty gopuram, joined the radiant devotees, praised the feet of the Lord who performs the dance, and with folded hands, scattered fragrant flowers, and prostrated on the ground with all eight and five limbs." It is the custom of temple worship to bow before the gopuram (temple tower) and prostrate on the ground before entering the temple. Accordingly, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal bowed to the gopuram and entered. He entered the temple with the devotees who were steadfast in their devotion, serving the Lord without wavering in all aspects of their lives. There, the Lord who performs the dance resides. The dance refers to the fivefold divine acts (Panchakrithya) of grace. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal praises the feet of such a Lord who performs the dance. He clasped his hands in reverence, scattered fresh flowers, and performed Ashtanga and Panchanga Namaskarams (prostrations with eight and five limbs touching the ground). This is a method of worship. The courtyard of the Lord's temple is a glorious place, possessing a unique significance not found in any other place. On such sacred ground, with great devotion, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal performed Ashtanga and Panchanga Namaskarams many times. Now, the 99th verse: "Having received abundant grace there, with overflowing love welling up in his heart, he bowed before the Lord's feet, which are like fresh flowers, praised Him, and then came out of the temple. He then proceeded with the faultless devotees, worshipping the established forms of the Lord who pervades everywhere and rejoices in those places." Thus, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, having worshipped the Lord in Thiruppurambayam, received abundant divine grace there. With overflowing love welling up in his heart, he bowed before the Lord's feet, which are like fresh flowers, praised Him, and then came out of the temple. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal then proceeded to worship the established forms of the Lord who pervades everywhere and rejoices in those places. The Lord pervades everywhere; this is one of the qualities that shows His supreme nature and omnipresence. This is called Sarva Vyapakam (all-pervading). He is everything and resides in everything without separation. This quality is discussed in the Nindra Thiruththandagam, Sri Rudram, and other texts. Such a Lord pervades everywhere. He can be seen everywhere. If the Lord who is everywhere can be seen everywhere, then why should one go to many sacred places and temples to worship Him? The Lord manifests brightly like ghee in some places, while in other places He remains hidden. Therefore, it is essential to go and worship Him in places where He manifests brightly. He manifests brightly in those places, that is, in the form of devotees and in Shiva temples. This is the reason given for the indispensability of Shiva temple worship in the 12th Sutra, 3rd Adhikarana of Sivagnanabodham. Therefore, to worship the divine forms of the Lord in places where He rejoices, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal proceeds, accompanied by faultless devotees who have received grace. We will continue to contemplate. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 16 - Yeyar Kon Kalikkaama Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Sekkizhar Peruman's Periya Puranam, Part 854, Vampara Vari Vandu Sarukkam, Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar Puranam. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, traveling northward from Thiruppurambayam, crossed the Kollidam River and, after visiting sacred places on the northern bank of Kollidam such as Govandaputhur, Pazhuvur, Kadambur, Karakkoyil, and Kadambai Ilankoyil, decided to go to Thirumudhukunram, also known as Virudhachalam. At that time, there was a sacred place called Thirukkoodalaiyatrur on the way. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal approached that place. The 100th verse: "Vanthondar, with a garlanded chest, bowed to many sacred places where our Lord, who wears the Vanni, Konrai, Thumbai, white Adambu, the moon, and pure water on His head, resides, and then approached Koodalaiyatrur, where women dance." Nambigal, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, has a chest adorned with many garlands. Our Lord wears the Vanni, Konrai, Thumbai, white Adambu (a type of plant), the moon, and pure Ganga water on His sacred head. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal visited and worshipped many sacred places where such a Lord joyfully resides, and then arrived at Thirukkoodalaiyatrur. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal arrived at Thirukkoodalaiyatrur, which is on the way to Thirumudhukunram, also known as Virudhachalam. The 101st verse: "As the peerless and renowned Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, who cannot be described, proceeded towards Thirumudhukunram, the Supreme Being, who is one with Uma, appeared as a Brahmin, wearing a three-stranded sacred thread, before Nambi Arurar." As Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, of peerless fame, proceeded towards Thirumudhukunram, Lord Shiva appeared before him as a Brahmin, wearing a three-stranded sacred thread. He appeared in the form of a Brahmin, wearing a three-stranded sacred thread. The Brahmin is the form, and the three-stranded sacred thread is the adornment. Thus, Lord Shiva appeared before Sundaramoorthy Swamigal in this form. The 102nd and 103rd verses: "Looking at the one who stood there, he bowed with a melted heart and asked, 'Tell me the way to Mudhukunru today.' The Lord, who held the mountain as a bow, said, 'This is the way to Koodalaiyatrur,' and accompanied him as a guide. Those who saw him clasped their hands, bowed, and followed, but could not see the Lord of Konrai, who had a beautiful battle-axe. They praised him and said, 'I do not know the wonder of the Lord of the celestials having come.' With rising devotion, he entered Koodalaiyatrur." Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, looking at Lord Shiva, who stood before him in the form of a Brahmin, asked with a melted heart, bowing to the Brahmin, "Today, I need to go to Thirumudhukunram. Please tell me the way." Sundaramoorthy Swamigal first bowed to the Brahmin who stood in his path. The Brahmin also made Sundaramoorthy Swamigal look at him. Upon seeing that divine form, a sense of familiarity arose in Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's heart. Even though the form had changed, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal recognized him as Lord Shiva due to their previous connection. Therefore, he bowed to the Brahmin with devotion. He asked, "Today, I need to go to Thirumudhukunram. Please tell me the way to Thirumudhukunram, also known as Virudhachalam." To this, Lord Shiva, who held the mountain as a bow, replied, "This is the way to Koodalaiyatrur," and then accompanied Sundaramoorthy Swamigal along that path. The Brahmin, who was asked for the way to Thirumudhukunram, said, "This is the way to Koodalaiyatrur," and then took Sundaramoorthy Swamigal with him. The Lord, who accompanies souls on their journey through various births, never leaving them, is the companion on the path, isn't He? He is the Margasahayar (helper on the path), isn't He? As it is said, "Others will leave the path, but Shiva alone is the eternal companion on the path for all beings in all births." Thus, as a companion on the path to Koodalaiyatrur, the Brahmin accompanied Sundaramoorthy Swamigal for some distance. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal followed the Brahmin, clasping his hands and bowing.

Suddenly, the Brahmin disappeared. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal could no longer see the Brahmin who had been accompanying him. Immediately, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal began to praise the Lord who wears the Konrai flower, starting with "Vadivudai Mazhu" (beautiful battle-axe). He sang a Thiruppathigam, saying, "I do not know the wonder of the Lord of lords, who appeared in disguise and then disappeared." Thus, singing the Thiruppathigam, he arrived at Thirukkoodalaiyatrur. On his way to Thirukkoodalaiyatrur, he composed this Thiruppathigam with the refrain "I do not know the wonder of this path" or "I do not know the wonder of the Lord having come this way" in each verse. The Thiruppathigam thus composed is the 85th Thiruppathigam of the 7th Thirumurai, set to the tune of Puranirmai. The first and last verses of this Thiruppathigam are as follows: Thiruchitrambalam. "O Lord, who holds the beautiful battle-axe, who wears the skin of the mad elephant, whose sacred body is adorned with ash, who is with Uma of the curly hair, in Koodalaiyatrur with lofty mansions and banners, I do not know the wonder of Your having come this way." "Those who master these ten verses, spoken by Naval Ooran, about the Lord who delights in dancing with the slender-waisted one in Koodalaiyatrur, saying 'this is a wonder,' will attain their reward." Thiruchitrambalam. Thus, singing the Thiruppathigam, he went to the Thirukkoodalaiyatrur temple and worshipped the Lord. Thirukkoodalaiyatrur is the third sacred place in Nadunadu. This place is called Koodalaiyatrur because it is located at the confluence of the Thiru Manimutharu and Vashishta rivers, which later join the Vadavellaru. The Vashishta river, after joining the Vadavellaru, is known as Nivathi. It is located west of Chidambaram on the road to Kumarakkudi. To reach Koodalaiyatrur, one can come to Kumarakkudi from the Sethiyathoppu-Kumbakonam road, then take the road to Srimushnam, turn onto the Kavalakkudi road, and travel a short distance. The Lord's name is Narthana Vallabhesvarar or Nerikattunathar. The Goddess's name is Parasakthi, Gnanasakthi, or Purikuzhal Nayagi. There are two Amman shrines. The sacred waters are Manimutharu, Vellaru Sangama Theertham (confluence of Vellaru and Manimutharu), Brahma Theertham, Agasthiya Theertham, and Karthiyayana Theertham. This place is called Dakshina Prayagai. It is said in the Sthala Puranam that at one time, the temple was damaged by a flood, and the stones from it were used to build a new temple in this village, where the Lord and Goddess were consecrated. Sage Agasthiyar prayed to the Lord here so that he would not forget the knowledge he had gained. Agasthiyar prayed to the Lord for a daughter. By the grace of the Lord, Agasthiyar found Mahalakshmi on a lotus flower in the Manimutharu river flowing here. He took the child, named her Ambujavalli, and raised her. Vishnu, in his Varaha avatar, married Ambujavalli. There is a separate shrine for Vishnu here. Lord Shiva revealed His dance to Vyagrapada Munivar and Patanjali Munivar in Chidambaram. Brahma worshipped the Lord at this place and prayed. Granting his wish, the Lord danced for Brahma and Saraswati here. Therefore, the Lord is known as Narthana Vallabhesvarar. Dinakara Maharaja, who ruled the Chola country, suspected his wife's conduct and killed her. Because of this, he was afflicted with the sin of killing a woman (Sthrihaththi Dosham). He wandered from town to town due to this sin. At that time, a dog was following him. One day, the dog plunged into a river and emerged healthy and radiant. Seeing this, the king bathed in the confluence of the two rivers and was freed from his sin. He then built a temple for Lord Shiva in Koodalaiyatrur. The main deity's vimana (tower above the sanctum) has two levels. If one climbs the stairs, there is a beautiful mandapam (hall). Among the processional deities here, Chitragupta is special, depicted holding a stylus in one hand and a palm leaf in the other. There is no Navagraha shrine here. In the two Amman shrines, sacred ash is given at the Parasakthi Amman shrine, and kumkum (vermilion) is given at the Gnanasakthi Amman shrine. Outside the compound wall, there is an idol of Agasthiyar. For the first three days of the Chithirai month, the sun's rays fall on the main deity, and Surya Puja (sun worship) is performed. During Masi Magam, a 13-day Brahmotsavam (grand festival) is held. These are the Sthala Purana notes about Thirukkoodalaiyatrur. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, having sung the Thiruppathigam, arrived at the Thirukkoodalaiyatrur temple. The 104th verse: "Sundaramoorthy Swamigal entered the glorious temple of the Lord with the Konrai-garlanded matted hair in Koodalaiyatrur, and with overflowing devotion, he worshipped the feet adorned with sounding anklets, which dance in the golden hall, praised Him, received abundant grace, and then proceeded to Thirumudhukunram." Sundaramoorthy Swamigal entered the glorious temple of the Lord with the Konrai-garlanded matted hair in Koodalaiyatrur, and with overflowing devotion, he worshipped the feet adorned with sounding anklets, which dance in the golden hall, praised Him, received abundant grace, and then proceeded to Thirumudhukunram, also known as Virudhachalam. We will continue to contemplate. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 17 - Yeyar Kon Kalikkaama Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Sekkizhar Peruman's Periya Puranam, Part 855, Vampara Vari Vandu Sarukkam, Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar Puranam. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal travels from Thirukkoodalaiyatrur to Thirumudhukunram, also known as Virudhachalam. In this sacred place, Lord Shiva bestows 12,000 gold coins upon Sundaramoorthy Swamigal. Sekkizhar Peruman sings about this divine history in four consecutive verses: 105, 106, 107, and 108. "He circumambulated the temple with its grand gopuram (tower), entered, bowed, and prostrated. He sang a garland of beautiful verses beginning with 'Nanjiyidai' to the Lord who performs the dance, and stood with folded hands. When he praised the Lord with the intention of asking for wealth, with a heart free from impurities, the Lord who wears the Konrai flower, granted him grace. The leader of the Shiva Brahmins, Nambi, received 12,000 gold coins from the Lord, who had a body adorned with white ash and a moon-crested matted hair. With overflowing joy, he bowed and approached the Lord, whose throat was dark from poison, and said, 'Let all this gold that you have given me be received in Thiruvarur, to the astonishment of its residents.' The Lord, with clear words from the sky, said, 'Deposit all this wealth in the Manimutharu river, and you will receive it in the Thiruvarur tank.'" Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, upon reaching Thirumudhukunram, bowed to the grand gopuram, prostrated on the ground, circumambulated the temple, entered, and praised the Lord who performs the dance. "Nadam navizhvar" means one who performs a dance. The Lord's dance is the fivefold dance of grace, the Panchakrithya, which bestows blessings upon the world. The Lord performs this. "Navizhuthal" means to perform. If "navizhuthal" means to speak, then it implies that the Lord imparts knowledge to the world through His dance. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal sang a garland of beautiful verses beginning with "Nanjiyidai" to such a Lord who performs the dance, and stood with folded hands. "Nanjiyidai" is the beginning of the Thiruppathigam. The Thiruppathigam composed by Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, beginning with "Nanjiyidai," is the 43rd Thiruppathigam of the 7th Thirumurai, set to the tune of Kolli Kavaanam. The first and last verses of this Thiruppathigam are as follows: Thiruchitrambalam. "O Lord of Thirumudhukunram, where conches resound amidst the dense groves, what shall I do if I die tomorrow, having been poisoned by those who hate me? Tell me, Lord, will the cotton in the wick burn? Grant me your grace." "These incoherent words of Ooran, the servant of the Lord, who is like a madman, about the Lord of Thirumudhukunram, who is surrounded by the sacred Manimutharu river, will not cause any trouble to those who are true yogis and worship Him. There is no suffering for those who worship Him, no matter what penance they perform." Thiruchitrambalam. Thus, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal composed the Thiruppathigam. Now, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal intends to receive wealth from the Lord. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal did not explicitly ask the Lord for it; he only intended in his heart to receive wealth from the Lord. The Lord also gave His divine consent. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, realizing the Lord's divine consent, then composed another Thiruppathigam. We saw earlier in Thiruppachilachiramam that even though Sundaramoorthy Swamigal intended to receive wealth and composed a Thiruppathigam, the Lord did not reveal His divine consent. Therefore, due to not receiving grace, he composed a Thiruppathigam there as if complaining to the devotees present. However, here in Thirumudhukunram, as soon as he intended to receive wealth from the Lord, the Lord revealed His divine consent to Sundaramoorthy Swamigal. Upon receiving this, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal composed another Thiruppathigam. In this Thiruppathigam, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal emphasized his right to receive grace by saying "Ezhupirappum Engal Nambi" (Our Lord for seven births) and used "Nambi Nambi" (Lord, Lord) throughout the verses. The Lord, who wears the garland of blossoming Konrai flowers, granted the grace of giving wealth. Nambigal, the leader of the Shiva Brahmins, sang a Thiruppathigam beginning with "Meiyil Vennpodi" (white ash on the body). The Thiruppathigam thus composed is the 63rd Thiruppathigam of the 7th Thirumurai, set to the tune of Thakkesi. As we saw earlier, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal used "Nambi Nambi" throughout the verses of this Thiruppathigam. Therefore, this Thiruppathigam is known as the "Nambi Thiruppathigam." The first and last verses of such a Thiruppathigam are as follows: Thiruchitrambalam. "A Lord who smears his body completely with ash, who recites the Vedas, who holds a white battle-axe in his hand, who has three eyes, a beautiful Lord, a Lord with small reddish matted hair, a Lord who shot an arrow with a bow to burn the three cities, a Lord who rules me, our Lord for seven births, behold!" "The Lord who milks, the Lord who melts those who are unyielding, the Lord who bestows joy in this world and the next upon those who melt, the Lord of abundant grace." Thiruchitrambalam. Only half of the tenth verse of this Thiruppathigam is available to us. Thus, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal composed the Thiruppathigam. As Sundaramoorthy Swamigal composed the Thiruppathigam, the Lord with the moon-crested matted hair bestowed 12,000 gold coins, as mentioned in the verse, "bestows 12,000 gold coins with great grace." Lord Shiva bestowed 12,000 gold coins with great grace. Upon receiving this, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, with great joy, praised the Lord from where he was and then moved a little closer. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal then made another request to the Lord. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal requested the Lord, "Let all this gold that You have given me be received in Thiruvarur, to the astonishment of its residents." Upon this request, the Lord, with clear words from the sky, spoke as an ethereal voice, saying, "Deposit all these 12,000 gold coins in the Manimutharu river, and you will receive all of it in the Thiruvarur tank." Sundaramoorthy Swamigal had requested that the 12,000 gold coins be received in Thiruvarur, to the astonishment of its residents. Accordingly, in the continuation of this story in Thiruvarur, there is a delay in the arrival of these 12,000 gold coins, which causes astonishment. Later, upon singing a Thiruppathigam, these 12,000 gold coins reappear in the Kamalalayam tank in Thiruvarur. This verse here serves as an introduction or a hint to that event. Therefore, Lord Shiva commands that the gold be deposited in the Manimutharu river flowing in Thirumudhukunram and be received in the Thiruvarur tank, to the astonishment of everyone there. Now, the 109th and 110th verses: "After receiving such sweet grace from the Lord, Vanthondar cut a piece of the gold, took it in his hand, and immersed the gold ingots in the Manimutharu river. He then departed, thinking, 'I will know the grace that forcibly took me as His own on that day.' With fervent love and devotion, he received true spiritual growth. He then bowed and departed towards the ancient city of Thillai, surrounded by ponds, with the intention of worshipping the dance of the Lord with the dark throat in the Puliyur hall, where Brahmins of pure lineage reside." As commanded by Lord Shiva, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, having received the divine word of Lord Shiva, took the 12,000 gold coins and went to the Manimutharu river. There, he cut a small portion from the 12,000 gold coins given by the Lord, kept it with him, and immersed the gold ingots in the Manimutharu river. It was a custom for merchants to cut a small piece from a pile of gold ingots to compare its quality. Therefore, the 12,000 gold coins given by the Lord were gold ingots, and Sundaramoorthy Swamigal cut a small piece from one of them, kept it with him, and immersed the rest in the Manimutharu river. As he immersed them, what did Sundaramoorthy Swamigal think? He thought, "I will truly know the grace that forcibly took me as His own in Thiruvannainallur on that day, through this act," and immersed them in the Manimutharu river. He thought, "I will receive the 12,000 gold coins that I immersed in the Manimutharu river in Virudhachalam in the Thiruvarur tank. Through this, I will realize and understand that the Lord's divine grace is eternal." Thus, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal immersed them. Sekkizhar Peruman praises such a Sundaramoorthy Swamigal in the next verse, saying, "He received true spiritual growth, filled with fervent love and devotion." Sundaramoorthy Swamigal was born with the task of serving the Lord with great love and devotion as his life's purpose. Sekkizhar Peruman describes this as "Mei Viruththi Petrar," meaning he received true spiritual growth. "Mei Viruththi" refers to good deeds performed by the body. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal was born into the lineage of Brahmins who served Lord Shiva, who is half-female, generation after generation. Therefore, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal naturally possessed the inner service (Agampadi Thondu) that is unique to Shiva Brahmins or Sivacharyas, in accordance with the lineage in which he was born. Moreover, when the Lord forcibly took him as His own in Thiruvannainallur, the Lord commanded him, saying, "Let your worship be songs, therefore, sing Tamil songs about us on earth." As commanded by the Lord, he performs the service of true divine speech. Therefore, Sekkizhar Peruman praises Sundaramoorthy Swamigal as "Mei Viruththi Petrar," meaning he received true spiritual growth. "Viruththi" means profession or occupation, which is called "Jeevanopayam" (livelihood). Since Sundaramoorthy Swamigal had no other occupation than serving the Lord and praising Him, it became the Lord's duty to protect Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, who accepted that service. That is why the Lord bestows upon Sundaramoorthy Swamigal whatever he desires, whenever and wherever he desires it. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal also receives it. Such a Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, who received true spiritual growth, now intends to perform "Aaviyin Viruththi," which is the spiritual benefit or occupation. He intends to directly behold and worship the divine dance of the Lord with the blue throat in the Puliyur Thiruvambalam (Chidambaram), where Brahmins reside. With this intention, he departs on a pilgrimage towards Thiruthillai. Having received divine permission from the Lord in Thirumudhukunram, he bows to the Lord and proceeds towards the ancient city of Thiruthillai, surrounded by ponds. We will continue to contemplate. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 18 - Yeyar Kon Kalikkaama Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Sekkizhar Peruman's Periya Puranam, Part 856, Vampara Vari Vandu Sarukkam, Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar Puranam. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal departs from Thirumudhukunram and arrives at Chidambaram. The 111th verse: "Having visited and worshipped the surrounding sacred places, he arrived at the ancient city of Thillai, where the Lord, the consort of the Goddess, performs the dance, and entered through the beautiful gate, reaching the glorious street adorned with lofty mansions." Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, departing from Thirumudhukunram (Virudhachalam) and traveling towards Thiruthillai, visited and worshipped all the sacred places along the way. He visited places like Kadambur and Thiru Erukkaththampuliyur. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal then arrived at the ancient city of Thillai, where the Lord of abundant bliss performs the dance. From Virudhachalam to Chidambaram, the path leads to the western gate of the city of Chidambaram, allowing entry into Chidambaram. Therefore, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal entered the city of Thillai through the western gate, which is the gate of the ancient city that pervades all worlds, and arrived at the glorious street adorned with lofty mansions where the Thillai Brahmins and heads of monasteries reside. Now, Sekkizhar Peruman describes the divine history of Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's darshan of Thillai in five verses: 112, 113, 114, 115, and 116. "He bowed to the golden street, approached the good sacred gate that shines as the fruit of merit, and bowed to it on the ground before entering. He circumambulated the golden-walled enclosure, bowed, and placed his hands on his head. He entered through the beautiful golden gopuram before the Lord who performs the dance, bowed, and approached the dancing red feet in the shining golden hall. With overflowing tears of joy from his eyes, his voice choked with emotion, he bowed to the sacred steps, prostrated with all five and eight limbs, and with intense desire, the divine dance filled his heart and overflowed. He beheld the Lord, who was revealed without concealment in his heart, and felt complete satisfaction. He sang a Thiruppathigam beginning with 'Madiththadum Adimaikkan,' praising the Lord who, with His power to bestow grace upon all living beings, prevents those who follow the good path from straying and falling into hell, emphasizing the vision he had in Perur, and saying, 'We have attained the Lord who performs this unique dance in our minds.' Having received endless grace, he came out and worshipped the street. Vanthondar, who was devoted to the Lord, resided there, praised by the Brahmins, and with boundless love, worshipped the golden city. He then departed and arrived at Thirukkaruppariyalur, where the Lord who conquered Yama resides, and worshipped Him." These five verses are like the Sri Panchaksharam. This is Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's second visit to Chidambaram. However, considering that he saw the blissful divine dance of the Thillai Lord in Perur, this is his third darshan of the blissful dance. Now, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, "Naraimali Alangal Marpar" (one whose chest is adorned with honey-filled garlands), bowed to the beautiful street of Thillai, approached the good sacred gate that shines as the fruit of merit, and bowed to it. The gopuram (tower) of Chidambaram is a sacred gate that shines as the fruit of merit. The merit mentioned here is Shiva Punya (merit gained through devotion to Shiva). It is true that one receives the blessing of entering this sacred gate only as a result of countless Shiva Punyas performed in the past. As it is said, "Even a little knowledge gained from worshipping Shiva for many eons will lead to liberation." We should contemplate this idea here. In the 1165th verse, Sekkizhar Peruman elaborates on this idea, saying, "The gate, like the hearts of the great people living in that city, stands tall and steadfast. It is a path that leads to the good conduct taught by Uma's consort for the salvation of all worlds, and it prevents one from straying from the right path, upholding truth and righteousness." Thus, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal bowed to the gopuram, prostrated on the ground, circumambulated the golden-walled enclosure, bowed, and placed his hands on his head. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal entered through the beautiful golden gopuram before the Lord who performs the blissful dance and bowed. This is the inner gopuram directly in front of the Lord Nataraja. This inner gopuram, where the flagstaff is located, is also called the Thiruvannukkan Thiruvayil (the gate near the Lord). Entering through that inner gopuram, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal approached the dancing red feet in the shining golden hall. With overflowing tears of joy from his eyes, his voice choked with emotion, he bowed to the sacred steps. He prostrated with all five and eight limbs. These are called Panchanga and Ashtanga Namaskarams, respectively. Thus, bowing, with his intense desire growing stronger, the divine dance filled his heart and overflowed. He beheld the Lord, who was revealed without concealment in his heart, and felt complete satisfaction, recalling the vision he had in Thirupperur. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal then sang a Thiruppathigam beginning with "Madiththadum Adimaikkan." Sundaramoorthy Swamigal composed this Thiruppathigam, reflecting on the Lord's boundless compassion and nature. He sings about the Lord who, with His power to bestow grace upon all living beings, prevents those who follow the good path from straying and falling into hell, emphasizing the vision he had in Perur, and saying, "We have attained the Lord who performs this unique dance in our minds." Sundaramoorthy Swamigal sings with great joy, "What a blessing it is that we have attained the Lord who performs this unique dance!" When living beings, due to their past karma, take birth and experience the fruits of their actions, they follow the path of dharma. If they stray from this path, they will fall into hell. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal sings that our Lord, the great Lord, prevents those who follow the path of Shiva from straying and falling into hell. There is also a hint here that Sundaramoorthy Swamigal praises the divine grace, reflecting on it. Earlier, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's attempted marriage in Puthur was not an evil path but a good one in worldly life. However, if that marriage had taken place, it would have led to further births. Therefore, to prevent him from falling into further births and to grant him liberation, the Lord appeared as an old Brahmin and forcibly took him as His own. Moreover, when the Lord commanded him to go to the earthly realm from Thirukkailai, he requested a boon that when he goes to the earthly realm and becomes confused as a human, the Lord should come and forcibly take him as His own. Accordingly, the Lord took him as His own. Similarly, Lord Shiva, who is the refuge of devotees, helps Shiva devotees to remain steadfast in the path of Shiva and prevents them from straying. If one strays from the path of Shiva, one will experience terrible hell. The Lord, with such great compassion, prevents Shiva devotees from straying from the path of Shiva and falling into hell. He is the great compassionate Lord. He showed this very vision of the Chidambaram dance in Perur. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal sings this Thiruppathigam with great joy, saying, "What a blessing it is that we have attained such a Lord who performs this unique dance!" The Thiruppathigam thus composed is the 90th Thiruppathigam of the 7th Thirumurai, set to the tune of Kurinji. The first and last verses of this Thiruppathigam are as follows: Thiruchitrambalam. "O Lord, who forcibly takes me as Your own, not just as a servant who dances with folded hands, but also when the messengers of Yama, who is fierce and dances, come to throw me into the oil press, You come and take me as Your own. We have attained our Lord in the Chidambaram of Puliyur, who dances with a drum in His hand, a fierce snake, and a black snake." "We have attained our Lord in the Chidambaram of Puliyur, the Lord of Thillai, the consort of Uma, whose waist is like a snake, who rides a bull with green eyes, who is the Lord of Oor, who will take me as His own when the messengers of Yama come to throw me into the oil press. We have attained the Lord of Perur in the beautiful Kanchi of Kongu, in the Chidambaram of Puliyur." Thiruchitrambalam. Thus, having composed the Thiruppathigam and received loving permission from the Lord, he came out of the temple and worshipped the street. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, the devoted servant of the Lord, resided there, praised by the Thillai Brahmins. At this point, a divine story from the Perur Puranam is cited by our Sivakavimani Desikanar. The Thillai Brahmins, having heard the Thiruppathigam sung by Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, in which he says, "We have attained the Lord of Perur in the Chidambaram of Puliyur," asked Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, "There is no other place in the world like Thillai, yet you have praised Perur here. Why?" Sundaramoorthy Swamigal replied, "Just as in Thillai, the Lord always performs the blissful divine dance in the Arasampalam of Perur." Hearing this, the Thillai Brahmins went to Perur, saw the Lord performing the blissful divine dance there, established a sacred place called Azhagiya Thiruchitrambalam, worshipped there, and then returned to Thillai. This divine story is mentioned in the Azhagiya Thiruchitrambalam Padalam of the Perur Puranam. The first three verses from it are: "The Thillai Brahmins, whose Tamil music was excellent, heard Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, who had arrived at Perur, worshiped, and praised the Lord with matted hair, sing in his Thiruppathigam, 'We have attained the Lord of Perur.' They asked the friend of the Lord, 'Our Lord resides in Thillai, and there is no other place in the world as exalted as this. Why did you praise the glory of Perur?' When the Brahmins of noble character asked, Vanthondar, with great joy, explained to them the situation, saying, 'Just as the Lord performs the dance here, so too does our Lord perform the blissful divine dance eternally in the forest of Bodhi there.'" Thus, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, having received divine permission from the Lord in Thillai, departed from the temple and, coming out to the street, worshipped it. Vanthondar, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, who was devoted to the Lord, resided there, praised by the Thillai Brahmins. With boundless love, he worshipped that beautiful sacred place and then departed, arriving at Thirukkaruppariyalur, where the Lord who conquered Yama resides, and worshipped Him. We will continue to contemplate. Thiruchitrambalam.

Thiruchitrambalam. Sekkizhar Peruman's Periya Puranam, Part 857, Vampara Vari Vandu Sarukkam, Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar Puranam.

Chapter 19 - Yeyar Kon Kalikkaama Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Sekkizhar Peruman's Periya Puranam, Part 857, Vampara Vari Vandu Sarukkam, Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar Puranam. From Chidambaram, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal goes to Thirukkaruppariyaloor and worships. Following this, Sekkizhar Peruman describes the pilgrimage undertaken by Sundaramoorthy Swamigal in 5 verses, from the 117th to the 121st. Those verses are as follows: "He who kicked Yama, in Thirukkokudi Temple, having worshipped the tower and entered, surrounded by devotees, with great love, he bowed and praised, and with boundless joy in his heart, he praised and came out, staying in that town. Recalling the saints and speaking of that joy, he adorned himself with the Tamil garland of true Thiruppathigam (hymn) beginning with 'Simmandhu' and stayed there for some days."

"Having worshipped Thirukkaruppariyaloor, beloved by the Lord with the eye on His forehead, he departed and reached Thiruppazhamannippadikkari, where carp leap in the fertile Manni River. There, he bowed to the Lord who shares His body with the Goddess, praised Him, and sang a Thiruppathigam of countless glories, beginning with 'Munnavan' (The Foremost One). As he was going, he remembered Thiruvazhkozhiputhur, which he had not yet reached, and returned, entering it. He began by praising 'Thalaikkalan' (He who wears the skull as an ornament) and sang a Thiruppathigam. Having reached there, at the entrance of the Lord's temple, with rising goosebumps, he bowed, entered, worshipped, and praised with melting love. He praised the Lord who has the daughter of the mountain king on His left side, came out, and stayed there. Leaving Thiruvazhkozhiputhur, where beautiful black sugarcane fields thrive, he reached Thirukkanattumulloor. As he approached Thirukkanattumulloor, the Lord with the eye on His forehead appeared before him. Seeing Him, 'I saw and worshipped the lotus feet of the Lord with matted locks adorned with pure, soft Konrai flowers,' he sang a Thiruppathigam that rules the heavens, a garland of powerful Tamil verses beginning with 'Valvai'. He then proceeded to Thiruedhirkolpadi, surrounded by fragrant flower gardens. As he went, he sang a Thiruppathigam expressing his resolve, 'Let us reach Thiruedhirkolpadi, where the Lord with the elephant-like strength is praised and worshipped in all directions.' Having arrived, he worshipped the rich and grand temple, bowed to the Lord, stayed there, received His grace, and reached Thiruvelvikudi. There, he praised the Lord who grants liberation, singing a Thiruppathigam beginning with 'Mooppathillai' (There is no old age) in Thiruthuruthi." Thus, the 5 verses conclude.

He reached Thirukkaruppariyaloor, the temple of the Lord who kicked Yama, worshipped the temple tower, entered, and surrounded by devotees, with great devotion, he bowed and praised. With boundless joy filling his heart, he praised and came out, staying in that town. Recalling the holy Lord and speaking of the joy that comes from it, he adorned himself with the Tamil garland of Thiruppathigam beginning with 'Simmandhu' and stayed there. The Thiruppathigam sung by Sundaramoorthy Swamigal for Thirukkaruppariyaloor is the 30th Thiruppathigam in the 7th Thirumurai (collection of hymns). The first and last verses of this Thiruppathigam are as follows: Thiruchitrambalam. "Simmandhu simbuliththu sindhaiyinil vaithu ugandhu thirambavannam kaimavin urivaip porththu umai veruva kandanai karuppariyaloor koimavin malarcholai kuyil pada mayiladum kogudikkoyil emmanai manaththinal ninainthabodhu avar namakku iniyavare." "Kalaimalintha thenpulaivar katrortham idarthirkkum karuppariyaloor kulaimalintha kol thengu mattu ozhugum pooncholai kogudikkoyil ilaimalintha mazhuvanai manaththinal anbu seythu inbam eythi malaimalintha tholvooran vanappagai appan uraiththa vanthamizhgale." Thiruchitrambalam.

Thirukkaruppariyaloor is the 27th shrine on the northern bank of the Kaveri River, in the Chola country. It is also known as Melaikkazhi Thalaignayiru. Kogudikkoyil is the name of the temple. The temple history states that once, Indra, filled with arrogance, went to Kailash. When the Lord appeared before him in the form of a Yaksha, Indra, unknowingly, launched his Vajrayudha (thunderbolt) at Him. Realizing his mistake, he bowed and worshipped, and to atone for his fault, he came here, worshipped the Lord, and received blessings. The Lord's name is Kutram Poruthanathar (Lord who forgives sins), and the Goddess's name is Kolvalai Nayaki. This place can be reached a short distance west from Thiruppunkur, then reaching Mannippallam, and from there a short distance north.

Having worshipped Thirukkaruppariyaloor and received the Lord's grace, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal reached Thiruppazhamannippadikkari, a fertile land where carp leap in the water. There, he bowed to the lotus feet of the Lord, who shares His body with Uma, praised Him, and sang a Thiruppathigam of countless glories, beginning with 'Munnavan'. Thus, he worshipped the Lord. The Thiruppathigam sung by Sundaramoorthy Swamigal for Thiruppazhamannippadikkari is the 22nd Thiruppathigam in the 7th Thirumurai, set to the tune of Nattaraga Pann. The first and last verses of this Thiruppathigam are as follows: Thiruchitrambalam. "Munnavan engal piran mudhal kanbu arithaya piran senniyil engal piran thiru neelamidatru empiran manni engal piran marai nankum kallal nizhal keezh panniya engal piran pazhamanni padikkaraiye." "Palvuyir vazhum thenneer pazhamanni padikkaraiyai alli antha marai thar arooran uraiththa thamil solluthal kettal vallar avarkkum thamarkkum kilaikkum elliyum nanpagalum idarkooruthal illai anre." Thiruchitrambalam.

The Lord's abode is Pazhamannippadikkari. Sekkizhar Peruman praises this as a Thiruppathigam of countless glories because it describes the Lord's attributes and urges devotees to reach and worship Him. Thiruppazhamannippadikkari is the 30th shrine on the northern bank of the Kaveri River in the Chola country. It is also known as Izhaippaippattu. The temple history states that when the Lord consumed poison, the Goddess prevented it from going down further by holding His throat, thus revealing His blue throat (Thiruneelakandam). The Lord's name is Thiruneelakandar, and the Goddess's name is Amirthakaravalli. We can see the Lord's name praised as 'Thiruneelamizhatru Empiran' in the Thiruppathigam. It is reachable a short distance from Vaidheeswaran Koil.

Having worshipped Thiruppazhamannippadikkari, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal continued his pilgrimage. At that time, he suddenly remembered that Thiruvazhkozhiputhur was nearby and that he was passing by without worshipping there. So, he turned back, reached Thiruvazhkozhiputhur, praised the Lord, and sang a Thiruppathigam. Geographically, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal was going west from Thirukkaruppariyaloor to Pazhamannippadikkari. It is customary and a duty of pilgrimage to worship the shrines that fall in between. However, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, while going from Thirukkaruppariyaloor to Pazhamannippadikkari, passed Thiruvazhkozhiputhur without worshipping it. After going some distance, he remembered Thiruvazhkozhiputhur, returned, and sang a Thiruppathigam, with the refrain "Vazhkozhiputhur manikkaththai marandhu en ninaikkene" (Why do I think of anything else, having forgotten the gem of Vazhkozhiputhur?) in each verse.

The Thiruppathigam sung by Sundaramoorthy Swamigal for Thiruvazhkozhiputhur is the 57th Thiruppathigam in the 7th Thirumurai, set to the tune of Thakkesi Pann. The first and last verses of this Thiruppathigam are as follows: Thiruchitrambalam. "Thalaikkalan thalaimel thariththanai thannai ennai ninaikka tharuvanaik kolaik yanai uriporththu ugandanai kootruthaiththa kuraiser kazhalanai alaiththa senkan vidaiyeravallanai anaiyal adiyen adinayen malaiththa sennal vayal vazhkozhiputhur manikkaththai marandhu en ninaikkene." "Valankizhar pozhil vazhkozhiputhur manikkaththai marandhu en ninaikken endru ulankulir thamil ooran vanthondan sadaiyan kadhalan vanappagai appan nalankizhar vayal navalar vendhan nangai singadi thanthai payantha palankizhar thamil padavallar mel paraiyumam seytha pavangalthane." Thiruchitrambalam.

The Lord's name in Thiruvazhkozhiputhur is Manikkavannar, and the Goddess's name is Vandamar Poonkuzhali. It is the 29th shrine on the northern bank of the Kaveri River in the Chola country. It is also known as Vazhozhithuthur and Vazhapputrur. Since it contains a Manikya Lingam worshipped by Vishnu, this shrine is called Arathanapuram in Sanskrit. We see that the Lord's name is Manikkavannar. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal also praises Him as 'Manikkaththai' in every verse of the Thiruppathigam. References to Vishnu's worship and Arjuna's worship are also found in the Thiruppathigam. The history states that Arjuna hid his sword in an anthill (putru), hence the name Vazhozhithuthur. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal has sung a Thiruppathigam of 12 verses for this shrine. The Sthala Vriksham (sacred tree) is the Vagai tree, and the Theertham (sacred water body) is Brahma Theertham.

Having sung this Thiruppathigam and reached the shrine, he bowed at the entrance of the Lord's temple, with goosebumps rising on his body, worshipped, praised, and glorified the Lord who has the daughter of the mountain king on His left side. Then, he came out and stayed there. Leaving that Thiruvazhkozhiputhur with beautiful, soft sugarcane, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal approached Thirukkanattumulloor. As he reached Thirukkanattumulloor, the Lord with the eye on His forehead appeared before Sundaramoorthy Swamigal. Expressing the sentiment "I saw and worshipped the Lord adorned with pure, fresh, soft Konrai flowers," Sundaramoorthy Swamigal praised Him by adorning Him with a garland of rich Tamil verses beginning with 'Valvai', which has the power to command the celestial world.

The Thiruppathigam thus sung is the 40th Thiruppathigam in the 7th Thirumurai, set to the tune of Kolli Kavuvanam Pann, and belongs to Thirukkanattumulloor. Sekkizhar Peruman praises this as "Vaan Alum Thiruppathigam" (the Thiruppathigam that rules the heavens). In this Thiruppathigam, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal praises the Lord as the King of the celestials, the one who swallowed and spat out the worlds, the four-faced one, the one who became the world, the one who is the five elements, the one with eight attributes, the one who is yesterday, today, and tomorrow, the jewel among the Devas, the King of the celestials, the Lord of the immortals, the one with many forms, the one who shows us the true path so that we do not sink into the fierce hell, the primordial one of the Vedas. Because Sundaramoorthy Swamigal thus describes the Lord's ultimate nature and His nature as the Lord of the Devas, Sekkizhar Peruman praises it as "Vaan Alum Thiruppathigam," meaning the Thiruppathigam that governs the celestials. In the Thirukkadaikkappu (concluding verse) of this Thiruppathigam, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal also states the benefit of the Thiruppathigam: "They themselves will stand as leaders even for the celestials."

The first and last verses of such a special Thiruppathigam are as follows: Thiruchitrambalam. "Valvai mathimizhum valar sadaiyinanai maraiyavanai vaaimozhiyai vanavar tham konai pulvai keendulagam vizhungi umizhnthanai pon niraththin muppuri nool nan mugaththinanai mulvai madarazhuvi mudathazhai eendru mottalarnthu virainarum muruguviri pozhil soozh kalvai karunguvazhai kanvalarum kalani kanattumullooril kandu thozhuthene." "Thirainar kadal soozhntha thennilangai konai setravanai senjadai mel venmathiyinanai karainar punal thazhuvu kollidaththin karaimel kanattumullooril kandu kazhal thozhuthu urainar mathaiyanai navalar arooran urimaiyal urai seytha onthamizhgal vallar varainar vagai gnanamandavarkkum tham poy vanavarkkum thalaivaray nirpar avar thame." Thiruchitrambalam.

Thirukkanattumulloor is the 32nd shrine on the northern bank of the Kaveri River in the Chola country. It is also known as Kanattampuliyur. It is the shrine where Patanjali Muni worshipped the Lord. Therefore, the Lord's name is Patanjalinathar, and the Goddess's name is Kanar Kuzhali Kol Valai Kai Ammai. Surya Theertham and Surya Pushkarani are the sacred water bodies. The Sthala Vriksham is Vellarku. It is an ancient temple built of red bricks. An inscription from the time of Vikrama Chola mentions this shrine as Virutharaja Bhayankara Valanattu Keelkanattumulloor, also known as Thiruchitrambala Chathurvedi Mangalam. It is located a short distance southwest of Chidambaram and very close to Mannargudi.

Having worshipped Thirukkanattumulloor, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal received the Lord's grace and proceeded to Thiruedhirkolpadi. Let us continue our contemplation. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 20 - Yeyar Kon Kalikkaama Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Sekkizhar Peruman's Periya Puranam, Part 858, Vampara Vari Vandu Sarukkam, Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar Puranam. Having worshipped Thirukkanattumulloor, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal received the Lord's grace and proceeded to Thiruedhirkolpadi. In Thiruedhirkolpadi, he praised the Lord and sang a Thiruppathigam beginning with 'Mathaiyanai' (The intoxicated elephant). In it, he sang a Thiruppathigam expressing his resolve, "Let us reach Thiruedhirkolpadi." This Thiruppathigam emphasizes the impermanence of the world and imprints it in our minds. Therefore, Sekkizhar Peruman praises it as "Siththanilai Thiruppathigam" (Thiruppathigam of resolute state).

The Thiruppathigam sung by Sundaramoorthy Swamigal for Thiruedhirkolpadi is the 7th Thiruppathigam in the 7th Thirumurai, set to the tune of Indalappann. The first and last verses of this Thiruppathigam are as follows: Thiruchitrambalam. "Maththa yanaiyeri mannar soozha varuveergal seththa pothil arumillai sindhaiyul vaimingal vaiththa ullam matra venda vammin manaththire aththar koyil edhirkolpadi enbathu adaivome." "Muththuneetru pavazhameni senjadaiyan uraiyum paththar panthaththu edhirkolpadi paramanaiye paniyasiththam vaiththa thondar thondan sadaiyan avan siruvan paththan ooran padal vallar patham panivare." Thiruchitrambalam. In this entire Thiruppathigam, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal emphasizes the impermanence of the body, wealth, and other aspects of life.

Having worshipped Thiruedhirkolpadi, he worshipped the rich and grand temple, bowed to the Lord's feet, stayed in that town, received His grace, and proceeded to Thiruvelvikudi to worship Lord Shiva. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal worshipped Thiruvelvikudi and then went to Thiruthuruthi to worship the Lord. He combined the two shrines, Thiruvelvikudi and Thiruthuruthi, and sang a Thiruppathigam beginning with 'Mooppathillai' (There is no old age). That Thiruppathigam is the 18th Thiruppathigam in the 7th Thirumurai, set to the tune of Nattaraga Pann. The first and last verses of this Thiruppathigam are as follows: Thiruchitrambalam. "Mooppathum illai pirappathum illai irappathu illai serppathu kattagaththu oorinamaaga sinthikkin allal kappathu velvikudi than thurutthi em kon araimel arpathu nagam arinthomel nam ivarkku atpadome." "Koodalar mannan kula navaloor kon nalathamizhai padavalla paraman adiyarkku adimai valuva nadavalla thondan arooran atpadumaru solli padavallar paralogaththu iruppathu pandam anre." Thiruchitrambalam. We have already contemplated the special features of Thiruthuruthi and Thiruvelvikudi.

Thiruedhirkolpadi is the 24th shrine on the northern bank of the Kaveri River in the Chola country. It is known as Melaithirumanjeri. Thirumanjeri, the 25th shrine, is very close to it. It is said that the name Edhirkolpadi came from the history of the Lord going to meet and escort a prince, who was a devotee, in the guise of a father-in-law. It is a shrine where Airavatham worshipped and received blessings. The Lord's name is Airavatheeswarar, and the Goddess's name is Malarkozhil Nayaki.

Now, the 122nd and 123rd verses: "Having worshipped the beautiful wedding form of the Lord in Thiruvelvikudi with devotion in his heart, Nambi Aroorar praised Him. Surrounded by ascetics, he departed from there, filled with love. He worshipped the many shrines where the Lord had taken him as His own, and then, with boundless love, accompanied by the devotees, he praised the many abodes of the Lord with the poison-stained throat. Surrounded by flower gardens where clouds gather and lotus ponds, he reached Thiruvarur, with its rich paddy fields and fertile lands." These are the verses.

In Thiruvelvikudi, the Lord is in the form of a bridegroom, known as Kalyana Sundarar. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal worshipped that beautiful wedding form of the Lord with devotion in his heart and praised Him. Surrounded by devotees who had accumulated merits through long penance, he departed from there. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal worshipped many shrines where the Lord, bound by love, had taken him as His own. The companions were devotees who had accumulated merits through penance, devotees who had performed great penance over many births. It is implied here that only such steadfast devotees could have the privilege of being surrounded by Sundaramoorthy Swamigal and worshipping him. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, accompanied by such devotees who possessed boundless and unwavering love, worshipped and praised many abodes of the Lord. These shrines include Thiruazhundhur, Thiruazhuvur, Thirumeeysur, Thiruthilathaipathimutram, Thiruambar Maganam, Thiruambar Perunthirukkoil, Thirukkondeeswaram, Thiruvirkudi, Thiruppalliyin Mukkoodal, and others.

Sundaramoorthy Swamigal undertook a very long pilgrimage. Therefore, he decided to go from Thiruvelvikudi to Thiruvarur and continued that journey. The shrines mentioned above are those that fell on his way to Thiruvarur. He praised all these shrines and reached Thiruvarur. We do not have the Thiruppathigams sung by Sundaramoorthy Swamigal for these shrines. Having worshipped many such abodes, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal reached Thiruvarur, which is surrounded by flower gardens where clouds gather, and lotus ponds, with abundant fertile lands. The verses here describe the water resources, land fertility, and other glories of Thiruvarur. It is described that clouds surround the gardens, the gardens enclose the lotus ponds, and these ponds, being numerous, surround the fields. Thus, Thiruvarur is surrounded by fertile lands rich in fields.

Now, the next verse, the 124th verse: "In the prosperous Thiruvarur, where Devas and sages gather, they came to the grand temple tower, worshipped, and entered. With boundless love, they raised their hands in supplication, and with many devotees, they approached the Lord." Thiruvarur is specifically spoken of as "Selvamali Thiruvarur" (Thiruvarur rich in wealth). Here, wealth refers to both worldly wealth and the wealth of liberation. We previously saw the explanation of the name Thiruvarur as "Thiru Arkindra Oor" (the town where prosperity abounds). Such is Thiruvarur, rich in wealth. There, Devas and sages gather at the grand temple tower, the Devasiriya Thirumandapam. The devotees gathered in the temple, along with the devotees accompanying Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, all together, with boundless love, raised their hands above their heads and, accompanied by many devotees, approached the Lord.

The 125th verse: "The Lord, who is the beginningless, middleless, and endless primordial one, who has the bull banner, is enshrined in Thirumoolattanaththu. With unending great love, he worshipped Him and came out. He then reached the mansion of Paravaiyar, whose fame is unwavering." The Lord is always full of divine qualities, without beginning, middle, or end. He is enshrined in Thirumoolattanaththu in Thiruvarur. One can see the Lord, who is everywhere, manifested in Thirumoolattanaththu. Thirumoolattanam in Thiruvarur is the place where Vanmeeganathar, the Lord who resides in the anthill, is enshrined. It is said that he worshipped the Lord "in Thirumoolattanaththu," not "Thirumoolattanaththu Iraivar" (the Lord of Thirumoolattanam). This implies that the one supreme Lord Shiva, who is omnipresent, is enshrined here in Thirumoolattanam, and also in Thirumuthukundram, and in many other shrines in the form of Linga manifestations and in temples. This refers to the fact that he received gold in Thirumuthukundram, released it into the river, and is now going to retrieve that gold from the Thiruvarur tank by the grace of the Lord. Thus, this verse is structured with that implication.

Having worshipped the Lord, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal came out of the temple. The first duty upon reaching a shrine is to worship the Lord in the temple before doing anything else. This was the practice of the elders in ancient times, the path shown to us by them. We are reminded here of the history of Sivapatha Hrudayar, who, upon reaching Madurai, first worshipped Lord Sokkanathar in Thirualavai before going to the monastery where Thirugnanasambandha Peruman resided. Unlike this, nowadays, some people, upon reaching a shrine, first attend to all other matters and only then go to the temple. This is very regrettable. Having worshipped the Lord and come out of the temple, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal proceeded to Paravai Nachiyar's mansion. Let us continue our contemplation. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 21 - Yeyar Kon Kalikkaama Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Sekkizhar Peruman's Periya Puranam, Part 859, Vampara Vari Vandu Sarukkam, Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar Puranam. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal arrived in Thiruvarur, worshipped Vanmeeganathar (the Lord who resides in the anthill), and then proceeded to Paravaiyar's mansion. Four verses, 126, 127, 128, and 129, follow consecutively. They are as follows: "With surging great love, he bowed at the feet of Paravaiyar, whose lotus eyes and red fruit-like lips were praised by many women with flowing tresses. She sweetly said, 'You have remembered even us,' and with that beautiful lady, he stayed happily. On those days, the Nayanar said, 'The good treasure given to us by the Lord of Muthukundram, we cast it into the pure Manimutharu River. By His grace, go and retrieve it from the tank on the western side of His temple's mansion, and bring it.' When he said this, she, with her lightning-like waist, smiled faintly and said, 'What a wonder is this! What are you saying?' He, who knew the truth, said, 'By the grace of my Lord, I will retrieve the gold from the tank and give it to you. It will not be false.' As she also accompanied him, with boundless joy, they entered and worshipped the ancient Lord who rejoices within the beautiful temple. They circumambulated the grand temple mansion and then went to the beautiful tank on the western side, accompanied by Paravaiyar, his unique companion."

When Sundaramoorthy Swamigal arrived at Paravaiyar's mansion, Paravai Ammaiyar came and bowed at Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's feet, saying, "Are we worthy of your grace, that you have remembered even us and come here?" Paravai Nachiyar meant, "You, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, whose heart thinks of none other than Lord Shiva, have remembered even us, who are not worthy of being remembered, and have come here. Are we worthy of such grace?" Thus, Paravai Ammaiyar bowed at Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's feet. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal spoke sweet words to Paravai Ammaiyar, and on a day when they were happily together, he said, "Lord Shiva, who is our Lord and resides in Thirumuthukundram, bestowed upon us a good treasure of gold. We cast that gold into the Manimutharu River in Virudhachalam. By the grace of Lord Shiva, we will go and retrieve that gold from the Kamalalayam tank, which is on the western side of His temple's mansion in Thiruvarur. You also come with me."

Sekkizhar Peruman refers to the gold given by the Lord as "Nannidhiyam" (good treasure), even though gold is a product of Maya. Although this gold is a material object of impure Maya, it is a divine gift received from the Lord, and therefore it becomes a beneficial good treasure. The Lord of Thirumuthukundram, who resides in Virudhachalam, is the same Lord who resides in the beautiful temple in Thiruvarur. There, in Thirumuthukundram, the Lord said, "Cast it into the Manimutharu River and retrieve it from the Thiruvarur tank." Accordingly, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal cast the gold into the river there. Therefore, it is now the Lord's duty to return that gold in the Thiruvarur tank as He had promised.

When Sundaramoorthy Swamigal said this, Paravai Nachiyar was greatly astonished. She said with a faint smile, "What a wonder is this! What a miracle!" Then Sundaramoorthy Swamigal said, "O lady with a beautiful forehead! By the grace of my Lord, I will retrieve all the gold from this tank and give it to you. It will not be false." Paravai Nachiyar's faint smile was a hint of sarcasm. Even though Paravai Nachiyar said, "What a wonder! What a miracle!" it was as if she spoke like those who doubt the Lord's grace. Therefore, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal said, "I will retrieve the gold that my Lord gave us from the tank and give it to you." Sundaramoorthy Swamigal also said, "You yourself accept that gold in your hand and serve ambrosia to the devotees during the Panguni Uthiram festival." Sundaramoorthy Swamigal also said that he would give all 12,000 gold coins without any reduction.

Having said this, as Paravai Ammaiyar also accompanied Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, with boundless joy, they entered and worshipped the holy Lord who rejoices within the beautiful temple of Thiruvarur. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal circumambulated the grand temple mansion of Thiruvarur. From there, he proceeded to the Kamalalayam tank, which is on the western side of the Thiruvarur temple. We can contemplate here the ideas from the 10th Sutra of Sivagnanabodham: "He does nothing without His grace," and "He stands as one, ever in the Lord's service." Sundaramoorthy Swamigal says, "By His grace, I will go and retrieve it," and "By the grace of my Lord, I will retrieve it from the tank and give it to you." These statements remind us of the ideas in that 10th Sutra. It is a proven truth that Nambigal (Sundaramoorthy Swamigal) was a supreme Acharya who was completely devoid of self-assertion and was inextricably merged in the Lord's grace. Therefore, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal proceeded to the Kamalalayam tank, which is on the western side of the Thiruvarur temple, accompanied by Paravai Nachiyar.

Now, six verses, 130, 131, 132, 133, 134, and 135, describe the subsequent divine event, as narrated by Sekkizhar Peruman. Those verses are as follows: "He came to the northeastern bank of the tank, made the young lady stand there, and the Lord of Munaippadi, with matted locks, worshipped the Lord and descended into the tank. As if he were putting it in and taking it out on that very day, he searched there. The Lord, beautiful with sacred ash, was pleased with his song and, in His divine play, did not bring the pure gold into the tank. 'What is this grace, that you cast it into the river and search for it in the tank?' asked the lady with bangles. As the unique devotee, following the grace he had received before, without a smile appearing on Paravai's beautiful, fragrant lips, he said, 'Give it to me.' He then sang a Thiruppathigam praising the Lord, 'O Brahmin with the sacred thread on your chest, O Lord with a body of gold, who resides in Muthukundram.' Even after eight verses, the gold that the celestials know was given in Muthukundram did not appear, and he was distressed by its absence. He sang the verse, 'Give me the gold that I cast, in front of her.' Even after eight verses, the gold did not appear. He sang the verse, 'I have never failed to praise you.' He prayed to the Lord with the eye on His forehead, who dances in the cosmic hall, protecting all worlds, 'O Dancer, grant it in front of this tender lady.' As he, who stood on a path unknown even to ascetics, prayed, by the grace of the Lord who dances with Konrai flowers on His head, a heap of gold emerged. He retrieved it in due order. Flowers rained from the sky, and the people on earth worshipped, saying, 'What a wonder is this! Who else can receive such a blessing?'"

Thus, the six verses conclude. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, with Paravai Nachiyar, reached the northeastern bank of the Kamalalayam tank in Thiruvarur. He made Paravaiyar stand on the bank, worshipped the Lord, and descended into the tank. He searched there as if he were putting in and taking out the 12,000 gold coins. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal searched on the northeastern bank. This northeastern bank of Kamalalayam is the bank that is on the northeastern side if one comes through the western gopuram (tower) entrance of the Thiruvarur temple. The northeast is considered the direction of Ishana, which holds great significance. Currently, there is a shrine for Maatruraiththa Pillaiyar (Pillaiyar who verified the purity of gold) there. This Pillaiyar is enshrined here with a name related to this story. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal is entering into a great act where the Lord's grace is about to manifest. Therefore, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal worships the Lord specifically before entering. We see this in the history of Sundaramoorthy Swamigal and in the history of great Acharyas.

Sundaramoorthy Swamigal searches as if to retrieve the 12,000 gold coins. The Lord, beautiful with sacred ash, is very pleased with Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's songs. Therefore, due to His divine play, wanting to receive Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's Thiruppathigam, He did not bring the 12,000 gold coins into the tank. Lord Shiva, beautiful with sacred ash, thus delays the appearance of the gold. Sacred ash signifies dissolution. After dissolution, re-creation occurs. Similarly, the gold that disappeared will be given again. The Lord desires to hear the sweet Thiruppathigam sung by Nambigal. Therefore, Lord Shiva delays for a short while, engaging in this divine play, in order to hear Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's Thiruppathigam.

As the Lord delayed, Paravai Ammaiyar, who had previously smiled with a hint of sarcasm, now confirmed it again, saying, "Did you cast it into the river and are now searching for it in the tank?" Paravai spoke, incorporating the proverb "searching in the tank after casting into the river." The underlying meaning of Paravai Ammaiyar's statement is that it is a futile act. An object cast into a river cannot be found even if searched for in the same spot in the river. In such a situation, casting it into a river in Virudhachalam (Thirumuthukundram) and searching for it in a completely different place, a tank in Thiruvarur, is an impossible task. Thus, Paravai Ammaiyar asks, "Are you searching in the tank after casting it into the river?" Here, the river refers to the Manimutharu River in Thirumuthukundram, and the tank refers to the Kamalalayam tank in Thiruvarur. This makes it a very apt occasion for Paravaiyar's laughter. Paravaiyar asks, "You said you would receive this by the Lord's grace. Is this how the Lord's grace manifests for us?" Paravai Ammaiyar asks and laughs.

Therefore, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal requests the Lord. With the intention that Paravaiyar should not laugh, "O Lord, please grant the gold as you graced me in Thirumuthukundram," Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, wearing the sacred thread on his chest, sings a Thiruppathigam beginning with "O Lord, whose body is like gold, who resides in Thirumuthukundram." The Thiruppathigam sung by Sundaramoorthy Swamigal is the 25th Thiruppathigam in the 7th Thirumurai, set to the tune of Nattaraga Pann. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal continues to sing the verses of this Thiruppathigam. Even after singing eight verses, the 12,000 gold coins did not appear. Therefore, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal sings the ninth verse, beginning with "Yen Imaihor" (Why the celestials). Sundaramoorthy Swamigal prays, "O Lord with the eye on your forehead, the great Dancer in the cosmic hall who protects all worlds, please grant the 12,000 gold coins in front of this tender Paravai." He is one who stands on a path unknown even to ascetics.

The first, ninth, and last verses of the Thiruppathigam sung by Sundaramoorthy Swamigal are as follows: Thiruchitrambalam. "Pon seytha meniyineer puli tholai araikkasaiththeer mun seytha mooveyilum eriththeer muthukundru amarnththeer min seytha nun idaiyal paravai ival than mugappe en seythavaaru adikel adiyen ittalam kedave." "Eththathu irunthariyene imaiyor thani nayakane mooththay ulagukku ellam muthukundram amarnthavane pooththarum kuzhalal paravai ival than mugappe kooththa thanthu arulvay kodiyen ittalam kedave." "Piraiyarum sadayam peruman arulay endru muraiyal vanthu amarar vanangum muthukundrar thammai maraiyarththam kurisil vayal naval arooran sonna iraiyar padal vallarkku elitham sivalogamathhe." Thiruchitrambalam.

At that time, by the grace of the Lord, the divine Dancer adorned with Konrai flowers, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal received the heap of 12,000 gold coins. He took it and came ashore. Flowers rained from the celestial world. All the people on earth worshipped, saying, "What a wonder is this!" They worshipped, saying, "Who else but Sundaramoorthy Swamigal can receive such divine grace? No one else." This is the Thiruppathigam sung by Sundaramoorthy Swamigal as if reminding the Lord in a friendly way, "You gave an ethereal voice in Thirumuthukundram, which was heard not only by kings but also by celestials. Therefore, you cannot refuse to give this. You cannot deny it."

Sundaramoorthy Swamigal receiving gold from the Lord, casting it into the river there and retrieving it from the tank here, and singing with Paravai Nachiyar as the focus—all these should not be misunderstood by worldly people as attachment to gold and women, leading to offenses. Therefore, Sekkizhar Peruman praises Sundaramoorthy Swamigal as "Neeththarum thodarvariya neri nindrar" (one who stood on a path unknown even to ascetics), meaning that he stood on the path of Shiva, which is difficult even for ascetics of severe penance to follow. Thus, Sekkizhar Peruman praises him. Having received the 12,000 gold coins, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal came ashore. Lord Shiva continues His divine play.

The 136th verse: "Having retrieved the good gold, which brought forth divine knowledge, and as the Lord with the blue throat, by His grace, delayed in giving the purity of the gold, the devotee praised the feet of the Lord, who is difficult for Vishnu and Brahma to reach." Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, having retrieved the 12,000 gold coins and come ashore, to the astonishment of all the people of the world, checked the purity of the gold. We have already seen that before casting the gold into the river in Thirumuthukundram, he cut off a small portion and kept it with him. So, when he compared the gold he now received from the tank, by the grace of Lord Shiva, the gold from the tank was of lower purity. Therefore, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal again praised Lord Shiva.

Gold is a material object born of Maya, which can lead to the delusion of Maya and rebirth. We should recall here the history of Aludaiya Arasugal Thirunavukkarasu Peruman, who, in the courtyard of Thiruppukalur, did not even touch the pure gold and precious gems that came by divine grace. He carried them in his sacred hoe and cast them into the beautiful lotus pond.
