There, the Lord, revealing the good state of Arasugal to the world, blessed him so that gold and gems appeared wherever Thirunavukkarasu Peruman used his hoe. Through that divine grace, the true state of Arasugal's goodness was established, and the world came to know it and attain salvation. Here, the Panguni Uthiram festival is approaching in Thiruvarur. This gold was received by Sundaramoorthy Swamigal from the Lord to prepare sacred food for the devotees on that festival day. He cast it into the river and retrieved it from the tank. Therefore, this becomes a good treasure, because it is gold given by the Lord to feed sacred food to the pure devotees of Lord Shiva. Therefore, Sekkizhar Peruman refers to it as "Narkanagam" (good gold) in this verse.

When an object received by divine grace is cast into a river and retrieved from a tank by divine grace, it is not to be assumed that it will be returned exactly as it was, without any reduction in purity. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, having cut off a small piece of gold and kept it with him to check its purity, caused Lord Shiva to now make the gold of lower purity. We are reminded here of the Thiruvandap Paguthi of Thiruvachagam: "To those who thought, 'We will see in this trick,' He removed that trick." Now, due to the Lord's divine play, He delayed the appearance of the gold, so Sundaramoorthy Swamigal sang a song. Similarly, here, when the Lord, by His grace, made the gold of lower purity, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal again praised the Lord and sang a Thiruppathigam.

Now, the 137th verse: "When he praised Him again, the Lord, who rejoices in songs born of true love, accepted it. The gold, which had a defect, became pure, without any flaw, and Sundaramoorthy Swamigal took it and came ashore." Sundaramoorthy Swamigal again praised the Lord. The Lord, who rejoices in songs born of true love, accepted it. The defect in the gold was removed, and He gave pure gold to Sundaramoorthy Swamigal without any flaw. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal took it and came ashore. It is understood that Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, who had been standing on the steps of the northeastern part of the tank until then, now came ashore. Correspondingly, the temple of Maatruraiththa Pillaiyar is also located in the northeastern part of the tank.

The 138th verse: "Having come ashore with Paravaiyar, he had all the gold carried by many people to the tall mansion. He then worshipped the fragrant lotus feet of the Lord of Thirumoolattanam, whose matted locks bear the flowing waters, and reached the street." Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, having come ashore, sent all the gold, which were gold ingots, to Paravai Ammaiyar's mansion. He had them carried as a burden by people and sent them to Paravai Ammaiyar. Then, he went to worship the lotus-like feet of Vanmeeganathar, who resides in Thirumoolattanesvarar in Thiruvarur. He came to worship Thirumoolattanesvarar to retrieve the gold. Now, having received the gold, when he returned, he worshipped Thirumoolattana Nathar and came to the street outside the Lord's temple, the street that leads to Paravai Ammaiyar's mansion, which is outside the southern entrance of the beautiful temple.

The 139th verse: "Having entered the mansion, as the sound of endless auspicious blessings rose in abundance, the protector of the people of Thiruvarur, with fragrant flower gardens, sat with the beautiful lady, filled with joy." Having arrived at the street on the southern side of the temple, he entered Paravai Ammaiyar's mansion. As the sound of endless auspicious blessings, offered by countless people, resounded there, Nambigal Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, the protector of the people of Thirunavalur, with fragrant flower gardens, sat with Paravaiyar in the mansion, filled with heartfelt joy. Let us continue our contemplation. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 22 - Yeyar Kon Kalikkaama Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Sekkizhar Peruman's Periya Puranam, Part 860, Vampara Vari Vandu Sarukkam, Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar Puranam. The 140th and 141st verses: "On a certain day, he worshipped the Supreme Being residing in the beautiful anthill of Thiruvarur. He sang a Thiruppathigam praising Him, with unending bliss overflowing. With the devotees, he inquired about the resolute truth, worshipped, danced, and sang. He began with 'Paaru Thaangi' (He who bears the earth), and with overflowing joy in his heart, goosebumps rose on his body, tears filled his eyes, and boundless bliss appeared in countless ways. He praised, rejoiced, and was filled with happiness."

On that day, when Sundaramoorthy Swamigal was residing in Thiruvarur, he regularly worshipped Vanmeeganathar, the Lord who resides in the beautiful anthill of Thiruvarur. At that time, he praised the Lord and sang a Thiruppathigam. In that Thiruppathigam, with unending bliss overflowing, he, along with the devotees, inquired about the resolute truths of the Lord's great grace in various ways, and thus sang the Thiruppathigam. Singing by mingling with devotees and asking questions about the Lord's greatness as if he did not know them is one of the most excellent ways to immerse oneself in divine grace. The 12th Sutra of Sivagnanabodham states, "Anbarudu Mariyi" (mingling with devotees). This is spoken of as one of the characteristics of Jivanmuktas (liberated souls). We can see this characteristic in the Thirukkandiyur Pillaiyar Thiruppathigam and in the Thiruppathigams sung when he first arrived in Thiruvarur, where he prioritized the devotees. We should remember that Thiruvarur is a shrine famous for its devotion.

Thus, he sang a Thiruppathigam. It was a Thiruppathigam that began with 'Paaru Thaangi' (He who bears the earth), perfectly set to music. With overflowing joy in his heart, goosebumps rose on his body, tears filled his eyes, and boundless bliss appeared in countless ways. He praised, rejoiced, and was filled with happiness. It was set to the tune of Kollippenn. The first and last verses of this Thiruppathigam are as follows: Thiruchitrambalam. "Paaru thaangiya kaadaro paduthalaiyaro malai pavai oor kooru thaangiya kuzhagaro kulaikkatha kurungottu ila eru thaangiya kodiyaro sudupodiyaro ilankum pirai aru thaangiya sadaiyaro namakku adigalagiya adigale." "Padisai neermayin bhakthargal paninthu ethinen paniyeer arulvadiyilan thirunavaluran vanappagai appan vanthondan sediyanagilum theeyanagilum thammaiye manam sinthikkum adiyan uranai azhvaro namakku adigalagiya adigale." Thiruchitrambalam.

Now, the 142nd verse: "On those days of joyful residence, having received the grace of the King adorned with snakes in the strong anthill, praised by the endless Vedas, he departed with loving devotion to worship the Lord with shining matted locks in countless other shrines." On those days when he resided there with joy and devotion, having received the grace of the Lord who resides in the anthill in Thiruvarur, praised and glorified by the Vedas, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal departed with boundless love and devotion to worship the Lord with shining matted locks in countless other shrines.

"With his retinue accompanying him, in every suitable shrine, he worshipped the feet of the Lord who wears the elephant hide, rejoiced, and praised Him. The pure and great devotee, Nambigal, proceeded to worship Thirunallar, where true devotees, who never forget Shiva, came to receive him." This is the 143rd verse. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal departed from Thiruvarur, accompanied by his retinue, his servants. He visited nearby shrines such as Thiruvilamar, Thiruppalliyin Mukkoodal, and others. He worshipped the feet of the Lord who wears the elephant hide, rejoiced, and praised Him. The pure and great devotee, Nambigal, proceeded to worship many shrines and reached Thirunallar. As Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, intending to worship Thirunallar, proceeded, true devotees, who never forgot Shiva, came to receive him, and he entered Thirunallar.

The 144th verse: "He bowed to the sky-touching gopuram (tower), raised his hands above his head, entered, circumambulated the Lord's temple, and with surging great love, he prostrated on the ground at the lotus feet of the Lord of Thirunallar." He bowed to the sky-touching gopuram of Thirunallar temple, rose, raised his hands above his head, entered, circumambulated the temple, and with surging great love, he prostrated on the ground at the beautiful lotus feet of the steadfast Lord of Thirunallar. Having worshipped thus, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal sang a Thiruppathigam. The Thiruppathigam for Thirunallar is the 68th Thiruppathigam in the 7th Thirumurai, set to the tune of Thakkesi Pann. The first and last verses of this Thiruppathigam are as follows: Thiruchitrambalam. "Semponmeni venniru anivanai kariya kandanai mal ayan kanaa sambuvai thazhal angaiyinanai samavedhanai than oppu ilanai kumbama kariyin uriyanai kovin melvarum kovinai engal nambanai nallaranai amuthai nayinen maranthu en ninaikkene." "Nerinthu solaigal soozhntha nallarru em sivanai navalur singadi thanthai maranthu nan matra ninaippathu ethu endru vanappagai appan uran vanthondan sirantha malai anjinodu anjum sinthai ulvorugi seppavallarkku iranthu pokku illai varavu illai agi inba vellaththul iruppargal inithe." Thiruchitrambalam.

In the 6th verse of this Thirunallar Thiruppathigam, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal provides internal evidence of the story of the Lord taking him as His own in Thiruvannainallur, saying, "Vennainalluril arputha pazha avanam katti adiyana ennai al athu konda nalpathaththai" (He showed the wonderful ancient document in Vennainallur and took me, His servant, as His own). In the 8th verse, he praises the Lord as "Adiyenukku elivantha thuthanai thannai thozhamai aruli thondan en seytha thurisugal porukkum nathanai" (The messenger who came easily to me, who granted me friendship, the Lord who forgives the faults committed by me, His servant). In the 10th verse, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal refers to himself as Singadi Thanthai Vanappagai Appan. All these serve as internal evidence for the history of Sundaramoorthy Swamigal.

Now, the 145th and 146th verses describe the history of Sundaramoorthy Swamigal visiting Thirukkadavur Mayanam and Thirukkadavur. "Having worshipped the Lord there and received His grace, he departed and reached Thirukkadavur, with its beautiful mansions where clouds gather. He worshipped the Thirumayanam of the Lord with the moon-adorned crown and praised Him with a surging musical Thiruppathigam, beginning with 'Maruvar Konrai'. He then worshipped the feet of the Lord of Thiruveerattanam, who destroyed the fierce valor of Yama, and with great devotion, he sang a garland of Tamil verses beginning with 'Podiyar Meniyane' and proceeded to Thiruvazhamburam, the Lord who bent the mountain." Having worshipped and praised the Lord thus, and having received His grace, he departed and reached Thirukkadavur, with its beautiful mansions where clouds gather. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal worshipped the Thirumayanam shrine of the Lord and sang a Thiruppathigam beginning with 'Maruvar Konrai'.

Thirukkadavur and Thirukkadavur Mayanam are two parts of the same shrine, with temples located close to each other. Kadavur is the name of the place. Thirumayanam is a shrine there, and Veerattam is another shrine. So, Thirukkadavur Thirumayanam temple and Thirukkadavur Veerattam temple are located in Thirukkadavur. The Thiruppathigam sung by Sundaramoorthy Swamigal praising the Lord there is the 53rd Thiruppathigam in the 7th Thirumurai, set to the tune of Pazhampanjuram Pann. The first and last verses of this Thiruppathigam are as follows: Thiruchitrambalam. "Maruvar konrai mathisoodi manikkaththin malai pola varuvar vidaimel matho magizhndhu boothappadai soozha thirumal biraman indrarkkum devar nagar thanavarkkum peruman kadavur mayanaththu periya peruman adigale." "Madam malku kadavurin maraiyor eththum mayanaththu peedai theera adiyarkku arulum peruman adigal seer nadi naval arooran nambi sonna nal thamizhgal padum adiyar ketpar mel papamana paraiyume." Thiruchitrambalam.

Having worshipped Thirukkadavur Mayanam, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal praised the feet of Amirthakadeswarar, the Lord of Devas, who resides in Thirukkadavur Thiruveerattanam, and the Lord enshrined there. He praised the Lord and sang a Thiruppathigam beginning with 'Podiyar Meniyane'. That is the 28th Thiruppathigam in the 7th Thirumurai for Thirukkadavur Veerattanam, set to the tune of Nattaraga Pann. The first and last verses of this Thiruppathigam are as follows: Thiruchitrambalam. "Podiyar meniyanai purinool orupal poruntha vadiyar moovilai mel valar angaiyin mangaiyodum kadiyar konraiyane kadavur thanul veerattaththu em adikel en amuthe enakku arthunai nee allathe." "Kararum pozhilsoozh kadavur thiruveerattaththul erarum iraiyai thunaiya ezhil navalar kon arooran adiyan adiththondan uraiththa thamil paror eththavallar paralogaththu iruppare." Thiruchitrambalam. We have already contemplated the temple histories of Thirukkadavur and Thirukkadavur Mayanam in the Puranams of Thirunavukkarasu Peruman and Thirugnanasambandha Peruman. Having worshipped thus, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal proceeded to Thiruvazhamburam.

The 147th verse: "He worshipped the feet of the Lord of Thiruvazhamburam, with its mountain-like ramparts, and sang a Thiruppathigam beginning with 'Enakku Ini'. Then he reached Thiruchaikkadu, where the ocean worships with conches, fragrant incense, jewel lamps, pearl flowers, and waves." This is the 147th verse. He worshipped the feet of the Lord enshrined in Thiruvazhamburam, with its mountain-like ramparts, and Sundaramoorthy Swamigal sang a Thiruppathigam beginning with 'Enakku Ini', where words and meaning align. This Thiruppathigam sung by Sundaramoorthy Swamigal for Thiruvazhamburam is the 72nd Thiruppathigam in the 7th Thirumurai, set to the tune of Gandharam Pann. The first and last verses of this Thiruppathigam are as follows: Thiruchitrambalam. "Enakku ini thinaiththanai pugazh idam arinthen panaikkani pazhampadum paravaiyin karaimel enakku iniyavan thamarkku iniyavan ezhumaiyum manakku iniyavan thanathu idam valamburame." "Varum kalamum pala penuthal karungadal irungula pirappartham idam valamburathinai arungulaththu arunthamizhuran vanthondan sol perungulaththavaru pithatruthal perumaiye." Thiruchitrambalam.

Thiruvazhamburam is the 44th shrine on the southern bank of the Kaveri River. We have contemplated the history of this shrine in the Puranam of Thirugnanasambandha Peruman. Having worshipped Thiruvazhamburam, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal proceeded to Thiruchaikkadu. It is a place located on the seashore, where the ocean worships with musical instruments, offering foam and spray as incense, precious gems as lamps, pearls as flowers, and waves as hands. This shrine, called Thiruchaikkadu or Sayavanam, is praised as being equal to Kasi. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal arrived there. He sang and worshipped the Lord there. However, the Thiruppathigam sung by Sundaramoorthy Swamigal for Thiruchaikkadu is not available to us, due to our lack of merit.

The 148th verse: "Having worshipped the Lord in Thiruchaikkadu, and sung a garland of beautiful Tamil verses, the poet-king then worshipped Thiruvengadu, where the Lord burnt the three cities of the enemies, and reached the city of Thirunanippalli." Having worshipped the Lord in Thiruchaikkadu and sung a garland of beautiful Tamil verses, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal proceeded further and worshipped Thiruvengadu, where the Lord burnt the three cities of the enemies. The Thiruppathigam sung by Sundaramoorthy Swamigal for Thiruvengadu is the 6th Thiruppathigam in the 7th Thirumurai, set to the tune of Indalappann. The first and last verses of this Thiruppathigam are as follows: Thiruchitrambalam. "Padankol nagam senni serththi paypuliththol araiyil veekki adangalor oor eriya seeri andru moovarkku arul purintheer madangal anai setru ugandheer manaikalthorum thalai kaiyenthi vidangaragi thirivathiyanne velai soozh venkada neere." "Viriththa vedam othavallar velai soozh venkadu meya viruththanaya vedhan thannai viri pozhil thirunavaluran aruththiyal arooran thondan adiyanketta malai paththum theriththavannam mozhiyavallar semmaiyalar vanvulare." Thiruchitrambalam.

Having worshipped Thiruvengadu, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal received the Lord's grace and proceeded to the city of Thirunanippalli. Let us continue our contemplation. Thiruchitrambalam.

Thiruchitrambalam. Periya Puranam, Part 861, Vampara Vari Vandu Sarukkam, Eyarkon Kalikkaama Nayanar Puranam. The 149th verse.

Chapter 23 - Yeyar Kon Kalikkaama Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Sekkizhar Peruman's Periya Puranam, Part 861, Vampara Vari Vandu Sarukkam, Eyarkon Kalikkaama Nayanar Puranam. The 149th verse. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, having prostrated at the feet of the Lord residing in Thirunanipalli, and having offered a pure and fragrant garland of good Tamil verses, proceeds to worship at Thirusembanpalli and many other abodes of the Lord with the moon-adorned matted locks, before arriving at Thirunintriyur on his singular bull mount. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal prostrates at the feet of the Lord who lovingly resides in Thirunanipalli and offers a sweet, pure, and fragrant garland of Tamil verses, which is a *Thiruppathigam* (a devotional hymn). This *Thiruppathigam* is praised by Sekkizhar Peruman as "Narsamizhin Punitha Narumthodai" (a pure and fragrant garland of good Tamil). The qualities of goodness, sweetness, purity, and fragrance are the special features of this *Thiruppathigam*. These can be individually observed in this *Thiruppathigam*.

The *Thiruppathigam* offered by Sundaramoorthy Swamigal to Thirunanipalli is the 97th *Thiruppathigam* in the 7th *Thirumurai* (collection of hymns), set to the *Panchamam* *pann* (melody). The first and last verses of this *Thiruppathigam* are as follows: Thiruchitrambalam. "Adhiyan Adhiraiyan Ayan Maal Aridarku Ariya Sodhiyan Sol Porulaya Surungaa Marai Naankinaiyum Odhiyan Umbardham Kon Ulakathinul Evvuyirkkum Naadhiyan Namberuman Nannum Oor Nanipalli Adhe." "Kaalamum Naazhigaiyum Nanipalli Manathin Kolamadhayavanai Madhithu Uraippaar Mann Marandhu Vaanor Ulakil Saala Nallam Eythi Thavalogathu Iruppaare." Thiruchitrambalam. In the 9th verse of this *Thiruppathigam*, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal praises Thirugnanasambandha Peruman. "Ena Maruppinadum Ezhil Aamaiyum Poondhu Ukandhu Vaanmadhil Aranam Malaiye Silaiya Valaitthaan Oonamil Kaali Thannul Uyar Gnanasambandharku Andru Gnanam Arul Purindhaan Nannum Oor Nanipalli Adhe." In this 9th verse, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal praises, "Oonamil Kaali Thannul Uyar Gnanasambandharku Andru Gnanam Arul Purindhaan" (He who bestowed knowledge upon the exalted Gnanasambandhar in the flawless Kali age). Having thus praised Thirunanipalli, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal proceeds to Thirusembanpalli and worships the Lord. From Thirusembanpalli, he sets out and visits Thiruvariyalur, Thiruvizhanagar, Thirumayiladuthurai, Thirukancharu, and other holy places, finally arriving at Thirunintriyur. The 450th verse. Sekkizhar Peruman composes this verse, saying, "Nintriyur Meyaarai Neyathal Pukku Irainghi Ondri Anbu Ulluruga Paadiye Ulagu Idar Neenga Paadiya Ezhu Ezhunoorum Andru Sirappithu Ansol Thiruppathigam Arul Seidhaar." Sundaramoorthy Swamigal praises the Lord residing in Thirunintriyur with a *Thiruppathigam*. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, with a heart full of melting love, sings in this *Thiruppathigam*, mentioning the number of *Thiruppathigams* composed by Thirunavukkarasu Peruman, thereby praising Thirunavukkarasu Peruman and composing a verse in this *Thiruppathigam*. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal states that Thirunavukkarasu Peruman, the "Aludaiya Arasugal" (the ruling king), sang 7,700 *Thiruppathigams* for the world to be free from suffering and attain happiness. Sekkizhar Peruman thus illustrates the greatness of this *Thiruppathigam* where Sundaramoorthy Swamigal praises Thirunavukkarasu Peruman. Thirunavukkarasu Peruman always sang for the world to be free from suffering. Thirunavukkarasu Peruman composed *Thiruppathigams* for the world to be eternally free from suffering and to prosper. In the history of the Lord's life, we see how the world was freed from various sufferings on the day Thirunavukkarasu Peruman sang *Thiruppathigams*. Not only on that day, but just as it was then, the "Arasugal" (king) sang for the world to be eternally free from suffering and to prosper. During the time of Thirunavukkarasu Peruman's incarnation, the *Thiruppathigams* brought about the removal of worldly suffering, such as the healing of the son of Appoodhi Adigal from snake venom, the alleviation of famine by receiving *Padikkasu* (coins) at Thiruvizhimizhalai, the opening of the temple doors at Thirumaraikkadu, and the removal of the obstruction by Jains at Thirupazhaiyarai Vaduthazhi, transforming it into a *Vimanam* (temple tower) for the Lord. We have seen all these miraculous deeds in his sacred history. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal states that Thirunavukkarasu Swamigal sang a total of 7,700 *Thiruppathigams*. Therefore, 7,700 *Thiruppathigams* amount to 4,900 *Thiruppathigams*. Nambiandar Nambigal, in his 11th *Thirumurai*, in the work called "Thirunavukkarasar Thiru Ekadasamalai," praises Thirunavukkarasu Peruman as "Pathigam Ezhu Ezhunooru Pagarum Maa Kavi Yogi" (the great poet who sang 7,700 *Thiruppathigams*). In the second verse of the Thirunintriyur *Thiruppathigam*, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal sings, "Ezhu Ezhunooru Iru Panuval Indranaavinuku Araiyan" (the king of tongues who gave 7,700 works). The first verse of such a Thirunintriyur *Thiruppathigam*, and the second and sixth verses that mention the number of *Thiruppathigams* sung by Thirunavukkarasu Peruman, are as follows: The Thirunintriyur *Thiruppathigam* is the 65th *Thiruppathigam* in the 7th *Thirumurai*, set to the *Thakkesi* *pann*. Thiruchitrambalam. "Thiruvum Vanmaiyum Thinthiral Arasum Silandhiyar Seidha Seyini Kandu Maruvu Kosengannan Thanaku Aliththa Vaarthai Kettu Nunmalar Adi Adaindhen Perugu Ponni Vandhu Undhu Panmaniyai Pillai Palganam Pannaiyul Nanni Theruvum Thettriyum Mutramum Patri Thirattum Then Thirunintriyurane." "Anigol Aadai Hamboona Animaalai Amudhu Seidhu Amudhamperu Thandi Inaikkol Ezhu Ezhunooru Irum Panuval Eendra Naavinuku Araiyan Kanaikkol Kannappan Endru Ivar Petra Kaadhal In Arul Aadharithu Adaindhen Thinaikkul Senthamizh Paingizhi Theriyaum Selvam Then Thirunintriyurane." "Kaadhu Potharai Kinnarar Ozhuvai Kadikkum Pannagam Pidipparum Sim Kothin Maadhavar Kuzhuvudan Ketpa Kolavaanizhal Keel Aram Pagara Edham Seidhavar Eythiya Inbam Yaanum Kettu Nin Inaiyadi Adaindhen Needhi Vedhiyar Niraipugazh Ulakinil Nilavu Then Thirunintriyurane." Thiruchitrambalam. This *Thiruppathigam* was composed by Sundaramoorthy Swamigal with the intention that he too came here after hearing about the gracious deeds of Lord Shiva and praising them. In every verse, one or more historical accounts of Lord Shiva's gracious deeds are extensively elaborated upon. This entire *Thiruppathigam* is a very rare *Thiruppathigam* and is worthy of recitation. The last three verses of this *Thiruppathigam* are not available to us. Thirunintriyur is the 19th *Thalam* (sacred place) on the northern bank of the Cauvery. We have already contemplated this sacred history in Appar Peruman's Puranam. Thus, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal offers the *Thiruppathigam*. Now, the 151st verse. "Appathiyil Anbarudan Amarndhu Agazhvaar Igalidathil Seppariya Pugazh Needur Paniyadha Selpozhudhil Oppariya Unarvinaal Ninaithu Aruli Thozhil Uruvaar Mei Porul Van Tamil Maalai Vilambiye Meendu Anaindhaar." Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, having lovingly resided with the devotees in Thirunintriyur, continues his sacred journey. At that time, as he proceeded on his journey without worshipping at Thiruneedur, a place of indescribable fame, he remembered Thiruneedur with unparalleled true awareness. Therefore, to worship Thiruneedur, he returns here and offers a garland of Tamil verses, a *Thiruppathigam*, to Thiruneedur. When he was leaving Thirunintriyur and started to proceed further without visiting the nearby Thiruneedur, an unparalleled true awareness reminded Sundaramoorthy Swamigal about Thiruneedur. This arises from a state of never forgetting Shiva. It is a rule that if there is a Shiva temple on the way, devotees must visit and worship there before proceeding further. To bypass this rule is a mistake, and true awareness itself reveals this. That is the characteristic of true awareness. The rich *Thiruppathigam* that emerged about such a true essence is the *Thiruppathigam* offered by Sundaramoorthy Swamigal to Thiruneedur. Sekkizhar Peruman explains Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's offering of the *Thiruppathigam* to Thiruneedur in the 152nd verse. "Madalaarum Punal Needur Maruvinar Thazh Vanangaadhu Vidaalaame Enum Kaadhal Viruppurum Aththiruppathigam Adaalaaru Suzhappadaiyaar Tham Paadi Adivanangi Udalaaru Mayirp Pulagam Nigappaninndhu Amaigindradhu." This verse states that Sundaramoorthy Swamigal praises the Lord of Thiruneedur with that *Thiruppathigam*, which expresses the desire, "Can one leave without worshipping the feet of the Lord who lovingly resides in Thiruneedur, a place rich in water with abundant flowers?" He sings about the powerful Lord with the discus weapon, prostrates at His feet, and with his entire body covered in goosebumps, he remains in that sacred place. The *Thiruppathigam* offered by Sundaramoorthy Swamigal to Thiruneedur is the 56th *Thiruppathigam* in the 7th *Thirumurai*, set to the *Thakkesi* *pann*. The first and last verses of this *Thiruppathigam* are as follows: Thiruchitrambalam. "Oorvadhu Or Vidai Ondru Udaiyaanai On Nudhal Thani Kan Nudhalaanai Kaaradhaar Karai Maadattraanai Karudhalaar Puram Moondru Eriththaanai Neeril Vaazhai Varaal Kudhikollum Nirai Punal Kazhani Selvam Needur Paarvulaar Paravi Thozhanindra Paramani Paniyadha Vidaalaame." "Peror Aayiramum Udaiyaanai Pesinaal Peridhum Iniyaanai Neerur Vaarsadai Ninmalan Thannai Needur Nindru Ukandha Piranaai Aaruran Adi Kaanbadharku Anbaai Aadharithu Azhaithitta Immaalaai Paarurum Paravi Thozhavallaar Pattharaai Mutthidhaam Peruvaare." Thiruchitrambalam. The Lord of Thiruneedur is Arulsomanatheswarar, Nirthagana Priyar, Gana Nira Sankarar, Bhadrakaleeswarar. The Goddess is Adithya Abhaya Prathambikai, Vedanayaki, Aalala Sundaranayaki, Veyuru Thozhiyamai. We have contemplated the history of Thiruneedur in Thirunavukkarasu Peruman's Puranam. Now, the 153rd and 154th verses. "Angan Inidhu Amarndhu Arulaal Thiru Punkur Anaindhu Irainghi Kongalarum Malarcholai Thiru Kolakka Anaiya Gangai Sadai Karandhavar Thaam Edhirkaatchi Koduttharula Pongu Viruppaal Thozhudhu Thiruppathigam Potrisaippaar Thirugnanasambandhar Thirukkaigalal Otri Perugu Aarvathudan Paada Pingyanar Kandu Irainghi Arulaale Thirutthaalam Aliththapadi Sirappithu Porul Maalai Thiruppathigam Paadiye Potriseidhaar Enbavai Thiruppaadalkal." These verses state that Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, after worshipping at Thiruneedur and receiving the Lord's grace, proceeds to Thirupunkur and worships the Lord there. He then arrives at Thirukolakka, a place surrounded by fragrant flower gardens. At that time, the Lord, who concealed the Ganga in His matted locks, appeared before Sundaramoorthy Swamigal. With overflowing devotion, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal prostrated and sang a *Thiruppathigam* in praise. He also praised the Lord who, seeing Thirugnanasambandhar sing with great enthusiasm, beating time with his hands, graciously bestowed golden cymbals upon him, and sang a *Thiruppathigam* as a garland of true meaning. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, after worshipping at Thiruneedur and receiving the Lord's grace, proceeds to Thirupunkur and worships the Lord. Then, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal arrives at Thirukolakka, which is surrounded by beautiful groves. At that time, the Lord appears before Sundaramoorthy Swamigal outside the town. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal praises the Lord who appeared before him even before he entered the temple at Thirukolakka, and offers a *Thiruppathigam*. The refrain "Kolakkavinil Kandukondene" (I saw Him in Kolakka) appears in every verse of this *Thiruppathigam* dedicated to Thirukolakka. The *Thiruppathigam* offered by Sundaramoorthy Swamigal to Thirukolakka is the 62nd *Thiruppathigam* in the 7th *Thirumurai*, set to the *Thakkesi* *pann*. The first, fifth, eighth, and tenth verses of this *Thiruppathigam* are as follows: Thiruchitrambalam. "Putril Vaazh Aravu Aarthapiranaai Bhoothanaadhanai Paadhame Thozhuvaar Patruvaan Thunai Enaku Elivandha Paavanaasanai Mevariyaanai Muttralaar Thirupuram Oru Moondrum Pondra Vendri Maal Varai Ari Ambaa Kotravil Angai Endhiya Konai Kolakkavinil Kandukondene." "Andru Vandhu Ennai Agalidaththavar Munnaaladhaaga Endru Aavanam Kaatti Nindru Vennai Nallurmisai Ozhiththa Nitthila Thiral Thotthinai Mutthiku Ondrinaan Thanaai Umbar Piranaai Uyarum Val Aranam Keda Seerum Kundravilliyai Melliyaludanai Kolakkavinil Kandukondene." "Naalum Innisaiyaal Thamizh Parappum Gnanasambandhanuku Ulakavar Mun Thaalam Eendhu Avan Paadaluku Iraingum Thanmaiyaalanai En Manakkaruthai Aalum Bhoothangal Paada Nindru Aadum Angananthanai En Ganam Iraingum Kozhili Perungoyil Ullaanai Kolakkavinil Kandukondene." "Kodaramabaiyil Sadaivudai Karumbai Kolakkavul Emmanai Meimmanai Paadar Angudi Adiyavar Virumba Payilum Naaval Aaruran Van Thundan Naadu Irainghi Mun Ariyum Anneriyaal Navindra Pathu Ivai Vilambiya Maandhar Kaadu Arangu Ena Nadam Navindraanpaal Gathiyum Eythuvar Padhi Avarku Adhuve." Thiruchitrambalam. Thirukolakka is the place where the Lord bestowed cymbals upon Thirugnanasambandha Peruman. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal praises and sings this *Thiruppathigam*, a garland of true meaning, extolling the Lord's gracious act of bestowing golden cymbals upon Thirugnanasambandhar, who sang with great enthusiasm, beating time with his hands. The verse that narrates this sacred history is the eighth verse in this *Thiruppathigam*, which begins with "Naalum Innisaiyaal Thamizh Parappum Gnanasambandhanuku." Now, the 155th verse. "Moovaadha Muzhu Mudalaaru Mudhal Kolakka Agandru Thaavaadha Pugazh Sanbai Valam Kondu Thaazhndhu Irainghi Naavaar Mutthamizh Viragar Narpadhangal Paravippoy Mevardham Puram Setraar Kurugaavur Mevuvaar." Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, having received the Lord's grace from Thirukolakka, circumambulates Sanbai, also known as Sirkazhi, a place of undying fame, prostrates, praises the sacred feet of Thirugnanasambandha Nayanar, the master of the three forms of Tamil, and then proceeds to Thirukurugavur, where Lord Shiva resides. We will continue to contemplate. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 24 - Yeyar Kon Kalikkaama Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Sekkizhar Peruman's Periya Puranam, Part 862, Vampara Vari Vandu Sarukkam, Eyarkon Kalikkaama Nayanar Puranam. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, departing from Thirukolakka, arrives near Sirkazhi and circumambulates the sacred place of Sirkazhi without entering it. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal circumambulates the sacred place of Sirkazhi and worships it, fearing to step inside the *Thalam* (sacred place) of Sirkazhi. We contemplated in the *Thaduthatkonda Puranam* (the Puranam of being stopped and taken over) that Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, not wanting to step inside the great and famous sacred place where Thirugnanasambandha Peruman, the *Aludaiya Pillaiyar* (the Lord's child), incarnated, worshipped from the outskirts, and the Lord appeared before him. Similarly, now, because it is the birthplace of the *Pillaiyar* (child), he does not step inside but circumambulates the town from outside, praises the feet of Thirugnanasambandha Peruman, and proceeds further. Thus, he goes and arrives at Thirukurugavur. There, the Lord prepares *Podhisoru* (packed food) and water for Sundaramoorthy Swamigal and his companions, and offers it to them, quenching their thirst and hunger. Sekkizhar Peruman continues to sing about this miraculous event. The verse: "Vetkai Pasiyaal Mozhi Paravaiyar Varum Paangar Needu Thirunudhalla Kaadhal Vara Karuthu Arindhu Thanneerum Podhisorum Kondu Vazhi Saargindrar." This verse states that Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, the husband of Paravai Nachiyar, who has a sweet voice, approaches. Paravai Nachiyar is known for her sweet voice, which is characteristic of high-born women. Her speech naturally carries the quality of a *pann* (melody). Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, her husband, approaches Thirukurugavur with his companions. The Lord realizes that Sundaramoorthy Swamigal is suffering greatly from thirst and hunger. *Neervetkai* means thirst. *Unneerin Vetkai* means the desire for drinking water, which needs to be precisely prepared and consumed. Water, besides being a cause for preparing other food items, also has the special quality of being a food item itself. Thus, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal approaches, suffering from thirst and hunger. Great souls sometimes experience hunger and thirst. It does not always appear in great souls; it appears occasionally. But in others, like us ordinary people, eating is always the profession. That situation does not exist for great souls. Even if hunger and thirst afflict them, great souls do not acknowledge them or give them importance, but continue in their devotion to Shiva. Previously, we saw a similar sacred history when Thirunavukkarasu Peruman was approaching Thirupangeli. Here, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal stands in Shiva's service, disregarding his bodily discomfort. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal did not acknowledge the suffering of hunger and thirst, but stood in Shiva's service. However, the Lord, who is the life of his life, residing within his thoughts, knows all these things. He waits with water and *Podhisoru*. There, just as the *Sivagnana Bodham* (a philosophical text) states, "Kandu Kaattalin" (by showing), in its 11th *Sutra* (aphorism), that the Primeval Being directly experiences what is perceived by souls, and just as it is quoted that "He also experiences their (the souls') matters," the Lord, who is the life of Nambigal's life, knows Nambigal's thirst and hunger. He is *Kannidu Thirunudhalaar* (one with a long, graceful forehead), one whose gracious gaze extends far. Therefore, He prepares water and *Podhisoru* and waits on the path where Sundaramoorthy Swamigal is coming. *Podhisoru* means packed rice. It is rice packed with curries and other food items. It is called *Kattusoru* (tied rice). Thirst is more severe than hunger. Therefore, water is placed first, and hunger second, and accordingly, the verse is arranged as "Thanneerum Podhisorum" (water and packed food). The Lord, assuming a sacred disguise, waits on the path where Nambigal is coming. Thirunavukkarasu Peruman, in his sacred words, has defined the duties of the Lord and the servant as "Than Kadan Adiyenaiyum Thaangudhal En Kadan Pani Seidhu Kidappadhe" (His duty is to support me, my duty is to serve and remain). Here, we see the Lord's duty placed first. The 157th verse. "Venil Oru Veyil Vemmai Thanipadharku Virai Kulir Menpaan Nal Malarthadambolum Pandhar Orupal Amaithe Aan Marai Vedhiyarai Arulvedam Kondirundhar Maanumarum Thirukkaraththaar Vandondar Thamai Paarthu." It is summer, and there is the heat of the sun. To alleviate the heat, the Lord, holding a deer in His sacred hand, assumes the sacred disguise of a Vedic Brahmin, and sets up a *pandhal* (temporary shelter) like a fragrant, cool, soft lotus pond on one side, anticipating the arrival of Sundaramoorthy Swamigal. It is summer, so there is the heat of the sun. To alleviate the heat of walking in the sun and the heat that would arise at the time of resting and eating the *Podhisoru* here, He sets up a cool *pandhal* like a lotus pond or a flower garden. He sets up this *pandhal* by the side of the path where Sundaramoorthy Swamigal is coming, and He, the Lord, assumes the sacred disguise of a Vedic Brahmin and waits there. It is a disguise of grace. The Lord has assumed this disguise to manifest His compassion. This disguise assumed by the Lord is not an illusory form like the disguises of others; it is a sacred form taken as the embodiment of compassion itself. He waits, anticipating Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's arrival. The Lord's action precedes the devotee's action. Just as it is said, "Avan Arulaale Avan Thazh Vanangi" (by His grace, worshipping His feet) and "Aattuviththaal Aaroruvar Aadaadhaare" (who can dance if not made to dance), among the two actions, the action of the soul and the grace of the Lord, the Lord's gracious action occurs first and precedes. Why does the Lord wait for Sundaramoorthy Swamigal? Because Sundaramoorthy Swamigal is a *Van Thondar* (a strong devotee). He is the one whom the Lord graciously accepted as His servant, even though he spoke strongly against the Lord, and the Lord Himself came and took him over. Therefore, it is the duty of the leader to prevent suffering rather than to alleviate the suffering of those who have accepted him as their leader. Just as Sundaramoorthy Swamigal says, "Vanmaigal Pesida Van Thondan Enbadhor Vaazhvu Thandhaar" (He gave me a life as a strong devotee for speaking strongly), and just as Thirunavukkarasu Peruman says, "Theerppadhandro Thalaiyaayavar Kadanavadhu Thaan" (Is it not the duty of the foremost to alleviate suffering?), the Lord thus assumes a sacred disguise and waits to quench Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's thirst and hunger. The 158th verse. "Kurugaavur Amarndharulum Kuzhagar Vazhi Paarthiruppa Thiruvur Thambiraan Thozhar Thiruththondarudan Varuvaar Appandhar Idai Pugundhu Thirumaraiyavar Paal Perugu Aarvam Selaiyirundhaar Sivaayanama Ena Pesi." The Lord, who lovingly resides in Thirukurugavur, the *Kuzhagar* (the youthful one), as mentioned earlier, was watching for the arrival of Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, the *Van Thondar*. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, the friend of the Lord from Thiruvur, would come with his devotees. When he came, he entered the *pandhal* set up by the Lord on the path. There, he approached the Lord, who was seated in the sacred disguise of a Vedic Brahmin, with great enthusiasm, and saying "Sivaya Nama," he sat down. The *pandhal* set up by the Lord was such that those who passed by could not avoid entering it. It was arranged in such a way, by its form, grace, and location, that those who came that way could not but enter that *pandhal*. Just as Appoodhi Nayanar set up a water *pandhal*, and Thirunavukkarasu Peruman, who came that way, entered it, similarly here, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, with his devotees, enters the *pandhal* and sits near the Lord, who is in the disguise of a Brahmin, saying "Sivaya Nama." It is a tradition for great souls, when they meet, to first praise the Lord's name and then begin to speak. This tradition, which Sundaramoorthy Swamigal follows here, unknowingly becomes a praise to the Lord standing before him, and we see it bearing fruit. Therefore, we realize here that all the traditions established by great souls are traditions related to such benefits. Now, the 159th, 160th, and 161st verses. "Aazha Nizhal Keel Irundhaar Avar Thammai Edhirnokki Saala Miga Pasiththeer Ippodhisoru Tharugindren Kaalamini Thaazhaame Kai Kondu Inidhu Arundhi Ela Narum Kulirthanneer Kudithu Izhaipu Theerum Ena Vanthondar Adhu Kettu Marai Munivar Tharum Podhisoru Indru Namaku Edhir Vilakkalaagadhu Endru Isaindharuli Pondhayangu Noolmaarbhar Tharum Podhisoru Adhu Vaangi Sendra Thiruththondarudan Thiruvamudhu Seidharuli Enniraaindha Parisanangal Ellaarum Inidhu Arundha Panniyapin Ammarungu Pasithu Anaindhaargalum Arundha Un Niraindha Aaramudhaai Oru Kaalam Ulavadhe Punniyanaartham Aliththa Podhisoru Pozhindhadha." These three verses describe the event. The Lord, who is seated under the shade of a banyan tree, here sits under the *pandhal*. The Lord, thus seated, addresses Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, saying, "You are very hungry. I will give you this *Podhisoru* that I have. Do not delay any longer. Take it and eat it sweetly, drink this good cool water, and relieve your weariness." The Vedic Brahmin says, "I will give you this *Podhisoru*." The meaning of this is, "I am an *Anaadhi Saivan* (an eternal devotee of Shiva). You are an *Aadhisaivar* (a Brahmin priest of Shiva) who will come by our grace. By this comparison, the food I give is acceptable." Therefore, now, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal also decides that he should accept the *Podhisoru* given by this Brahmin, as the Brahmin said. The Vedic Brahmin says, "Do not delay any longer." It is a compassionate word, expressing concern that if he delays any longer, hunger and the suffering that comes with it will increase even more. There is cool water suitable for that *Podhisoru* meal. Medical science states that cool water is suitable for drinking. Medical science states that only those with illness should consume hot water. Therefore, here, there is cool water suitable for the *Podhisoru*. The Lord says, "Eat the sacred food so that your hunger, thirst, heat from the sun, and fatigue from walking on the path are all relieved." The *Van Thondar* (Sundaramoorthy Swamigal), hearing this, thinks that he should accept this *Podhisoru* given by this Vedic Brahmin, and it is not proper for him to refuse it. He agrees to it. Although it is a tradition for great souls to refuse food offered by strangers on the way for some reason, here, this tradition cannot be applied. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal decides that he cannot refuse the words of the Brahmin. Therefore, by the Lord's gracious gaze and words, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal accepts the *Podhisoru* given by the Lord, who has a chest adorned with a golden sacred thread, and partakes of the sacred food with the devotees who came with him. After all the countless companions have eaten sweetly, those who arrived hungry in that vicinity also eat there. The *Podhisoru* flowed like abundant sweet nectar, never diminishing in any way, given by the virtuous Vedic Brahmin with compassion. Even though the Lord was seated there to give *Podhisoru* and water to Sundaramoorthy Swamigal and his accompanying devotees, those who passed by on the way also came to that *pandhal* and accepted the *Podhisoru* and water. Therefore, the Lord, who preached *Dharma* (righteousness), here demonstrates that these acts of charity are also to be performed. This charity of giving water and food to Nambigal and his companions is a *Pathi Dharma* (duty to the Lord). When it was given to others who came hungry, it became a *Pasu Dharma* (duty to living beings). Since the Lord Himself performed these *Dharmas*, their benefits reached those who accepted this food and water. If the *Podhisoru* and water given by the Lord were one and the same, then all those who ate it, except Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, were not as pure and exalted as Sundaramoorthy Swamigal. Therefore, here we see the Lord's grace bestowed according to the level of maturity of the souls. Like an inexhaustible treasure, that abundant nectar, the *Podhisoru*, and the cool water were consumed by everyone, quenching their thirst and hunger. It was food and water graciously given by the Lord. The Lord gave it to Nambigal, His devotee, who was suffering greatly from thirst and hunger. It also benefited the other companions and those who came there. Therefore, the *Podhisoru* and water that the Lord had prepared for Nambigal also benefited others. In this world, although it is truly the Lord who creates water and food and helps all beings to alleviate hunger, here, the Lord Himself made all those food items into *Podhisoru*, and it was consumed by Nambigal and all those who came as travelers, by their fate and destiny. Now, the 162nd verse. "Sankaranar Thiru Arul Pol Thanneerin Suvai Aarnthu Pongivarum Aadharavaal Avar Naamam Pugazhndhu Ethi Angu Ayarvaal Amarndhu Arugu Anaindhaargalum Thuyila Gangai Sadai Karandhaar Appandharodum Thaam Karandhaar." Just like the grace of Sankaranar, the water was also sweet, and with overflowing love, they praised His name. There, Nambigal, due to fatigue, lay down and slept, and those who had approached nearby also slept. The Lord, who concealed the Ganga in His matted locks, disappeared along with the *pandhal*. Sankaranar means one who brings happiness. Here, the name is significant because He waited on the path, gave *Podhisoru* and water, and removed their fatigue, thus bringing happiness. Just as the Lord's grace is sweet, the water was also sweet. The food consumed was as sweet and pleasant as the Lord's grace. With ever-increasing love, Nambigal praised and glorified the Lord's sacred name, the *Thiru Aindhezhuthu* (the five-letter mantra). There, due to bodily fatigue, Nambigal lay down and slept. The Lord's name, the *Thiru Aindhezhuthu*, is sweet like the juice of sugarcane that has been crushed. This is a sacred saying from *Thevaram*. Earlier, when Sundaramoorthy Swamigal first met the Vedic Brahmin, we saw that he sat down saying "Sivaya Nama." Now, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, unknowingly, praises the name of that Shiva Brahmin. It is the nature of great souls to praise Lord Shiva, considering all good things, no matter who brings them, as Shiva's grace. "Vazhukki Veezhinum Thiruppeyarallaal Matra Naan Ariyen Marumaatram" (Even if I stumble and fall, I know no other word than Your sacred name) is a sacred saying from Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's *Thevaram*. "Naan Marakkinum Sollum Naan Amasivayave" (Even if I forget, my tongue will utter "Nama Sivaya") is also Nambigal's sacred saying. Thus, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, having consumed the food and water while contemplating the Lord's name, lies down there, overcome by the Lord's grace. In reality, it was not the time, period, or place for sleeping. However, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, overcome by the Lord's grace, sleeps thus. As Sundaramoorthy Swamigal and those who had come there slept, Lord Shiva, who concealed the Ganga in His matted locks, disappeared along with the *pandhal*. The 163rd verse. "Siththanilai Thiriyaadha Thirunaavaluur Mannar Atthagudhiyinil Palli Unarndhu Avarai Kaanaamal Itthanai Yaamatra Marandhilen Ena Eduthu Meiththagaiya Thiruppathigam Vilambiye Sendradaindhaar." Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, the king of Thirunavalur, whose mental state never wavers, even while sleeping, remains in a state of *Sivayoga* (union with Shiva), never forgetting Shiva, with a conscious sleep. Even in such a state of *Sivayoga*, he cannot see the Lord unless the Lord reveals Himself. Therefore, while he was sleeping, after a short while, due to divine grace, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal wakes up from his sleep. At that time, he sees that the Vedic Brahmin and the cool *pandhal* have disappeared. He realizes this. He praises the Lord, saying, "Iththanai Yaamatra Marandhilen" (I have not forgotten You for so long), and offers a *Thiruppathigam*. While reciting such a *Thiruppathigam*, he proceeds to the Thirukurugavur temple. The *Thiruppathigam* offered by Sundaramoorthy Swamigal to Thirukurugavur Velladai is the 29th *Thiruppathigam* in the 7th *Thirumurai*. This *Thiruppathigam* is set to the *Nattaragam* *pann*. The first, third, and tenth verses of this *Thiruppathigam* are as follows: Thiruchitrambalam. "Itthanai Yaamatra Marandhilen Emberumaan Pitthare Endru Umaip Pesuvaar Pirarellaam Mutthinai Manithannai Maanikya Mulaiththezhundha Itthanai Kurugaavur Velladai Neeyendre." "Aaviyai Pogaame Thavirthu Ennai Aatkondaay Vaaviyil Kayalpaaya Kulaththidai Madaidhorum Kaaviyum Kuvalaiyum Kamalam Sengazhuneerum Meviya Kurugaavur Velladai Neeyendre." "Paaduvaar Pasitheerppaay Paravuvaar Pinigalivaay Oodu Nankalanaaga Unbaliku Uzhalvone Kaadu Nal Idamaaga Kadiyirul Nadamaadum Vedane Kurugaavur Velladai Neeyandre." "Valamkani Pozhilmalku Vayal Anindhu Azhagaaya Vilanguvoly Kurugaavur Velladai Uraivaanai Ilangilai Aaruran Vanappagai Aval Appan Ulangulir Thamizh Maalai Patthargatku Uraiyaame." Thiruchitrambalam. The 164th verse. "Kurugaavur Amarndharulum Kulaganar Koyilinukku Arugar Pon Gopuraththai Anaindhu Irainghi Ulpukku Varu Kaadhal Kura Valam Kondu Thirumun Vanangi Parugaa In Amudhatthai Kankalaal Paruginaar." Sundaramoorthy Swamigal approaches the temple of the Lord, the *Kulagar* (youthful one), who lovingly resides in Thirukurugavur. He prostrates at the beautiful golden *Gopuram* (temple tower), enters inside, and with overflowing love, circumambulates and prostrates before the Lord. He drinks with his eyes the sweet nectar-like Lord, who has not been drunk before. Sekkizhar Peruman sings, "Parugaa In Amudhatthai Kankalaal Paruginaar" (He drank with his eyes the sweet nectar that had not been drunk before). The Lord is the sweet nectar that has not been drunk before. Earlier, when the Lord appeared before him as a Vedic Brahmin, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal did not recognize Him as the Lord, and therefore, he drank the nectar given by Him as *Podhisoru* (packed food), but he did not drink His grace, recognizing Him as the Lord. Thus, he did not drink at the place of the *pandhal* where He revealed Himself, but here, he enters the Thirukurugavur temple and drinks the Lord with his eyes. Sekkizhar Peruman states that he drank the Lord with his eyes. Just as the gods drink nectar with their mouths and attain joy, here, the Lord is the undrunk sweet nectar. It is the nature of great souls to realize Him through their intellect and attain supreme bliss, seeing Him through their eyes. Therefore, Sekkizhar Peruman states that he drank with his eyes. The 165th verse. "Kannaarndha Innamudhai Kaiyaaru Thozhudhu Irainghi Pannaarndha Thiruppathigam Paadiye Panindhu Ethi Unnaadum Perum Kaadhal Udaiyavar Thaam Puraththu Eythi Nannum Aarva Thondarudan Angu Inidhu Nayandhu Irundhaar." Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, who had an overflowing desire to experience the Lord in the state of *Sivayoga* within his heart, prostrated with folded hands, worshipped the Lord, the sweet nectar that filled his eyes, and sang a *Thiruppathigam* full of *pann* (melody). He then came out of the temple and lovingly resided there with the greatly devoted *Thiruthondars* (devotees). Sundaramoorthy Swamigal was a person with a great desire to unite with the Lord in the state of *Sivayoga* within his heart and revel in the experience of *Sivananda* (bliss of Shiva). We saw in the *Thaduthatkonda Puranam* that he desired to practice *yoga* for many days in succession. "Theduvan Theduvan Thinnena Patri Seridara Aaduvan Aaduvan Aamaaththor Em Adigalai Kooduvan Kooduvan Kutramadhu Atra En Kurippode Endrum Ornana Ornana Ullaththulle Nindra Onporul Endrum Theduvan Theduvan Semmalar Paadhangal Naalthorum Naaduvan Naaduvan Naabiku Melayor Naalveerar Magizhndhu Ule Aaduvan Aaduvan." These are the sacred words of Sundaramoorthy Swamigal from the *Thiruppathigam* of Thiru Aamathur. These serve as internal evidence that Nambigal was a person who, in the state of *Sivayoga*, united with the Lord within his heart and reveled in the experience of *Sivananda*. Thus, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal lovingly resided in Thirukurugavur Velladai with the greatly devoted *Thiruthondars*. We will continue to contemplate. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 25 - Yeyar Kon Kalikkaama Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Sekkizhar Peruman's Periya Puranam, Part 863, Vampara Vari Vandu Sarukkam, Eyarkon Kalikkaama Nayanar Puranam. The 166th verse. "Annaalil Tham Perumaan Arul Kooda Panindhu Agandru Minnaar Senjadai Mudiyar Virumbum Idam Pala Vanangi Kannaadum Eyil Pudai Suzh Kalippaalai Thozhudhu Ethi Then Naavaluur Mannar Thiru Thillai Vandhu Adaindhaar." In those days, after worshipping and receiving the Lord's grace, the king of Thennavalur, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, departed. He worshipped at many places where the Lord with the lightning-like red matted locks resides, and then worshipped and praised Thirukazhippalai, which is surrounded by beautiful walls. Finally, he arrived at Thiruthillai. Having worshipped at Thirukurugavur, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, after receiving the Lord's gracious permission, sets out. He visits sacred places like Then Thirumullaivayil, Thirumahendrapalli, Thirunallur, Perumanam, and others. He then crosses the Kollidam river and proceeds north. However, the *Thiruppathigams* (devotional hymns) associated with these sacred places are not available to us due to our lack of merit. Thirukazhippalai is a sacred place where the Lord resides. This temple was formerly a large temple on the northern bank of the Kollidam river. It was destroyed by the river's floods. Now, a small individual temple has been established in Sivapuri, also known as Thirunelvayil. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal visits and worships at such a Thirukazhippalai. There, he praises the Lord and offers a *Thiruppathigam*. It is the 23rd *Thiruppathigam* in the 7th *Thirumurai*, set to the *Nattaragam* *pann*. The first and last verses of this *Thiruppathigam* are as follows: Thiruchitrambalam. "Sediyen Theevinayil Thadumaara Kandaalum Adiyaan Aavaan Enaadhu Ozhidhal Thagavaame Mudimel Maamathiyum Aravumudan Thuyilum Vadive Thaam Udaiyaar Magizhum Kazhippalai Adhe." "Pazhiser Il Pugazhaan Paraman Parameetti Kazhiyaar Selvamalkum Kazhippalai Meyaanai Thozhuvaan Naavaluurkon Aaruran Urai Thamizh Valuva Maalai Vallaar Vaanor Ulaku Aalbhavaare." Thiruchitrambalam. Thirukazhippalai is the 4th *Thalam* (sacred place) on the northern bank of the Cauvery in the Chola country. We have already contemplated the history of this *Thalam* in Thirunavukkarasu Peruman's Puranam. Thus, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, having worshipped at Kazhippalai, arrives at Thiruthillai, where the Lord resides. Now, this is Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's third visit to Thillai. We contemplated in the *Thaduthatkonda Puranam* that Sundaramoorthy Swamigal first visited Thiruthillai. In the Eyarkon Kalikkaama Nayanar Puranam, we saw that Sundaramoorthy Swamigal had visited Thillai before this. Now, this is Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's third visit to Thillai. The 167th verse. "Seervalarum Thiruthillai Thiruveedhi Panindhu Pugundhu Ervalar Pon Thirumandrul Eduththa Sevadi Irainghi Paarvalara Maraivalarkkum Padhi Adhanil Panindhu Uraivaar Porvalar Meruchilayaar Thiruththinai Maanagar Pugundhaar." Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, having worshipped the sacred streets of Thiruthillai, which flourishes with glory, enters inside. He prostrates at the raised sacred foot in the beautiful golden hall and resides in that sacred place, which fosters Vedic traditions for the prosperity of the world and the growth of the Vedas. From there, after receiving the Lord's gracious permission, he proceeds to Thiruthinai Managar, where the Lord with the bow of Meru resides. During Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's first visit to Thillai, he came to Thiruthillai from Thiruthinainagar, moving eastward. This time, he departs from Thiruthillai and proceeds westward to Thiruthinainagar. This is his second worship at Thiruthinainagar. The *Thiruppathigams* offered by Sundaramoorthy Swamigal during his visit to Thillai and Thiruthinainagar are not available to us. The 268th verse. "Thiruththinai Maanagar Mevum Sivak Kozhundhai Panindhu Poy Niruththanaar Amarndhu Arulum Nirai Padhigal Pala Vanangi Poruththamigum Thiruththondar Potru Thirunaavaluur Karuththil Varum Aadharavaal Kaithozhach Sendru Eythinaar." Sundaramoorthy Swamigal worships the divine flame of Shiva that lovingly resides in Thiruthinainagar. During Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's first visit to Thiruthinainagar, we contemplated the *Thiruppathigam* he offered in the *Thaduthatkonda Puranam*. We also contemplated the special features of the *Thalam* there. The *Thalaviraksham* (sacred tree) is the *Konnai* tree. Thiruthinainagar is now known as Theerthanagiri. It is a *Thalam* where Jambava worshipped and attained blessings. The *Lingam* he worshipped is in the Amman temple. Pachanda Desikar Adheenathu Kumaramaalai Marundhar has composed the *Thalapuranam* (history of the sacred place). Thus, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal worships the divine flame of Shiva that lovingly resides in Thiruthinainagar and then proceeds to worship many other sacred places where the Lord, the blissful dancer, lovingly resides. These are believed to be places like Thiruchopuram, Thirumanikkuzhi, Thirupallavanagaram, Thirumundeeswaram, Thirugunaparaveeswaram, Thiruvadhigai Veerattam, Thiruvaamur Siththavadam, Thirumanam Vandhaputhur, and others. Having visited all these sacred places, he arrives at Thirunavalur, which is praised by the fitting *Thiruthondars*. He arrives at Thirunavalur with the intention of worshipping there with the love that arises in his heart. We know that Thirunavalur is the birthplace of Sundaramoorthy Swamigal. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, who set out from Thirunavalur for his marriage, that is, to marry the daughter of Puthur Sadangavi Sivachariyar, was stopped and taken over by the Lord in Thiruvannainallur. From there, he again worshipped at Thirunavalur and continued his sacred journey. Now, he returns to Thirunavalur. However, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's worship at Thirunavalur now is not due to his affection for it as his birthplace, but rather according to the rule of worshipping all the sacred places that come on the path of pilgrimage. That Thirunavalur is Thirunavalur, which is praised by the *Thiruthondars*. It is the birthplace of Aludaiya Nambigal, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal. Therefore, it is a *Thalam* that is always praised by the devotees of Shiva. Thus, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal approaches Thirunavalur. The 169th verse. "Thirunaavaluur Mannar Sergindrar Ena Kettu Perunaama Padhiyorum Thondargalum Peruvaazhvu Varunaal Endru Alangarithu Vandhu Edhir Kondu Ul Anaiya Serunaagaththu Uripunaindhaar Sezhungoyil Ul Anaindhaar." Hearing the news that Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, the king of Thirunavalur, was coming, the people of the famous town and the devotees, considering it a day of great prosperity, decorated the town and came to welcome him and lead him inside. Those who belong to the town of Thirunavalur and Sundaramoorthy Swamigal have a worldly connection. But the devotees, without such a connection, come with a sense of right in the state of devotion, based on the vital connection between Nambigal and their Lord Shiva. As everyone came to welcome Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, he entered the prosperous temple where the Lord, who wears the skin of a fierce elephant, resides. The 170th verse. "Meviya Aththondarguzhaam Midaindhu Hara Endru Ezhum Osai Moovulagum Poy Olippa Mudhal Munbu Eythi Aaviyinum Adaivudaiyaar Adikkamalaththu Kovalan Eduthu Paadiye Kumbittaar." The crowd of devotees who accompanied Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, with the sound of "Hara Hara" rising, reverberated throughout the three worlds. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, arriving first, prostrated before the lotus feet of the Lord, who is dearer than life itself, and sang a *Thiruppathigam* beginning with "Kovalan." The Lord is the one who bestows a greater benefit than one's own life. This is the meaning of this. Thirunavukkarasu Swamigal has also stated this in the *Adaivu Thiruththandagam*. Thus, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal worships the Lord and offers a *Thiruppathigam* beginning with "Kovalan." This *Thiruppathigam* for Thirunavalur is set to the *Nattaragam* *pann*. It is the 17th *Thiruppathigam* in the 7th *Thirumurai*. The first and last verses of this *Thiruppathigam* are as follows: Thiruchitrambalam. "Kovalan Naanmugan Vaanavar Konam Kuttreval Seyya Mevalar Muppuram Thee Ezhuviththavar Or Ambinnaal Eevalanaar Vennainalluril Vaithu Ena Aalum Konda Naavalanaarku Idamavadhu Nam Thirunaavaluure." "Naadhanuku Ur Namaku Ur Narasinga Munaiyaraiyan Aadharithu Eesanuku Aatseyyum Ur Ani Naavaluur Endru Odha Nal Thakka Vanthondan Aaruran Uraiththa Thamizh Kaadhalithum Katrum Ketpavar Tham Vinaikkattu Arume." Thiruchitrambalam. In every verse of this *Thiruppathigam*, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal praises the greatness of the Lord who took him over in Thiruvannainallur and states that Thirunavalur is the Lord's abode. The Lord of Thirunavalur is Bhakthajaneswarar. The Goddess is Manonmani Amman. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's shrine is a separate temple in the southeast of the outer courtyard. There is also a Vishnu shrine by the name of Varadharajar. To the west of the temple is a small stream called Gomukhi Theertham, and a short distance to the south is the Thirukedila river. The *Thalapuranam* of Thirunavalur has been composed by Thiruvannainallur Rasappu Kavirayar. The 170th and 171st verses. "Nalampugum Appadhiyin Naadi Anpodu Nayandhu Kulampugum Thiruththondar Kuzhaaththodum Inidhu Amarndhu Salampugum Sadai Mudiyar Thaazh Vanangi Arul Petru Polampuri Nool Manimaarbhar Pirapadiyum Thozhappovaar Thandagamaam Thirunaattu Thanividaiyaar Magizhvidangal Thondar Edhir Kondu Anaiya Thozhudhupoy Thuya Nadhi Vandharai Poompuravu Malai Valam Marudham Pala Kadandhe Ennisaiyor Paravu Thirukazhukunrai Eythinaar." Thirunavalur is a sacred place where good things abound. There, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal lovingly resided with the crowds of devotees, and after worshipping the Lord with the matted locks adorned with the Ganga and receiving His grace, he, the one with the chest adorned with the golden sacred thread, proceeds to worship other sacred places. Here, Sekkizhar Peruman refers to the crowd of devotees as "Kulampugum Thiruththondar Kuzhaam" (the crowd of devotees who enhance the lineage). Here, the word "Kulam" refers to the types of services such as *Sariyai* (physical service) and *Kiriyai* (ritualistic service) performed by the devotees, and should not be taken to mean worldly lineage. Thus, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, departing from Thirunavalur, enters the sacred Thondai country. He worships at many places where the unique bull-mounted Lord Shiva resides, with the devotees welcoming him. These are believed to be places like Thiruppuravar Panangattur, Thindeeswaram, Thiru Achirupakkam, Rudrakodi, and others. Among these, Thiruppuravar Panangattur and Thindeeswaram are *Nadunattu Thalangal* (sacred places in the central region). These are the *Thalangal* located on the way from Thirunavalur to Thirukazhukunram. Thus, he visits these *Thalangal* and crosses many rivers, mountains, and fertile lands. He crosses many rivers like Pennaiyaru, Varaha Nadhi, Palaru, and others. He crosses small hills scattered here and there, such as Thindivanam, Senjimalai, Achirupakkam, Thiruvengunram, and others. Having crossed all these, he arrives at Thirukazhukunram. The verse. "Thenaarntha Malarcholai Thirukazhukunraththu Adiyaar Aanaadha Viruppinodum Edhirkolla Adaindharuli Thuya Nan Venmadhi Anindha Sudarkuzhundhai Thozhudhu Irainghi Paanaadum Innisaiyin Thiruppathigam Paadinaar." The devotees of Thirukazhukunram, surrounded by groves with honey-filled flowers, came with unceasing devotion to welcome him. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, having arrived, worshipped and prostrated before the radiant youthful Lord who wears the pure new white moon. He then sang a *Thiruppathigam* with sweet melodies that resonate with the *pann*. The *Thiruppathigam* offered by Sundaramoorthy Swamigal to Thirukazhukunram is the 81st *Thiruppathigam* in the 7th *Thirumurai*, set to the *Nattapadai* *pann*. The first and last verses of this *Thiruppathigam* are as follows: Thiruchitrambalam. "Kondru Seidha Kodumaiyaal Pala Sollave Nindra Paavavinaygal Thaam Pala Neengave Sendru Sendru Thozhumin Devarpiranaana Idam Kandrinodu Pidichuzh Kunrame." "Pallin Vellai Thalaiyan Thaan Payilum Idam Kallin Vellaiyaruviththan Kazhukunrinai Mallin Malku Thiral Thol Uran Vanappinaal Sollal Sollith Thozhuvaaraith Thozhuminkale." Thiruchitrambalam. Thus, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal sang a *Thiruppathigam* at Thirukazhukunram and resided there. We will continue to contemplate. Thiruchitrambalam.

Thiruchitrambalam. Sekkizhar Peruman's Periya Puranam, Part 864, Vampara Vari Vandu Sarukkam, Eyarkon Kalikkaama Nayanar Puranam.

Chapter 26 - Yeyar Kon Kalikkaama Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Sekkizhar Peruman's Periya Puranam, Part 864, Vampara Vari Vandu Sarukkam, Eyarkon Kalikkaama Nayanar Puranam. Having worshipped at Thirukazhukundram, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal proceeds to the sacred place of Thirukachoor. Here, the Lord, to appease Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's hunger, takes the form of a Brahmin, begs for alms from house to house in Thirukachoor, and offers it to Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, thereby satisfying his hunger. This divine and miraculous event is about to unfold, and Sekkizhar Peruman sings about it in verse 174:

"Paadiya appathiyinkan inithu amarndhu panindhu poy Naadiya nalvunarvinodu Thirukachoorthanai nanni Aadaka maamathil pudaisoozh Aalakkoyilin amuthai Koodiya meyyannbu uruka kumbittu puraththu anainthaar."

Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, who had stayed happily for some days in Thirukazhukundram, obtained divine permission from the Lord there and began his pilgrimage. With a good feeling, always seeking Lord Shiva, he reached the sacred place of Thirukachoor. This good feeling was always to seek and see Shiva in his heart, an inner feeling immersed in Shiva Yoga. With his mind absorbed in Shiva Yoga, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal went to the sacred place of Thirukachoor. Therefore, he was in a state of forgetting all worldly events. He was going very quickly to see the Lord, with his mind absorbed in Shiva Yoga. Therefore, while his attendants, who always accompanied Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, came slowly, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal reached Thirukachoor very quickly.

He arrives there. Therefore, all the attendants are coming very slowly and late. Nambigal (Sundaramoorthy Swamigal) goes very quickly, seeing Lord Shiva in a Shiva Yoga state, forgetting the world. He goes and worships the "Amudhu" (nectar) of Aalakkoyil (temple) in Kachoor with melting true devotion. Kachoor is the name of the town, and Aalakkoyil is the name of the temple. It is known as Thirukachoor Aalakkoyil. Sekkizhar Peruman refers to Lord Shiva as the Amudhu of Aalakkoyil. He sings "Aalakkoyilin Amudhu" as a hint to the history where this Lord later mercifully offered Amudhu to Nambigal to relieve his hunger. Sekkizhar Peruman also subtly states that even if it is Aalakkoyil, "Aalam" means poison, but even if he is inside Aalakkoyil, he is still Amudhu.

Having worshipped the Amudhu of Kachoor Aalakkoyil, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal comes out of the temple. Verse 175:

"Anaintharulum avvelai amuthu seyyum pozhuthaka Konarndhu amuthu samaiththu azhikkum parisanum kurukaamai Thanandha pasi varuthaththaal thambiran thiruvayil Punarndha mathil puraththu irundhaar munaippaadi puravalanar."

Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, having worshipped the Lord inside the temple, comes out of the temple. That time, that moment, is the time for taking "Thiru Amudhu" (sacred food). Generally, the time for taking food is midday or noon. After completing morning rituals and worshipping the Lord, one should eat at noon.

It was a good custom of our ancestors not to eat except at noon, and we see that here. This custom, according to medical texts, bestows good health and a long life. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's attendants, who were responsible for bringing and preparing food at that time, had not yet arrived. Sekkizhar Peruman mentions, "Konarndhu amuthu samaiththu alikkum parisanum kurukaamai" (the attendants who bring and prepare food had not arrived). Attendants are those who bring or prepare and offer food. If there is sufficient space and facilities to prepare and offer food, they would do so. If not, they would bring it. Bringing means obtaining food by begging from Brahmin households, as the Lord is about to do now, or bringing the necessary ingredients for preparing food.

Thus, bringing food from Brahmin homes or bringing ingredients for preparing food, or if ingredients and other items are available, preparing and offering the food – these were the duties of Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's attendants. In earlier days, buying cooked rice was not a custom. The trade of rice later emerged and became an evil practice that caused many great harms. We see here that such cooking attendants and servants accompanied Sundaramoorthy Swamigal on his pilgrimage. We saw that thousands of attendants and others accompanied Thirugnanasambandha Peruman, who was Aludaiya Pillaiyar. We also remember here that Thirunavukkarasu Peruman was in a state of asceticism, so no such attendants accompanied him.

Therefore, the attendants who could bring or prepare food had not yet arrived at the temple. They were far away. Due to his steadfastness in Shiva Yoga, the hunger that had previously been kept away from Sundaramoorthy Swamigal now comes to him. It is a worldly truth that "Pasi vandhida paththum parandhu pogum" (when hunger comes, all ten things flee). However, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal was one who had removed hunger from himself due to his steadfastness in Shiva Yoga. Nevertheless, it is true that hunger, for yogis too, occasionally causes distress.

He also sings, "Pasi noy seytha parithaan" (it is the distress caused by the disease of hunger) and "Pasi theerppay" (you will relieve hunger), as we see and remember in Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's Thevaram (hymns). We remember here that the Lord offered "Pothichoru" (food wrapped in a leaf) to Thirunavukkarasu Peruman in Thirupaingeli and to Sundaramoorthy Swamigal in Thirukkurukavoor. Therefore, with such intense hunger, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal was outside the temple wall. After the noon worship, the temple doors would be closed and locked. Due to the rule that those with hunger or other desires, or anyone else, should not stay inside the temple, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal was outside the temple wall, at the temple entrance. He is referred to as "Munaippadi Puravalanar" (the protector of Munaippadi) by Sekkizhar Peruman in this verse.

"Puravalar" means king. Even if he is a king, if there is a rule that hunger should come to him, then he will be subject to that rule, which is implied here. Sekkizhar Peruman shows here the truth that the Lord, who stands as the life of life within all beings, will not allow beings to act unless He feeds the hunger that those beings experience. Sekkizhar Peruman thus shows this truth to the world by implicitly referring to him as "Puravalanar." "Vagutthaan vaguttha vagaiyallal kodi thoguththarkkum thuyththal arithu" (Except for what the Creator has ordained, it is difficult even for those who have accumulated crores to enjoy) is a Kural (from Thirukkural). Here, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, though a king with attendants and all, had to suffer from hunger, which is a divine rule that reveals grace.

Verses 176, 177, and 178 are as follows:

"Vanthonder pasitheerkka malaiyin mel marunthanaar Minthangu venthalaiyodu olindhu oru vetroddu endhi Andrangku vaazhvaar or anthanaraay purappattu senru Anbar mugam nokki arulkura seppuvaar Meyppasiyaal miga varundhi izhaiththu irundheer vetkai vida Ippozhuthai soru irandhu ingku yaan umakku konarhinren Appuram neer akalaathe sirithu pozhuthu amarum ena Seppi avar kachoor manaithorum senru irappaar Venthiruneetru ani thigazha vilangku nool oli thuzhanga Kandavargal manam uruga Kadum pagal podu idum palikku Pundareeka kazhal puvimel porundhu manaithorum pukku Kondu thaam virumbi aatkondavar mun koduvanthaar."

To appease Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's hunger, the Lord, who is the medicine on the mountain in Thirukachoor, decided to act. Medicines on mountains are considered to have excellent qualities. Here, the Lord cures the disease of hunger. Therefore, Sekkizhar Peruman metaphorically refers to the Lord as medicine. Earlier, he referred to him as the Amudhu of Kachoor Aalakkoyil; here, he refers to him as the medicine on the mountain. Thus, the Lord, who is the medicine on the mountain, decided to act. Leaving his white skull (kapala), which is his begging bowl, he takes just an empty begging bowl and appears in the form of a Brahmin in that town of Thirukachoor. Thus appearing, he looks at Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's face and speaks. The Lord, understanding Nambigal's intense hunger and his state, and realizing that food must be brought very quickly, looks at Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's face and says: "You are suffering greatly from real hunger and are exhausted. To quench your thirst (for food), I will now beg for food and bring it to you. Do not go anywhere from here; stay here for a little while." The Lord says this. Hunger is one of the ailments of the body. The Lord, knowing its intense nature, says that he will bring food very quickly, so "you don't have to go anywhere else; I will go and bring food for you." Thus, he goes very quickly. We see that he reveals such compassionate nature.

The Lord, who came in the form of a Brahmin, says, "I will beg for rice and bring it." This indicates the custom of offering rice to Brahmacharis (celibate students) and others in Brahmin households at noon. It is because of this custom that Sundaramoorthy Swamigal agreed to what the Brahmin said without suspicion. Therefore, without further delay, the Lord, in the form of that Brahmin, goes to every Brahmin household in Thirukachoor and begs for alms. With the beauty of white sacred ash shining and the light of the sacred thread swaying, making the hearts of those who saw him melt, the Lord goes to every Brahmin household in Thirukachoor, with his lotus-like sacred feet touching the ground, to beg for alms offered at the harsh midday. He brings that food, which he obtained by begging, and offers it to Sundaramoorthy Swamigal. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal is the one whom the Lord himself came and stopped and took as his own, isn't he? He comes before him with the food he obtained by begging and reveals his divine form. Verse 179:

"Irandhu thaam koduvantha in adisilum kariyum Aranthai tharum pasitheera arunthuveer ena azhippa Perunthagaiya maraiyavar tham perarulin thirampeni Nirandha perum kaathalinaal nerthozhuthu vaanginaar."

That Brahmin gives the sweet rice and curries that he obtained by begging to Sundaramoorthy Swamigal and asks him to eat them to relieve his distressing hunger. He gives the sweet rice and the curries needed for it, and other things necessary for eating. How does he give them? He gives them saying, "Eat them so that your distressing hunger is relieved." He gives them with compassionate and kind words. If an item is given without compassion, no matter how excellent or important or essential it is, noble people will not desire it, nor will it bear fruit. Therefore, Nambigal, who is noble, praises the great compassionate nature of that Brahmin and, with great love arising in his heart, bows before him and receives the food.

"Mugam thirindhu nokka kuzhaiyum virundhu" (a guest who softens when looked at with a changed face) and "Mugaththaan amarndhu inithu nokki agaththaan insoll inithu aram" (to look at with a pleasant face and speak kind words from the heart is indeed righteousness) are truths taught in Thirukkural and other moral texts. Therefore, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, looking at that Brahmin's compassion and praising his compassionate nature, receives the rice with great reverence. The rice brought by that Brahmin, obtained by begging, is what Sundaramoorthy Swamigal receives. He does not receive it to satisfy his hunger, but rather to praise the Brahmin's noble act, thinking how much compassion this Brahmin has shown towards him. This is the implication. Begging for oneself and having others beg for oneself are essentially the same. Therefore, "Irandhum uyir vaazhthal vendil parandhu keduga ulagiyatriyaan" (If one wishes to live by begging, let the creator of the world perish). As per this, our great Acharya, the great Tamil poet Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, did not receive the rice obtained by begging to fill his stomach to satisfy his hunger. This is implied here. Therefore, Sekkizhar Peruman refers to Sundaramoorthy Swamigal as "Perunthagaiyaar" (the noble one). Verse 180:

"Vaangiya aththiru amuthu vanthondar marungku anaintha Oongu thavaththondarudan undu aruli uvandhu iruppa Aangku aruku nindraar pol avar thammai ariyaame Neenginaar epporulum neengatha nilaimaiyinaar maraiyavanar."

As he received it, the "Van Thondar" (Sundaramoorthy Swamigal) partook of that sacred food. At the same time, great devotees arrived there. Therefore, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal partook of that sacred food with those great devotees and was delighted. Devotees should not eat alone, but with other devotees; this is the implication. We have already seen, "Kannutharkku anbarodum vidhimurai theepam endhi mevum in adisilvootta" (to offer sweet food with lamps according to custom, along with devotees). Here, we will see in the Puranam of Siruthondar Nayanar, "Namakku thaniyunna onnaathu eesan adiyaar ippaangu nindraar thamai konarveer" (we cannot eat alone; bring the devotees of the Lord who are here). Therefore, devotees of Shiva always make it a rule to eat with other devotees, not alone.

Therefore, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal partakes of the sacred food with those great devotees. Those great devotees were blessed with the great fortune of eating the rice that the Lord himself, for Nambigal's sake, had obtained by begging from house to house, with his feet aching in the harsh midday, just as Sundaramoorthy Swamigal was eating. That great fortune cannot be obtained without previous penance. As they were partaking of the sacred food and rejoicing, at that very moment, the Brahmin who was standing nearby suddenly disappeared without Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's knowledge. Although he is the all-pervading one who never departs from anything at any time, he concealed that Brahmin form from there. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal could not recognize who he was even when he was standing nearby. Similarly, when he disappeared, he could not see his divine form. It is the Lord's divine power to make himself unrecognizable when present and to reveal himself as the Lord when he disappears. The all-pervading Lord is everywhere, so he never departs from anything. He had previously appeared in a visible form; now he disappeared without showing that form.

Verses 181 and 182:

"Thirunavalur aali sivayogiyaar neenga varunaama maraiyavanaar iraiyavanaar ena mathiththe Perunathach silambu ani sevadi varundha perum pagalkann oru naadi ezhuntharulithu en poruttaam ena uruki Muthuvaay oori enreduththu muthalvanaartham Perungarunai athuvaam ithu endru athisayam vandhu eitha Kanneer mazhai aruvi puthuvaar punalin mayirppulakam puthaiya pathikam pottru isaitthu Mathuvaar ithazhi mudiyaraai paadi magizhndhu vananginaar."

When the Lord, who came in the form of a Shiva Yogi and a Brahmin, disappeared, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, the leader of Thirunavalur, realized that the Brahmin who had come before him was indeed the Lord. His heart melted, thinking, "Did the Lord, whose sacred feet are adorned with great resounding anklets, come walking and suffering in the harsh midday for my sake?" The great resounding anklets of the Lord refer to the sound of "Shuddha Maya" (pure illusion), which is the source of all Vedas and other scriptures. The Gnanasastras (scriptures of wisdom) proclaim, "Thiruchilampoosai olivaliye senru nirutthanai kumbidu endru Undheepar" (worship the Lord by following the sound of the sacred anklets). The history of Kazharitrarivar Nayanar, who daily received the Lord's grace by hearing the sound of the sacred anklets, exists. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal sings a Thiru Pathigam (hymn) with the central theme, "Kazhalum silambum kalikka palikku endru uchcham podhaa oor oor thiriya kandal adiyaaru orukaare" (if one sees him wandering from village to village at noon, with anklets and bells jingling, for alms, devotees will not be distressed). Sekkizhar Peruman, in this verse, refers to it as "Perunathach silambu ani sevadi" (sacred feet adorned with great resounding anklets). The Lord, who has no name or form, took the form of a Brahmin and appeared as the indivisible Lord, seen as divisible. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal wonders at this great grace.
