Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's statement, "I have forgotten Him here," means that he is separated from the Lord of Thiruvarur. He feels "esaravu" (distress) thinking like this. Esaravu means the sorrow that comes from being separated from a precious thing. We remember that there are verses in Thiruvachagam in a section called "Thiru Esaravu." Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, greatly distressed by the separation, deeply remembered the Lord with shining red matted hair, who is the first of the Vedas, and who rejoices in the ever-glorious Thiruvarur. He then sang the Thiruppathikam (hymn) that begins with "Patthimaiyum Adimaiyaiyum Kaividuvaan" (I will abandon devotion and servitude), which is rich in words and meaning, set to the tune of Pazhampanchuram, and descended. This Thiruppathikam, which is full of words, was composed by Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, who remembered the Lord very deeply. The inner meaning of this Thiruppathikam is to experience and express the same meaning in many ways. In this Thiruppathikam, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal sings with melting heart in each verse, "How many days have I been separated from my Lord of Arur?" and "In what way have I been separated?" and "How have I been separated?" This Thiruppathikam, dedicated to the Lord of Thiruvarur, has twelve verses, two more than the usual ten verses in Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's Thiruppathikams. This Thiruppathikam begins with "Patthimaiyum Adimaiyaiyum Kaividuvaan." "Patthimai" (devotion) is an internal state, and "Adimai" (servitude) is an external state. "Kaividuvaan" means "I will let go." This Thiruppathikam was composed by Sundaramoorthy Swamigal with a distressed mind, grieving over his own situation, thinking, "I am here, letting go of that devotion and servitude." This Thiruppathikam belongs to the sacred place of Thiruvarur. It was composed by Sundaramoorthy Swamigal while he was in Thiruvottiyur. It is set to the tune of Pazhampanchuram. It is the 51st Thiruppathikam in the 7th Thirumurai. It is a central jewel in the 7th Thirumurai. The twelve verses of this Thiruppathikam are as follows:

Thiruchitrambalam.
"Aḻukku meykkoṭu uṉ tiruvaṭi aṭaintēṉ atuvum nāṉ paṭaṟpālatu aṉṟu āṉāl
Piḻukkai vāriyum pāl koḷvar aṭikēḷ piḻaippaṉ ākilum tiruvaṭi piḻaiyēṉ
Vaḻukki vīḻiṉum tiruppeyar allāl maṟṟu nāṉ aṟiyēṉ maṟu māṟṟam
Oḻukka eṉ kaṇukku oru maruntu uraiyāy oṟṟiyūr eṉum ūr uṟaivāṉē"

"Kaṭṭaṉēṉ piṟantēṉ uṉakku āḷāy kātal caṅkili kāraṇamāka
Eṭṭiṉāl tikaḻum tirumūrtti eṉ ceyvāṉ aṭiyēṉ eṭuttu uraippēṉ
Peṭṭaṉ ākilum tiruvaṭi piḻaiyēṉ piḻaippaṉākilum tiruvaṭikku aṭimai
Oṭṭiṉēṉ eṉṉai nī ceyvatu ellām oṟṟiyūr eṉum ūr uṟaivāṉē"

"Kaṅkai taṅkiya caṭaivuṭai karumpē kaṭṭiyē palarkkum kaḷai kaṇṇē
Aṅkai nelliyiṉ paḻattu iṭai amutē attā eṉ iṭar ārkku eṭuttu uraippēṉ
Caṅkum ippiyum calañcalam murala vayaṟam muttoṭu poṉmaṇi varaṉṟi
Ōṅkumā kaṭal ōtam vantu ulavum oṟṟiyūr eṉum ūr uṟaivāṉē"

"Īṉṟu koṇṭatu ōr cuṟṟam ōṉṟu aṉṟāl yāvarākil eṉ aṉpu uṭaiyārkaḷ
Tōṉṟa niṉṟu aruḷ ceytu aḻittiṭṭāl colluvārai allātaṉa collāy
Mūṉṟu kaṇ uṭaiyāy aṭiyēṉ kaṇkoḷvatē kaṇakku vaḻakkākil
Ūṉṟukōl eṉakku āvatu oṉṟu aruḷāy oṟṟiyūr eṉum ūr uṟaivāṉē"

"Vaḻitta paṭuvāṉ muyalkiṉṟēṉ uṉṉai pōl eṉṉai pāvikka māṭṭēṉ
Cuḻittalaippaṭṭa nīr atupōla cuḻalkiṉṟēṉ cuḻalkiṉṟatu eṉ uḷḷam
Kaḻittalaippaṭṭa nāyatu pōla oruvaṉ kōlpaṟṟi karakara iḻukkai
Oḻittu nī aruḷ āyiṉa ceyyāy oṟṟiyūr eṉum ūr uṟaivāṉē"

"Māṉai nōkkiyaṟ kaṇvalaippaṭṭu varunti yāṉ uṟṟa valviṉaikku añci
Tēṉaiyāṭiya koṉṟaiyiṉāy uṉ cīlamum kuṇamum cintiyātē
Nāṉum ittaṉai vēṇṭuvatu aṭiyēṉ uyiroṭum nakar narakattu aḻuntāmai
Ūṉam uḷḷaṉa tīrttu aruḷ ceyyāy oṟṟiyūr eṉum ūr uṟaivāṉē"

"Maṟṟu tēvarai niṉaintu uṉai maṟavēṉ neñciṉāru vāḻavu māṭṭēṉ
Peṟṟiruntu peṟātu oḻukiṉṟa pētaiyēṉ piḻaittiṭṭatai aṟiyēṉ
Muṟṟum nī eṉṉai muṉintiṭa aṭiyēṉ kaṭavatu eṉ uṉai nāṉ maṟavaṉēl
Uṟṟa nōy uṟuppiṉi tavirttu aruḷāy oṟṟiyūr eṉum ūr uṟaivāṉē"

"Kūṭiṉāy malai maṅkaiyai niṉaiyāy kaṅkai āyiram mukam uṭaiyāḷai
Cūṭiṉāy eṉṟu colliya pukkal toḻumpaṉēṉukku collalum āmē
Vāṭi nī iruntu eṉ ceyti maṉamē varunti yāṉ uṟṟa valviṉaikku añci
Ūṭiṉāl iṉi āvatu oṉṟu uṇṭē oṟṟiyūr eṉum ūr uṟaivāṉē"

"Makattil pukkatōr caṉi eṉakku āṉāy maintaṉē maṇiyē maṇavāḻā
Akatil peṇṭukaḷ nāṉ oṉṟu coṉṉāl aḻaiyal pō kuruṭā eṉa tariyē
Mukattil kaṇ iḻantu eṅkaṉam vāḻkēṉ mukkaṉā muṟaiyō maṟaivōti
Ukaikkum taṉ kaṭal ōtam vantu ulavum oṟṟiyūr eṉum ūr uṟaivāṉē"

"Ōtam vantu ulavum karaitaṉ mēl oṟṟiyūr urai celvaṉai nāḷum
Ñālattāṉ paravappaṭukiṉṟa nālmaṟai aṅkam ōtiya nāvaṉ
Cīlattāṉ peritum mika valla ciṟuvaṉ vaṉ tuṇṭaṉ ūraṉ uraita pāṭal
Pattu ivai vallavar tām pōy parakati tiṇṇam naṇṇuvar tāmē"

Thiruchitrambalam. In this Thiruppathikam, in the tenth verse, he mentions Paravaiyar, and in the eleventh verse, Sangiliyar, in a way that shows his sorrow, which is pitiable. Even though he was in Thiruvottiyur, the memory of Thiruvarur never left his heart. This Thiruppathikam was composed with a distressed mind, grieving, "I have forgotten the Lord of Thiruvarur here! How many days have I been separated?" This Thiruppathikam not only reveals Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's inner thoughts but also contains many internal evidences of Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's history. In the tenth and eleventh verses, the stories of his marriages to Paravai Nachiyar and Sangili Nachiyar, according to the Lord's command, are obtained. In the tenth verse, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal sings, "You are the seven notes, the fruit of music, the sweet nectar, my friend, and you are with me in all the wrongs I do." "Thurisugal" (wrongs) means faults. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal sings that the Lord is omnipresent, and therefore, He is present even in wrongs. The Lord blessed him, saying, "Play on earth with the form of a mental sacrifice." The Lord appeared under the Magizha tree as he wished. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal sings, "You are with me in all the wrongs I do," keeping in mind these and other blessings. In the twelfth verse, he sings, "I greatly desire the service of Lord Shiva of Thiruvarur." Even without seeing the Lord of Thiruvarur directly, he sings this, greatly remembering that servile devotion in his mind. Although this Thiruppathikam is about Thiruvarur, it was not composed there, but in Thiruvottiyur, remembering Thiruvarur deeply. This is an internal evidence for this. This Thiruppathikam is very moving, with profound meaning and musical harmony, and is also very memorable. Thus, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal composed this Thiruppathikam. We will continue to contemplate. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 42 - Yeyar Kon Kalikkaama Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periya Puranam, Part 880, Vampara Vari Vandu Sarukkam, Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar Puranam. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, while in Thiruvottiyur, remembers the Lord of Thiruvarur and composes a Thiruppathikam. The 274th verse:

"Piṉ oru nāḷ tiruvārūr taṉṉai peruka niṉaintu aruḷi
Uṉṉa iṉiyār kōyil pukuntu iṟaiñci oṟṟi nakar taṉṉai
Akala pukkār tām ceyta capatattāl muṉ aṭikaḷ tōṉṟātu
Kaṇmaṟaiya mūrcittār eṉpatu tiruppāṭal"

Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, who had been thinking of the Lord of Thiruvarur, one day thought deeply of the Lord of Thiruvarur and decided to go to Thiruvarur to worship Him. He entered the Adhipureeswarar temple in Thiruvottiyur, worshipped the Lord, and then set out from Thiruvottiyur to go to Thiruvarur. As he stepped out of Thiruvottiyur towards Thiruvarur, due to the vow he had made, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's eyesight disappeared, and he fainted. He bid farewell to the Lord of Ottiyur by worshipping inside the temple, then left and stepped across the boundary of Thiruvottiyur. At that moment, his eyesight disappeared unexpectedly, due to the vow he had made to Sangiliyamman under the Magizha tree, that he would not leave her. This was a sudden misfortune. Therefore, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal fainted and became somewhat unconscious. The 275th, 276th, 277th, and 278th verses:

"Ceyvataṉai aṟiyātu tikaittu aruḷi neṭuyirppār
Maiviravu kaṇṇārpāl cūḷuṟavu maṟutta ataṉāl
Ivviṉai vantu eytiyatām eṉa niṉaintu emperumāṉai
Eytiya ittuyar nīṅka pāṭuvēṉ eṉa niṉaintu
Aḻukku meykkoṭu eṉṟu eṭutta col patikam
Āti nīḻpuṟi aṇṇalai ōti vaḻuttu neñcoṭu
Tāḻntu niṉṟu uraippār mātorpākaṉār malarpata muṉṉi
Iḻukku nīkkiṭa vēṇṭum eṉṟu iṟantē eytu ventuyar
Kaiyaṟaviṉukkum paḻikkum velki nal icai koṭu paravi
Paṇintu cālavum pala pala niṉaivār aṅku nātar cey
Aruḷatuvāka aṅkai kūppi āṟūr toḻa niṉaintē
Poṅkukātal mīḷā nilaimaiyiṉāl pōtuvār vaḻikāṭṭa
Muṉpōntu tiṅkaḷvēṇiyār tiru mullai vāyil ceṉṟu iṟaiñci
Nīṭiya tiruppatikam caṅkilikkāka eṉ kaṉai maṟaittīr eṉṟu
Cāṟṟiya taṉmaiyil pāṭi toṇṭaimāṉukku aṉṟu aruḷ koṭuttu
Aruḷum tollai vaṇpukaḻ mullai nāyakaraik koṇṭa
Veṇṭuyar kaḷaika eṉa paravi kuṟitta kātaliṉ
Neṟikoḷa varuvār vaṇṭulā malarcōlaikaḷ cūḻntu
Māṭamāḷikai nīṭu veṇpākkam kaṇṭa toṇṭarkaḷ
Etirkoḷḷu vaṇaṅki kāyunākattaṟ kōyilai aṭaintār eṉpavaikal tiruppāṭalkaḷ"

Suddenly, his eyesight disappeared, and Sundaramoorthy Swamigal was bewildered, not knowing what to do, and sighed deeply. He thought that this misfortune had come upon him because he had broken the oath he made to Sangiliyar. He decided to sing to the Lord, praying for this suffering to be removed. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal thought that his eyesight had disappeared because he had broken the vow. At this point, Sivakavimani Desikanar writes as follows: "If one makes a vow and then acts contrary to it, will their eyesight disappear? Even in these days, people swear, 'I swear it is the truth, the whole truth, and nothing but the truth, so help me God,' and then they tell lies, give false testimony without any truth mixed in, and break their vows. But no harm comes to them. They live happily without any trouble. In this age, who would believe the story that eyesight disappeared because a vow was broken?" Many people doubt and mock, saying this. Those people are unaware of the nature of time, their own state, and the laws of karma. The manifestation of truth changes from age to age; this is the conclusion of the Gnana Sastras (scriptures of wisdom). We also see this in experience. This is why it is said that Dharma Devata (the deity of righteousness) walks on four legs in the first age, three legs in the second age, two legs in the third age, and one leg in the fourth age. In this Kali Yuga (the fourth age), the manifestation of truth will happen slowly, according to the nature of this age. But no matter what, it will happen; it will not fail. Seeing that the manifestation of truth does not happen immediately, one should not conclude that there is no place for truth here.

The fact that the manifestation of truth changes according to the Yuga Dharma (the dharma of the age) is stated in many scriptures. Thiruvilaiyadal Puranam, Rasavatha Padalam, states, "His golden body changed, and he took a suitable form for the people of the fourth age of falsehood." Many other scriptures also mention this. If one asks, "Does the state of truth change according to time?" the answer is that the state in which truth manifests changes, but it never becomes false. The seasons that come in a year, the small periods that come in a day, and so on, are divisions of time, and according to them, the efforts, discipline, etc., of living beings in the world are regulated. We see with our own eyes that these change according to time. Time is a Tattva (principle); it is one of the seven Vidya Tattvas. Therefore, the state in which truth manifests changes according to time, but it never becomes false; this is the rule. Sivakavimani Desikanar also writes about some incidents he experienced. The commentator was also a lawyer in Coimbatore in those days; this is his history. So he gives examples and truths he has seen. A rich man gave false testimony in a criminal court. As soon as he got down from the witness stand, his eyesight disappeared. He went home with the help of others, groping with his hands and feet. His eyesight remained lost until he died. He mentions this incident. He also mentions another incident. Similarly, a rich man gave false testimony in a civil court. From that day on, he fell ill and died within a month. There are many such incidents. Therefore, our ancestors, having seen the truth, advise us not to act too boldly, thinking that the consequences of breaking a vow will not happen immediately. They tell us to live according to the path of truth and be saved. Now, the next part of this verse: Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, realizing that his eyesight had disappeared because he had broken the vow, says that he will sing to the Lord to remove this suffering. The verse says, "I will sing to the Lord for this suffering to be removed." Now, another question arises: "If one does bad things and then suffers the consequences, will singing to the Lord remove it? If so, then these principles give room for those who do evil to do evil, and then pray to the Lord, sing praises, and escape the consequences of their evil deeds." Some people may raise such questions. Engaging living beings in evil deeds is an act of ignorance, which is the darkness of "aanavam" (ego). It is an evil act. Seeking a solution for it, realizing that it is an evil act, is an act of knowledge that living beings acquire when the Lord reveals it. Therefore, it is a rule to seek solutions for all the evil deeds one has done with the help of that knowledge. Just as a person who lives without adhering to the rules prescribed by scriptures, and thus falls ill, seeks a doctor and tries to recover from the illness with the doctor's help, here, singing to the Lord is a medicine to remove the suffering caused by the evil deeds one has committed. This is the truth stated in the Gnana Sastras. Just as a doctor, when truthfully informed about the disease and its history, gives medicine accordingly, so too the Lord, who is the physician of karma, will cure the disease of karma by prescribing the appropriate time and other factors for experiencing and exhausting it. Therefore, remembering the Lord and praising the Lord are the prescribed stages and remedies for the solution of that evil deed. It is true that the disease of karma cannot be exhausted without experiencing it. If people realize their faults, feel remorse, and truly melt and pray to the Lord, the Lord, out of compassion, will remove their suffering. Some people research this. In fact, forgiveness of sins is not a principle of Saivism. It is a principle of external religions like Christianity. It is these principles that encourage people to engage in evil deeds. But the truth discovered by Saivism is that if one remembers and praises the Lord, it acts as a medicine for that disease, and the Lord manifests within it and bestows grace for the ripening of karma in living beings. He will exhaust the consequences of that sin as physical karma, or He will make them experience and exhaust that suffering quickly, or He will make them experience and exhaust that suffering without even knowing that they are experiencing suffering. Therefore, in some way, the Lord bestows grace only after making living beings experience the sufferings appropriate to the karma they have done. This is the principle of Saivism. The Thiruppasuram states, "The way He bestows grace upon His devotees and His prime greatness are immeasurable if one tries to describe them. One need not speak. The principles and actions do not approach. Those who bow down and stand firm are worthy of hearing these." The Thiruvakku of Sivagnana Bodham is, "It is the duty of the leader to protect those who seek refuge." Therefore, the karma of those who worship the Lord will be destroyed in the way mentioned above, and not by the principle of forgiveness of sins. We must understand this. Even in the Thiruppathikams that came from our Paramacharyas, as benefits of the Thiruppathikam, it is said that the karma of those who sing this Thiruppathikam and worship the Lord will be destroyed or will flee. All these mean that the Lord's nature of bestowing grace upon living beings is as explained above. In the upcoming historical accounts, wherever such instances occur, we must understand the histories by keeping this truth in mind. Therefore, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, praying to the Lord and thinking, "I will remove this suffering; I will sing to the Lord for this suffering to be removed," composes a Thiruppathikam remembering the Lord of Ottiyur. We will continue to contemplate. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 43 - Yeyar Kon Kalikkaama Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periya Puranam, Part 881, Vampara Vari Vandu Sarukkam, Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar Puranam. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal praises the Lord of Thiruvottiyur and sings the Thiruppathikam that begins with "Azhukku Meykodu" (With a soiled body). He sings this Thiruppathikam with a humble heart, praising and worshipping the primal Lord of Thiruvottiyur. He prays and appeals to the Lord's lotus-like feet, asking for the removal of the fault that has befallen him. He is in a state where he has lost his support, and since he has lost his eyesight, he needs the help of others to protect him and guide him. He grieves, thinking, "I lost my eyesight because I broke the vow I made to Sangiliyar, and now people are saying many bad things about me!" He remembers the Lord and sings the Thiruppathikam that begins with "Azhukku Meykodu." This Thiruppathikam belongs to the sacred place of Thiruvottiyur. It is the 54th Thiruppathikam in the 7th Thirumurai, set to the tune of Thakkēsi. This Thiruppathikam is as follows:

Thiruchitrambalam.
"Aḻukku meykkoṭu uṉ tiruvaṭi aṭaintēṉ atuvum nāṉ paṭaṟpālatu aṉṟu āṉāl
Piḻukkai vāriyum pāl koḷvar aṭikēḷ piḻaippaṉ ākilum tiruvaṭi piḻaiyēṉ
Vaḻukki vīḻiṉum tiruppeyar allāl maṟṟu nāṉ aṟiyēṉ maṟu māṟṟam
Oḻukka eṉ kaṇukku oru maruntu uraiyāy oṟṟiyūr eṉum ūr uṟaivāṉē"

"Kaṭṭaṉēṉ piṟantēṉ uṉakku āḷāy kātal caṅkili kāraṇamāka
Eṭṭiṉāl tikaḻum tirumūrtti eṉ ceyvāṉ aṭiyēṉ eṭuttu uraippēṉ
Peṭṭaṉ ākilum tiruvaṭi piḻaiyēṉ piḻaippaṉākilum tiruvaṭikku aṭimai
Oṭṭiṉēṉ eṉṉai nī ceyvatu ellām oṟṟiyūr eṉum ūr uṟaivāṉē"

"Kaṅkai taṅkiya caṭaivuṭai karumpē kaṭṭiyē palarkkum kaḷai kaṇṇē
Aṅkai nelliyiṉ paḻattu iṭai amutē attā eṉ iṭar ārkku eṭuttu uraippēṉ
Caṅkum ippiyum calañcalam murala vayaṟam muttoṭu poṉmaṇi varaṉṟi
Ōṅkumā kaṭal ōtam vantu ulavum oṟṟiyūr eṉum ūr uṟaivāṉē"

"Īṉṟu koṇṭatu ōr cuṟṟam ōṉṟu aṉṟāl yāvarākil eṉ aṉpu uṭaiyārkaḷ
Tōṉṟa niṉṟu aruḷ ceytu aḻittiṭṭāl colluvārai allātaṉa collāy
Mūṉṟu kaṇ uṭaiyāy aṭiyēṉ kaṇkoḷvatē kaṇakku vaḻakkākil
Ūṉṟukōl eṉakku āvatu oṉṟu aruḷāy oṟṟiyūr eṉum ūr uṟaivāṉē"

"Vaḻitta paṭuvāṉ muyalkiṉṟēṉ uṉṉai pōl eṉṉai pāvikka māṭṭēṉ
Cuḻittalaippaṭṭa nīr atupōla cuḻalkiṉṟēṉ cuḻalkiṉṟatu eṉ uḷḷam
Kaḻittalaippaṭṭa nāyatu pōla oruvaṉ kōlpaṟṟi karakara iḻukkai
Oḻittu nī aruḷ āyiṉa ceyyāy oṟṟiyūr eṉum ūr uṟaivāṉē"

"Māṉai nōkkiyaṟ kaṇvalaippaṭṭu varunti yāṉ uṟṟa valviṉaikku añci
Tēṉaiyāṭiya koṉṟaiyiṉāy uṉ cīlamum kuṇamum cintiyātē
Nāṉum ittaṉai vēṇṭuvatu aṭiyēṉ uyiroṭum nakar narakattu aḻuntāmai
Ūṉam uḷḷaṉa tīrttu aruḷ ceyyāy oṟṟiyūr eṉum ūr uṟaivāṉē"

"Maṟṟu tēvarai niṉaintu uṉai maṟavēṉ neñciṉāru vāḻavu māṭṭēṉ
Peṟṟiruntu peṟātu oḻukiṉṟa pētaiyēṉ piḻaittiṭṭatai aṟiyēṉ
Muṟṟum nī eṉṉai muṉintiṭa aṭiyēṉ kaṭavatu eṉ uṉai nāṉ maṟavaṉēl
Uṟṟa nōy uṟuppiṉi tavirttu aruḷāy oṟṟiyūr eṉum ūr uṟaivāṉē"

"Kūṭiṉāy malai maṅkaiyai niṉaiyāy kaṅkai āyiram mukam uṭaiyāḷai
Cūṭiṉāy eṉṟu colliya pukkal toḻumpaṉēṉukku collalum āmē
Vāṭi nī iruntu eṉ ceyti maṉamē varunti yāṉ uṟṟa valviṉaikku añci
Ūṭiṉāl iṉi āvatu oṉṟu uṇṭē oṟṟiyūr eṉum ūr uṟaivāṉē"

"Makattil pukkatōr caṉi eṉakku āṉāy maintaṉē maṇiyē maṇavāḻā
Akatil peṇṭukaḷ nāṉ oṉṟu coṉṉāl aḻaiyal pō kuruṭā eṉa tariyē
Mukattil kaṇ iḻantu eṅkaṉam vāḻkēṉ mukkaṉā muṟaiyō maṟaivōti
Ukaikkum taṉ kaṭal ōtam vantu ulavum oṟṟiyūr eṉum ūr uṟaivāṉē"

"Ōtam vantu ulavum karaitaṉ mēl oṟṟiyūr urai celvaṉai nāḷum
Ñālattāṉ paravappaṭukiṉṟa nālmaṟai aṅkam ōtiya nāvaṉ
Cīlattāṉ peritum mika valla ciṟuvaṉ vaṉ tuṇṭaṉ ūraṉ uraita pāṭal
Pattu ivai vallavar tām pōy parakati tiṇṇam naṇṇuvar tāmē"

Thiruchitrambalam. This Thiruppathikam, sung by Sundaramoorthy Swamigal to the Lord of Thiruvottiyur, is very moving. In this Thiruppathikam, the phrases "Even if I am a sinner, I will not abandon your feet, O Lord, who accepts even the dung and milk," and "O three-eyed one, if my eyes are to be counted as a debt, then grant me a staff," and "Like a dog dragged by a stick, remove my suffering and do what is good for me," and "O Saturn who entered my Magam (birth star), how can I live with blind eyes, O three-eyed one, is this justice?" are parts that bring tears to our eyes. This Thiruppathikam was sung by Sundaramoorthy Swamigal as he left Thiruvottiyur and proceeded towards Thiruvarur, with an unceending love for Thiruvarur. He sings, "With a soiled body, I have reached your feet." "Azhukku" (soiled) refers to the state of impurity, full of dirt like falsehood, not only in the inner faculties but also external impurities. It also refers to the body itself being impure. It signifies the need to think of Lord Shiva in a way that all these impurities should be washed away. The phrase "Piḻukkai vāriyum pāl koḷvar" (They will take the milk even if it has dung in it) is a proverb. Those who raise goats, when they milk the goats, sometimes goat dung falls into the milk. But they remove it from the milk and keep the milk. Similarly, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal sings to the Lord, "Why don't you purify me by removing my impurities and accept only my loving services? You should accept them in that way." At the same time, he sings to the Lord, "Even if I err, I will not abandon your feet; even if I stumble, I have never strayed from my love for your feet." He further sings in the Thirumullaivayil Thiruppathikam, "I have done many excesses, acting disrespectfully and stubbornly, like a madman." Therefore, these are not Shiva offences. These are merely worldly interactions that disappear. True Shiva offences are those for which there is no atonement. Therefore, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal says here, "I will not abandon your feet." He means, "I have not committed any Shiva offence. Even if I have erred in worldly matters, I have not committed any error in loving your feet." The merits that exist are building ponds, digging wells, providing water shelters, giving medicine, etc. These are "pasu punniyangal" (merits related to the soul). "Pathi punniyangal" (merits related to the Lord) are those that gradually lead to Shivam (liberation) by remembering Shiva. These Pathi punniyangal ultimately lead to moksha (salvation), which is attaining the Lord's feet. Pasu punniyangal, however, lead to enjoyments in heaven and other realms, and without remembering Shiva, they lead to repeated births. Therefore, one must understand Pathi punniyangal and Pasu punniyangal very well, understand the difference between them, and perform Pathi punniyangal to end rebirth. Pathi punniyangal should not be performed on par with Pasu punniyangal. Therefore, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal says, "Even if I stumble, I do not know any other name than your sacred name. Tell me a medicine for my eyes to see." Therefore, it is a good habit to always remember and utter the name of Shiva without ceasing, no matter what joys or sorrows come. Practicing this will bring great benefit. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's Thiruppandi Kodumudi Thiruppathikam states, "Even if I forget you, O Lord, I will utter 'Namassivaya' (Hail Shiva)." But one should not think that all this will come easily. It does not come easily. It comes as a result of penance and long practice. He continues to sing in the next verse, "Even if I am a liar, I will not abandon your feet." "Pettan" means liar. In the third verse, he says, "You are the nectar in the fruit of the gooseberry in the palm of my hand." This means that just as the parts of a gooseberry, from the outer part of the fruit to the seed inside, are clearly visible when placed in the palm of the hand, so too the Lord reveals His grace to those who see Him. The Thiruvachagam states, "The gooseberry in the palm of my hand." Therefore, no matter who they are, if they surrender themselves to Him with love, He will not speak words that will cause them suffering. "O Lord, who has three eyes, you have made me lose the sight of my two eyes! Is this your grace? You have hidden my eyesight. Now, at least give me a staff." Sundaramoorthy Swamigal asked for a staff, unable to even find one for himself. This is a truth we can see here. Isn't it a proverb that "the God who gave eyes will give a staff"? Therefore, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, relying on the Lord's grace, prays, "I will not even try to find a small staff for myself; you yourself must grant it." We are about to see that the Lord grants the staff in Thiruvenpakkam. "Suzhithalaippatta Neer" (water caught in a whirlpool) means water in a whirlpool. Just as it spins without stability, I am confused and spinning, and my mind is also spinning in confusion. I am like a dog tied to a stick and dragged. Living with lost eyesight is like living in hell. Even though I had received your grace before, I am a fool who has not received that grace now and has lost my eyesight. I do not know what great mistake I made to deserve this loss of eyesight. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal sings all this. He sings, grieving that he lost his eyesight because he forgot the vow. But even now, he sings, "I don't know what mistake I made to lose my eyesight to this extent." This is because the suffering that has come upon him now, even though he never forgets the Lord's feet, is a suffering that is disproportionate to his actions, as Sundaramoorthy Swamigal thought in his sacred heart. We also see that he appeals for its removal. He addresses the Lord as "Makathil Pukkatōr Sani Enakku Aanay" (You have become like Saturn entering my Magam star). Astrological texts state that the period when Saturn transits the Magam star is a time of great suffering. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal appeals to the Lord, saying that suffering like that has come upon him. Thus, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal composes this Thiruppathikam with various beautiful analogies, very movingly. Moreover, from the time he left Thiruvottiyur until he reached Thiruvarur, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's sacred history is a heart-wrenching one, and the Thiruppathikams he composed are heart-wrenching. After crossing the boundary of Thiruvottiyur, Nambigal (Sundaramoorthy Swamigal), who had lost his eyesight, proceeds to Thiruvarur from there. On the way, he reaches the sacred place of Thirumullaivayil and prays, "Remove my suffering, O Lord." In the same state, he goes to Thiruvenpakkam and appeals to the Lord. The Lord there grants Sundaramoorthy Swamigal a staff and says, "We are here; you may go." After that, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal worships at Thiruvallangadu and Thiruvooral, and then goes to Thirukachiyekambam and worships. There, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal regains the sight of his left eye. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal then leaves Kanchi, worships at Thiruaamathur, Thirunelvayil Arathurai, and Thiruavaduthurai, and then worships at Thiruthuruthi, where he is cured of the skin disease on his body. Then he goes to Thiruvarur and worships, and also regains the sight of his right eye. The Thiruppathikams he composed at these times are very moving. They are Thiruppathikams that are rich in Tamil, containing the essence of sorrow. They are Thiruppathikams that are excellent in word, meaning, and composition. They are Thiruppathikams that serve as excellent internal evidence for Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's sacred history. We mostly see that these Thiruppathikams are set to the tune of Thakkēsi. We see that the Thiruthandagam Thiruppathikams in Thirunavukkarasar Peruman's Thiruppathikams, the Thiruppathikams set to the tune of Megaraga Kurinji in Pillaiyar's Thiruppathikams, and the Thiruppathikams set to the tune of Thakkēsi in Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's Thiruppathikams are greatly excellent in moving the hearts of anyone. We hope that devotees will study these deeply and benefit. Sekkizhar Peruman, taking Nambigal's sacred heart as his own, explains this history in this section in 38 verses, from the 274th verse to the 311th verse. Thus, Sekkizhar Peruman arranges it. Therefore, we will continue to contemplate the upcoming historical sections. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 44 - Yeyar Kon Kalikkaama Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periya Puranam, Part 882, Vampara Vari Vandu Sarukkam, Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar Puranam. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal departs from Thiruvottiyur, praising and worshipping the Lord, and continues to sing Thiruppathikams. Even though Nambigal praised and appealed to the Lord of Thiruvottiyur, the Lord remained silent. Even the state where the Lord does not bestow grace is indeed grace. Sivagnana Siddhiyar states this: "All that the Lord does is grace in every state." Due to his love for worshipping Thiruvarur, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal does not think of returning to Thiruvottiyur, and without minding his lost eyesight, he continues to proceed towards Thiruvarur. On the way, he reaches the sacred place of Thirumullaivayil, near Chennai. His attendants guide Sundaramoorthy Swamigal by holding his hand, and Sundaramoorthy Swamigal reaches Thirumullaivayil. There, he worships the Lord and composes a Thiruppathikam, expressing how his eyes were hidden for Sangili. In that Thiruppathikam, composed at the sacred place of Vadathirumullaivayil, the history of that sacred place mentions how the Lord of Thirumullaivayil had bestowed grace upon Thondaiman in the past. He praises that ancient generosity and prays to the Lord of Thirumullaivayil to remove his severe suffering. "Aṭiyēṉ paṭutuyar kaḷaiyāy pācupatā parañcuṭarē" (Remove my suffering, O Pasupatha, Supreme Light) is the refrain that appears in the Thiruppathikam. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal sings this in every verse of the Thiruppathikam.

Thus, the Thiruppathikam composed by Sundaramoorthy Swamigal for the sacred place of Vadathirumullaivayil is the 69th Thiruppathikam in the 7th Thirumurai, set to the tune of Thakkēsi. The verses of this Thiruppathikam are as follows:

Thiruchitrambalam.
"Tiruvum meypporuḷum celvamum eṉakku uṉ cīrvuṭaikkaḻalkaḷ eṉṟueṇṇi
Oruvarai matiyātu urāmaikaḷ ceytum ūṭiyum uraippaṉāytirivēṉ
Murukamarcōlai cūḻ tirumullaivāyilāy vāyiṉāl uṉṉai paraviṭum aṭiyēṉ paṭutuyar kaḷaiyāy pācupatā parañcuṭarē"

"Kūṭiya iḷaiyam cati piḻaiyāmai koṭiyiṭai umaiyavaḻkāṇa
Āṭiya aḻakā arumaṟai poruḷē aṅkaṉā eṅkuvuṟṟāy eṉṟu
Tēṭiya vāṉōr cēr tirumullaivāyilāy tiruppukaḻ viruppāl
Pāṭiya aṭiyēṉ paṭutuyar kaḷaiyāy pācupatā parañcuṭarē"

"Viṇpaṉiṉ ēttum vētiyā mātar veṟuviṭa vēḻum aṉṟu urittāy
Ceṇpakac cōlai cūḻ tirumullaivāyilāy tēvartam aracē taṉ poḻil oṟṟi
Mānakar uṭaiyāy caṅkilikkā eṉ kaṇkoṇṭa paṇpaṉiṉ aṭiyēṉ paṭutuyar kaḷaiyāy pācupatā parañcuṭarē"

"Poṉṉalam kaḻaṉi putuvirai maruvi poṟivaṟivaṇṭu icaippāṭa
Aṉṉalaṅkamala taviciṉmēl uṟaṅkum aḷavaṉ vantu ulaviṭa
Aḷḷal ceṉṉel aṅkaḻaṉicūḻ tirumullaivāyilāy tiruppukaḻviruppāl
Paṉṉalaṉ tamiḻāl pāṭuvēṟku aruḷāy pācupatā parañcuṭarē"

"Cantaṉavēṟum kārakil kuraṭum taṉmayil pīliyum kariyin
Tantamum taraḷa kuvaiyum pavaḷakkoṭikaḷum cumantu koṇṭu
Unti vantu iḻi pāli vaṭakarai mullaivāyilāy
Mācilā maṇiyē pantaṉai keṭuttu eṉ paṭutuyarkaḷaiyāy pācupatā parañcuṭarē"

"Maṟṟu nāṉ peṟṟatu ār peṟavallār vaḷḷalē kaḷḷamē pēci
Kuṟṟamē ceyyiṉum kuṇameṉa koḷḷum koḷkaiyāṉ mikaipala ceytēṉ
Ceṟṟumītu ōṭum tiripuram eritta tirumullaivāyilāy aṭiyēṉ
Paṟṟiyilēṉ uṟṟa paṭutuyarkaḷaiyāy pācupatā parañcuṭarē"

"Maṇikaḻu cevvāy veṇṇakai kariya vārkuḻal māmaiyil cāyal
Aṇikaḻu koṅkai aṅkaiyarkaṇṇār aruṇaṭam āṭal aṟāta
Tiṇipoḻil taḻuvu tirumullaivāyil celvaṉē elliyum pakalum
Paṇiyatu ceyvēṉ paṭutuyarkaḷaiyāy pācupatā parañcuṭarē"

"Nampaṉē aṉṟu veṇṇai nallūril nāyiṉēṉ taṉai āṭkoṇṭa
Campuvē umparār toḻutu ēttum taṭaṅkaṭal nañcuvuṇṭa kaṇṭā
Cempoṉmāḷikai cūḻ tirumullaivāyil tēṭiyāṉ tiritarvēṉ kaṇṭa
Paimpoṉē aṭiyēṉ paṭutuyarkaḷaiyāy pācupatā parañcuṭarē"

"Maṭṭulām malarkoṇṭu aṭiyai vaṇaṅkum māṇi taṉmēl matiyātē
Kaṭṭuvāṉ vanta kālaṉai māḻa kāliṉāl āṟuyir cekutta
Ciṭṭaṉē celva tirumullaivāyil celvaṉē ceḻumaṟai pakarnta
Paṭṭaṉē aṭiyēṉ paṭutuyarkaḷaiyāy pācupatā parañcuṭarē"

"Collarum pukaḻāṉ toṇṭaimāṉ kaḻiṟṟai cūḻkoṭi mullai kaṭṭiṭṭu
Ellaiyil iṉpam avaṉ peṟa veḷippaṭṭu aruḷiya iṟaivaṉē eṉṟum
Nallavar paravum tirumullaivāyil nātaṉē naraiviṭai ēṟi
Palkalai poruḷē paṭutuyarkaḷaiyāy pācupatā parañcuṭarē"

"Viraitaru malarmēl ayaṉoṭu mālum veṟuviṭa nīṇṭa emmāṉai
Tiraitaru puṉalcūḻ tirumullaivāyil celvaṉai nāval ārūraṉ
Uraitaru mālai ōr añciṉoṭu añcum uḷkuḷirntu ēttavallārkaḷ
Narai tirai mūppum naṭalaiyum niṉṟi naṇṇuvar viṇṇavarkku aracē"

Thiruchitrambalam. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal sings, "Wealth, true substance, and prosperity are your glorious feet to me," and "I have wandered, speaking disrespectfully and stubbornly, not valuing anyone," expressing the idea that those who rely solely on the Lord need not value anything else in the world. We also recall the sacred words of Thirunavukkarasar Peruman here: "We are not slaves to anyone," and "There is nothing to fear, and nothing to come that will cause fear." Thus, after worshipping at Thirumullaivayil, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal proceeds to Thiruvenpakkam. The devotees in that town, seeing Sundaramoorthy Swamigal arriving at Thiruvenpakkam, come to meet him and lead him. The sacred place of Thiruvenpakkam is currently submerged in the Poondi reservoir near Chennai. The 279th verse:

"Aṇainta toṇṭarkaḷ uṭaṉ vaḷamāka aṅkaṉ nāyakar kōyil muṉ eyti
Kuṇaṅkaḷ ēntiyē paravi añcaliyāl kuvitta kai talaimēl koṇṭu niṉṟu
Vaṇaṅki nīr makiḻkōyil uḷḷīrē eṉṟa vaṉ toṇṭaṟku ūṉṟukōl aruḷi
Iṇaṅkilā moḻiyāl uḷum pōkīr eṉṟu iyaṃpiṉār ētilār pōla"

As mentioned earlier, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, accompanied by the devotees who came to meet him, circumambulated and reached the front of the Lord of Thiruvenpakkam's temple. He praised all the Lord's gracious qualities, worshipped with folded hands raised above his head, and asked, "O Lord, are you residing happily inside this temple?" The Lord then granted Sundaramoorthy Swamigal a staff and, like a stranger, said, "We are here; you may go." Sundaramoorthy Swamigal asks the Lord, "Are you residing happily inside this temple?" because he cannot see the Lord directly with his eyes. He asks this because the Lord has not yet bestowed grace upon him. Therefore, understanding that the Lord is not shining brightly inside the temple, he asks, "Are you residing happily inside this temple?" Sundaramoorthy Swamigal knows that the Lord has resided alternately in Thiruvottiyur, under the Magizha tree, and inside the temple. Therefore, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's question implies, "Are you here inside the temple, or under the Magizha tree?" Earlier, in the Thiruvottiyur Thiruppathikam, he had asked, "Can't you even give me a staff?" Accordingly, the Lord of Thiruvenpakkam gives a staff to Sundaramoorthy Swamigal and says, "You asked if we are inside this temple. We are here. You may go." Because the Lord said, "Uḷōm Pōkīr" (We are here, you may go), this sacred place is also known as "Uḷōm Pōkīr Puram" (the place where the Lord said, 'We are here, you may go'), as seen in inscriptions. Thus, the Lord speaks to Sundaramoorthy Swamigal like a stranger, using unfamiliar words. The 280th verse:

"Piḻaivuḷḷaṉa poruttiṭuvar eṉṟu eṭuttu peṇ pākam
Viḷaivaṭivil perumāṉai veṇpākkam makiḻntāṉai
Aṇinta iṟaiyāṉai pāṭiṉār maḻai tavaḷum neṭumcai
Nāvalūr maṉṉavaṉār eṉpatu tiruppāṭal"

The Lord granted Sundaramoorthy Swamigal a staff and said, "Uḷōm Pōkīr." Sundaramoorthy Swamigal then sings a Thiruppathikam to the Lord of Thiruvenpakkam, beginning with "Piḻaiyuḷḷaṉa Poruttiṭuvar" (He will forgive faults). Sundaramoorthy Swamigal sings about the Lord who delights in Thiruvenpakkam, who is praised by devotees as "Sankaraparanam" (adorned with a snake), and who is adorned with a snake. By singing that the Lord has a feminine half, he implies that the Lord is the abode of compassion. By singing that the Lord approaches snakes, he implies that the Lord accepts even evil as good. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's notes of appeal are embedded in this Thiruppathikam. Thus, the Thiruppathikam composed by Sundaramoorthy Swamigal for Thiruvenpakkam is the 89th Thiruppathikam in the 7th Thirumurai, set to the tune of Seekamarappann. The 1st, 9th, 10th, and 11th verses of this Thiruppathikam are as follows:

Thiruchitrambalam.
"Piḻaiyuḷḷaṉa poruttiṭuvar eṉṟu aṭiyēṉ piḻaitta
Kāl paḻiyataṉai pārātē paṭalam eṉ kaṇ maṟaippittāy
Kuḻaiviravu vaṭikātā kōyiluḷāyō eṉṉa
Uḷaiyuṭaiyāṉ uḷḷiruntu uḷōmpōkīr eṉṟāṉē"

"Poṉṉavilum koṉṟaiyiṉāy pōy makiḻkīḻkiru eṉṟu
Coṉṉa eṉ kāṇāmē cūḷuṟavu makiḻkīḻē
Eṉṉavalla perumāṉē iṅkiruntāyō eṉṉa
Oṉṉalarai kaṇṭāl pōl uḷōmpōkīr eṉṟāṉē"

"Māṇṭikaḷum caṅkiliyai tantuvaru payaṉkaḷellām
Tōṉṟa aruḷ ceytu aḻittāy eṉṟu uraiikka ulakamellām
Īṉṟavaṉē veṇkōyil iṅkiruntāyō eṉṉa
Ūṉṟuvatōr kōl aruḷi uḷōmpōkīr eṉṟāṉē"

"Ērārūm poḻil nilavu veṇpākkam iṭam koṇṭa
Kārārūm iṭaṟṟāṉai kātalittiṭṭa aṉpiṉṉoṭum
Cīrārūm tiruvārūr civaṉ pēr ceṉṉaiyil vaitta
Ārūraṉ tamiḻ vallārkku aṭaiyā valviṉai tāṉē"

Thiruchitrambalam. In every verse of this Thiruppathikam, we see Sundaramoorthy Swamigal singing with a melting heart, "Uḷōm Pōkīr eṉṟāṉē" (He said, 'We are here, you may go'). This sacred place of Thiruvenpakkam is the 17th sacred place in Thondai Nadu. It is also known as Thiruvulampudur and Uḷōm Pōkīr Puram. Thiruvenpakkam is submerged in the Poondi reservoir. When the Poondi reservoir, which was created for Chennai's drinking water, was expanded, this temple was submerged. Therefore, the Shiva idols from there were taken and installed in another place, where they are worshipped. The Lord is Venpakkanathar. The Goddess is Minnal Oli Ammai (Goddess of Lightning Light) and Kanivay Mozhi Ammai (Goddess of Sweet-Speaking Mouth). The Sthala Vriksham (sacred tree) is the Ilanthai tree. The idol of Nambigal with the staff is in this temple. The Goddess guided Sundaramoorthy Swamigal like lightning, intermittently. Therefore, the Goddess in this sacred place is named Minnal Oli Ammai. Thiruvallur is to its northwest, on the road to Thiruppachur, on the way to Oottukkottai. This sacred place is submerged in the reservoir and is therefore inaccessible. The 281st verse:

"Muṉṉiṉṟu muṟaippāṭu pōl moḻinta moḻimālai
Paṇṇum icai tiruppatikam pāṭiya piṉ paṟṟāya
Eṉṉuṭaiya pirāṉ aruḷ iṅku ittaṉai kolām eṉṟu
Maṉṉu perum toṇṭaruṭaṉ vaṇaṅkiyē vaḻi koḷvār eṉṟu amaikiṉṟatu"

After singing this musical garland of words, this melodious Thiruppathikam, as if standing before the Lord and expressing his complaint, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal worships with the great devotees who were with him, thinking, "Is this all the grace of my Lord, who is my support?" Even though Sundaramoorthy Swamigal composed this Thiruppathikam as if appealing to the Lord and expressing his grievances, in reality, it is a verse that expresses joy and overflowing love, praising the Lord's grace. Therefore, Sekkizhar Peruman describes it as "Muṉṉiṉṟu muṟaippāṭu pōl moḻinta" (He spoke as if standing before the Lord and complaining). Sundaramoorthy Swamigal asked, "Are you here?" And the Lord, granting him a staff, replied, "We are here, you may go." In this Thiruppathikam, we see how he praises and appreciates that reply from the Lord in a friendly manner. Thus, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, worshipping the Lord with the great devotees who accompanied him, now continues his journey towards Thiruvarur. We will continue to contemplate. Thiruchitrambalam.

Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 45 - Yeyar Kon Kalikkaama Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Sekkizhar Peruman's Periya Puranam, Part 883, Vampara Vari Vandu Sarukkam, Eyarkon Kalikkaama Nayanar Puranam. Verses 282 and 283. "Anganardham pathiyadhanai agandru poy anbarudan pangaya poondhadam panai soozh pazhaiyanoor uzhaiyeidhi thangguvaar ammai thiruththalaiyaale valam kollum thingal mudiyar aadum thiruvalangattin munnindru thozhuthu ethi muththa endru eduththaruli pannum isai thiruppadhigam paadi magizhndhu eththuvaar annindru vananggi poy thiruvooral amarndhu irainji kanni madhil manimaadak kaanchi maanagar anaindhaar enbavai thiruppaadalkal." Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, having departed from Thiruvenpakkam, proceeds further with the devotees and reaches the boundary of the holy place of Thiruvalangadu.

We see that Thiruvalangadu and Pazhaiyanoor are sung about as a single holy place. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal reaches the boundary of Thiruvalangadu, a place surrounded by lotus-filled ponds and palm trees, where Ammai (Karaikkal Ammaiyar) circumambulated with her head. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, having reached the boundary of Thiruvalangadu, stays there without entering the holy place. The greatness of Thiruvalangadu is that it is circumambulated and worshipped. We see that Sundaramoorthy Swamigal stayed outside in this manner. In the Puranam of Thirugnanasambandha Peruman, we saw the historical event where Thirugnanasambandha Peruman also stayed outside without entering this holy place. This is the place where Ammai walked with her head, isn't it? "Ammai" (mother) here refers to Karaikkal Ammaiyar. If "Ammai" is said plainly, it refers to Karaikkal Ammaiyar in the Saivite tradition. Due to her immeasurable greatness, and the custom of not even uttering her name, she is revered as "Ammai" in the Saivite tradition. We recall here that Sekkizhar Peruman has already shown that Appar also praised Thiruvalangadu. We also see that the Thiruppadhigam (hymn) composed by Ammai is known as "Mootha Thiruppadhigam" (Elder Thiruppadhigam).

We have seen in detail the history of Ammaiyar circumambulating Thiruvalangadu with her head in Ammaiyar's Puranam. Here, we contemplated the state of Ammai, who is seated in Thiruvalangadu, singing at the feet of the Lord and attaining bliss. The Lord is the one who performed the dance of Urdhuva Thandavam, lifting his foot high up to the heavens, and performing the cosmic dance. His dance happens here eternally. This is one of the five great halls of the Lord, known as Rathnasabai (Hall of Gems). Therefore, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, standing and worshipping this holy temple, composes a Thiruppadhigam. This Thiruppadhigam is for the holy place of Thiruvalangadu, set in the ancient Panjara Pann (melody), and is the 52nd Thiruppadhigam in the 7th Thirumurai.

The Thiruppadhigam is as follows: Thiruchitrambalam.
"Muttha muththitharavalla mugil menmulaiyal umai panggaa
Siththa siththi thiram kaattum sivane thevar singgame
Paththa paththar palar pottum paramaa pazhaiyanoormeya
Aththa alangadaa un adiyaarkku adiyen aavene.
Poyye seythu puramburame thiriven thannai pokamai
Meyye vandhu ingu ena aanda meyya meyyar meypporule
Paiyaadu aravam araikkasaiththa paramaa pazhaiyanoor meya
Aiya alangadaa un adiyaarkku adiyen aavene.
Thoondaa vilakkin narchothi thozhuvaarththangal thuyar theerppay
Poondaay elumbai puram moondrum podiyaachchatra ponniyane
Paandu aal vinaigal avai theerkkum paramaa pazhaiyanoormeya
Aandaa alangadaa un adiyaarkku adiyen aaven.
Mari ner onkan madanallaar valaiyil pattu madhimayanggi
Arive azhindhen aiyaa naan maiyaar kandam udaiyaane
Pariya vinaigal avai theerkkum paramaa pazhaiyanoor meya
Arive alangadaa un adiyaarkku adiyen aavene.
Velangadu thadangannar valaiyul pattu un neri marandhu
Malanggu aadi marandhozhindhen maniye muththe maragathame
Palanggu aadi neyyaadi padarpun sadaiyaay pazhaiyanoor
Alangadaa unnudaiya adiyaarkku adiyen aavene.
Ennar thangal eyil eytha enthaay enthai perumaane
Kannay ulagam kaakkindra karuthaa thiruththalagaathaay
Pannar isaigal avai kondu palarum eththum pazhaiyanoor
Anna alangadaa un adiyaarkku adiyen aavene.
Vandar kuzhali umai nangai pangga
Gangai manavaazhaa vindar purangal eriseytha vidaiyaay veda neriyane
Pandaal vinaigal pala theerkkum paramaa pazhaiyanoor meya
Anda alangadaa un adiyaarkku adiyen aavene.
Pezhvaay aravin anaiyaanum periya malarmel urai vanum
Thaazhaadhu un than saran paniyath thazhalaay nindra thaththuvane
Paazhaam vinaigal avai theerkkum paramaa pazhaiyanoor thannai
Aazhvaay alangadaa un adiyaarkku adiyen aavene.
Emman enthai mooththappan ezhil padigaal emaiyaanda
Pemman eema purangattil peyodu aadal purivaane
Palmaa malargal avai kondu palarum eththum pazhaiyanoor
Ammaa alangadaa un adiyaarkku adiyen aavene.
Paththar siththar palar eththum paraman pazhaiyanoor meya
Aththan alangadan than adimaiththirame
Anbaagi siththar siththam vaiththa pugazh siruvan oorana on thamizhgal
Paththum paadi aaduvaar paramanadiye panivare."
Thiruchitrambalam. Having composed this melodious Thiruppadhigam, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal departs from here, worships the holy place of Thakkolam, known as Thiruvooral, and reaches Kanchi Maanagar (Kanchipuram). Kanchi Maanagar is one of the seven Mukthi Sthalams (places of liberation) and the capital of Thondai Nadu. Therefore, Sekkizhar Peruman refers to it as Kanchi Maanagar. Sekkizhar Peruman, without any delay in the journey, such as stopping along the way, quickly narrates this sacred history, showing his intention not to prolong Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's suffering, which began when his sight was obscured after crossing the boundary of Thiruvottiyur.

We see that the historical accounts in this section are moving very quickly, with the intention of not prolonging Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's suffering. From Thiruvottiyur to Kanchi Maanagar, Sekkizhar Peruman only mentioned the completion of the Thiruppadhigams sung in Thiruvenpakkam, where Sundaramoorthy Swamigal was blessed with a walking stick. He quickly moved through the other holy places, mentioning the Thiruppadhigams there with verbal participles, and concluded by saying that Sundaramoorthy Swamigal reached Kanchi Maanagar to receive one of his eyes. Now, Sekkizhar Peruman, with his sweet poetic skill, is going to narrate the wonderful sacred history of Sundaramoorthy Swamigal receiving the light of one of his eyes in Kanchi Maanagar.

Let us continue to contemplate. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 46 - Yeyar Kon Kalikkaama Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Sekkizhar Peruman's Periya Puranam, Part 884, Vampara Vari Vandu Sarukkam, Eyarkon Kalikkaama Nayanar Puranam. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal reaches Thirukachi Ekambam. Verse 284: "Thenilavu pozhil kachchi thirukkama kottamathil oonil valar uyirkkellam ozhiyaadha karunaiyinaal aana thiru arampurakkum ammai thirukkoiyilin mun vaanivalar thiruvaayil vananginaar van thondar." Thirukachi Kamakottam, surrounded by gardens filled with honeybees, is the place in Kanchipuram where Kamakshi Ammaiyar performs penance and fosters dharma (righteousness). This is where the Kamakshi Ammaiyar Temple is currently located. Ammai's compassion flows to all types of mature souls born in creation, according to their nature. Ammaiyar is compassionate to all beings equally.

This is the place where she shows compassion and fosters dharma. Therefore, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal first seeks and worships Ammaiyar's grace. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, the ardent devotee, worships before the towering entrance of Ammaiyar's temple, which reaches up to the sky. We have contemplated in his Puranam the history of Thirugnanasambandha Peruman also worshipping Ammai here. The mother nurtures and matures all the countless beings that come into creation. If it is Ammai's compassionate work to provide worldly enjoyments to the beings that come into creation, then the father's work involves the five functions of protection, destruction, and recreation. Sekkizhar Peruman illustrates this in the subsequent verses.

Verses 285 and 286: "Thozhudhu vizhundhu ezhundhu arulaal thudiththup poy thol ulagam muzhudhum azhiththu azhiththu aakkum mudhalvar thiru ekambam pazhudhil adiyaar munbu puga pukku panugindraar ezhudha yen thirumunbe yenmozhiyen endru irainji vinnaazhvaar amudhu unna mikka perum vidam unda kannaalaa kachchi ekambane kadaiyanen ennaadha pizhai poruththu ingu yaan kaana ezhil pavala vannaa kan azhiththu arulaay ena veezhndhu vananginaar." Sundaramoorthy Swamigal prostrates himself, rises, praises with grace, and then enters the Thiruekambam temple of the Prime Lord, who protects, destroys, and recreates all these worlds, performing the five cosmic functions. He enters behind the faultless devotees. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal praises the Lord with grace. This implies that even to worship the Lord, one needs His grace. "Avan arulale avan thazh vananggi" (by His grace alone, one worships His feet) is from Thiruvachagam. In other holy places, it is customary and traditional to first worship the Lord and then, as a continuation of that worship, worship the Goddess. Here, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal worships Ammaiyar separately at Thirukachi Kamakottam. Furthermore, our great Acharyas (spiritual teachers) are those who have the discipline of worshipping no one other than Lord Shiva. Here, in Kanchipuram alone, as an exception to this rule, our great Acharyas have worshipped Ammaiyar's temple.

We see this in their Puranams. Even here, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal went to Ammaiyar's temple and worshipped Ammaiyar separately first. This happened by divine grace to enable Sundaramoorthy Swamigal to regain his sight. Sekkizhar Peruman states this as "Oonil valar uyirkkellam ozhiyaadha karunaiyinaal aana thiru arampurakkum ammai." We know that the Thirukamakottam where Kamakshi Ammai resides is a separate temple, and the Thiruekambam where the Lord resides is a separate temple. Therefore, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal worships Ammai and then goes to Thiruekambam to worship. How does he worship? He worships by following behind the faultless devotees who go before him. These devotees are blessed to lead Sundaramoorthy Swamigal. Therefore, they are faultless devotees. Moreover, they are devotees who remove all the faults of beings. Since Sundaramoorthy Swamigal has no eyesight, it is evident that the devotees lead the way, and he follows behind them. Furthermore, this teaches us the custom of following devotees. This is philosophical. We have also seen that Sundaramoorthy Swamigal composed the Thiruthondar Thogai Thiruppadhigam about this. He enters and worships in that manner. Sundaram means falsehood. It also has a meaning of ignorance. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal says this because he is a liar and therefore not worthy of speaking anything before the Lord.

"Thannenchari vadhu poyyarka poyththapin than nenje thannai sodum" (Let not one's mind know falsehood; if it does, one's own mind will punish oneself) is from Thirukkural. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal knows that the Lord, who is the life of all lives, knows the falsehood he has committed and what he desires. Therefore, he has nothing else to say to the Lord. He bows with folded hands, indicating that he has committed a mistake and is not worthy of speaking anything. "O Lord, who swallowed the great poison to enable the gods of the celestial world to drink nectar, O Lord with eyes, O Lord of Kachi Ekambam, please forgive the mistakes I, a lowly one, have committed unintentionally, and grant me sight so that I may see your beautiful coral color," thus Sundaramoorthy Swamigal pleads. "Kannalaa" (Lord with eyes) implies one who bestows grace. He implies, "Just as you swallowed poison and gave nectar, please forgive my mistakes and grant me sight." Here, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal addresses the Lord as "Kachi Ekambane" (Lord of Kachi Ekambam). Kachi is the place where He openly resides. He addresses Him as "Pavala Vanna" (coral-colored), which refers to the Lord's bodily hue. He addresses Him as "Vidam Undavare" (one who swallowed poison), which refers to the Lord's nature. Therefore, in accordance with the Thiruvachagam "Munnam avanudaiya naamam kettal" (first, she heard His name), Sundaramoorthy Swamigal addresses and worships the Lord in these three ways. He tells the Lord that the mistake was not committed with the intention of doing wrong. "Kadaiyanen ennaadha pizhai" (I, a lowly one, committed an unintentional mistake), he says.

If it were a deliberate mistake, it could not be forgiven; the karmic fruit would have to be experienced to be exhausted. This is an unintentional mistake, so he pleads with the Lord to forgive this mistake, saying that he does not want to experience it any further than he already has. He says that granting him sight is not for any other reason, but to see the Lord's beautiful coral color, and therefore addresses the Lord as "Pavala Vanna." Thus, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal pleads. Verse 287: "Pangkayach chenggai thaliraal panimalar kondu arutchiththu sengkayalkan malaivalli panindha sevadi ninaindhu ponggiya anbodhu paravi pottiya aroorarkku manggai thazhuva kuzhaindhaar maraindha idakkan koduththaar." Kanchipuram is the place where Ammai performs Shiva Puja. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, remembering the sacred feet of Thirukachi Ekambapperuman, which Ammai worshipped by scattering cool flowers with her lotus-like red tender hands, praises and worships with overflowing love. To Nambi Arooran, who thus worshipped, Thiruekambar, whose form softened when embraced by Uma Mangai, grants the sight of his lost left eye. The left side is Ammai's side. Here, it seems that because Sundaramoorthy Swamigal first worshipped Ammaiyar and received her grace, the Lord granted him the sight of his left eye. Thus, Kachi Ekambapperuman grants Sundaramoorthy Swamigal the sight of his left eye.

The place where Sundaramoorthy Swamigal lost his sight was Thiruvottiyur. He regained his left eye in Thirukachi Ekambam. He is yet to regain his right eye in Thiruvarur. These three places—Thiruvottiyur, Kanchipuram (Thirukachi Ekambam), and Thiruvarur—are Prithvi Sthalams (earth element places). As the saying goes, "Oduggi malarththulladhaam," whatever merges somewhere will reappear from there. Moreover, earth (mann) is the first of the 36 Tattvas (principles), and the nature of the first element, Prithvi, is suitable for creation and manifestation. Therefore, such divine miracles have occurred in these places. Upon regaining the sight of his left eye, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal praises the Lord and composes a Thiruppadhigam. Verse 288: "Gnnalanthan idandhavanum nalir visumbu kadandhavanum moolanthan arivariyark kannaliththu mulaichchuvattu kolanthan kaattudhalum kurugi vizhundhu ezhundhu kazhiththu aalanthan ugandhavan endru eduththu aadi paadinaar." Thirumal, who dug the earth, and Brahma, who tried to cross the sky, could not find the beginning and end of our Lord. Our Lord stood unknowable to them. To show Sundaramoorthy Swamigal His form, which bears the mark of Ammai's embrace, He grants him one eye and enables him to see. "Kaattuviththal aaroruvar kaanaadhaare" (who can see unless He shows?) is a saying from Thevaram. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal is one who always sees the Lord with his inner eye in a state of Shiva Yoga.

Now, the Lord has granted him sight to see Him with his outer eye as well. Thus, He enabled him to see. Therefore, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal composes the Thiruppadhigam beginning with "Aalanthan ugandhu," aligning with the theme "Kaana kan adiyen petravare" (I, your servant, have received eyes to see). Since he first worshipped Ammaiyar and received her grace, it is implied that the Lord showed him the form bearing the mark of Ammaiyar's embrace. The Thiruppadhigam composed by Sundaramoorthy Swamigal is the 61st Thiruppadhigam in the 7th Thirumurai, set in the Thakkési Pann (melody). The first, tenth, and eleventh verses of this Thiruppadhigam are as follows: Thiruchitrambalam.
"Aalanthan ugandhu amudhu seythaanai aadhiyai amarar thozhudhiyeththum
Seelanthan peridhum udaiyaanai sindhippar avar sindhai ulaanaai ezhavaar
Kuzhalaal umai nangai endrum eththi vazhipada petra kaalakaalanai
Kamban emmaanai kaanakkan adiyen petravare.
Elgalindri imayavarkonai eesanai vazhipaadu seyvaal pol
Ullaththu ulgi ugandhu umai nangai vazhipadach chendru nindravaa kandu
Vellam kaatti veruttida anji verivioadi thazhuva velipatta
Kallakkambanai engal piraanai kaanakkan adiyen petravare.
Petram eru ugandhu era vallaanai periya emperumaan endru eppodhum
Katravan paravappaduvaanai kaanakkan adiyen petradhu endru
Kottravan kamban kooththan emmaanai kulirpozhil thiru naaval arooran
Nattramizh ivai eer aindhum vallar nanneri ulagu eyththuvarthame."
Thiruchitrambalam. Verse 289: "Paadi miga paravasamaay panivaarkku paavaivudan neediya kolam kaatta niraindha viruppudan irainji soodiya anjaliyinaray thozhudhu purambondhu anbu koodiya mey thondarvudan kumbittu angu inidhu amarvaar enbadhu thiruppaadal." Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, thus singing, becomes greatly ecstatic and bows. The Lord shows him His long form, accompanied by the Goddess. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, with overflowing desire, worships Him, bows with folded hands on his head, and then comes out of the temple. He then joins the true devotees, bows with them, and happily resides there. Let us continue to contemplate. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 47 - Yeyar Kon Kalikkaama Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Sekkizhar Peruman's Periya Puranam, Part 885, Vampara Vari Vandu Sarukkam, Eyarkon Kalikkaama Nayanar Puranam. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, after regaining his left eye in Kanchipuram, stays there for a few days, worships the Lord, and then begins his pilgrimage towards Thiruvarur. Verses 290 and 291: "Maamalaiyaal mulaichchuvadum valaiththazhumbum anindha madhippoo malivaar sadaiyarai pottri aruladhu aagaththé malarvaar pozhil kaanchi thirunagaram kadandhu agazhvaar paamalar maalai padhigam thiruvaroor mel paravi andhiyum nanpagalum ena eduththu aarvaththudan nasaiyaal enthai piraan thiruvaroor endru kol eythuvadhu endru sandha isai paadi poy thaanggariya aadharavu vandhu anaiya anbarudan magizhndhu vazhi kolgindraar enbavai thiruppaadalkal." Sundaramoorthy Swamigal praises the Lord with long matted hair, who bears the mark of Ammaiyar's embrace and the scar from her bangles, and whose grace is abundant. The Lord's grace to Sundaramoorthy Swamigal in Kanchipuram is limited to granting only his left eye. Having received the Lord's grace, he crosses the boundary of Kanchi city and proceeds towards Thiruvarur. At that time, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal composes a Thiruppadhigam remembering Thiruvarur. It is the Thiruppadhigam that begins with "Andhiyum Nanpagalum" (Dusk and Noon). In it, he sings with melodious rhythm, indicating his desire to know when he will reach the Lord's Thiruvarur. "Enthai piraan thiruvaroor endru kol eythuvadhu" (When will I reach my Lord's Thiruvarur?) is the theme and inner meaning of this Thiruppadhigam.

In each verse of this Thiruppadhigam, which begins with "Andhiyum Nanpagalum," Sundaramoorthy Swamigal asks, "Endru kol eythuvadhe?" (When will I reach?). These lines form the crown of the Thiruppadhigam. He praises the Lord of Aroor in various ways in all the verses, saying, "Enthai piranarai en uyirkku in amudhai idubali kolliyai ezhulagaazhiyai en maniyai em iraiyai" (My Lord, the sweet nectar of my life, who receives offerings, the ruler of the seven worlds, my gem, my Lord), and sets it to the question, "When will I reach the Lord in Thiruvarur?" Sekkizhar Peruman refers to this Thiruppadhigam as "Sandha Isai Paadi" (singing with melodious rhythm). "Isai" here refers to "Pann" (melody). It means a Thiruppadhigam set to music. The "Pann Puraneermai" (melody of the outer nature) of this Thiruppadhigam is suitable for dawn. Therefore, we understand that Sundaramoorthy Swamigal departs from Kanchipuram towards Thiruvarur at dawn, during the Vaikarai Yamam (early morning). This also implies that the time for his remaining suffering, which is like darkness, to disappear is approaching. The Thiruppadhigam composed by Sundaramoorthy Swamigal is the 83rd Thiruppadhigam in the 7th Thirumurai, set in the Puraneermai Pann. The first, second, third, and tenth verses of this Thiruppadhigam are as follows: Thiruchitrambalam.
"Andhiyum nanpagalum anju padham solli mundhi ezhum pazhaiya valvinai moodaamun
Sindhai paramariya then thiru aaru pukku enthai piranarai endru kol eythuvadhe.
Nindra vinai kodumai neengga irupozhudhum thundrumalar ittuchchoozhum valam seythu
Thenran manam kamazhum then thiru aroor oor pukku endhan manam kulira endru kol eythuvadhe.
Munnai mudarpiravi moodhariyamaiyinaal pinnai ninaindhanavum pedhuravum ozhiya
Sennal vayalkazhani then thiru aroor pukku en uyirkku in amudhai endru kol eythuvadhe.
Minnedum sedaiyanmeviya aroorai nannedum kaadhalmaiyaal naavalarkon oorana
Pannedum sol malarkondu ittana paththum vallar pon udai vinnulagam nannuvar punniyare."
Thiruchitrambalam. Verse 292: "Mannu thiruppadhigal thorum vanniyodu koovizhamum sennimisai vaiththu uvandhaar koyilin mun sendru irainji pannu thamizh thodai saaththi paraviye pondhu anaindhaar annamali vayal thadanggal soozhndha thiru aamaaththoor." Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, on his way to Thiruvarur, joyfully places the Vanni (Prosopis cineraria) and Vilvam (Aegle marmelos) leaves on his head at each holy place, goes before the Lord's temples, worships, offers garlands of Tamil verses, praises, and then proceeds. These are the places on the way from Kanchipuram to Thiruvarur. These include Thiruvil, Thirukkurangganil Muttram, Purisai Nadu Purisai, Thiruvenkuntram, Thindisaram, Thiruppuravaar, Panangattur, and other places. These are all places located on the road directly south from Kanchipuram. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal worships all these places as he goes. However, due to our lack of merit, we do not have Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's Thiruppadhigams for these places. Most of these places are Vaipu Sthalams (places mentioned in hymns but not necessarily having a dedicated hymn) and some are Thevaram-sung places. Some of these places are also Vaipu Sthalams in Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's Thiruppadhigams. Having passed through all these places, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal reaches Thiru Aamathur, a place surrounded by fields and ponds where swans abound.

Verse 293: "Angganarai aamaaththoor azhagar thamai adivananggi thangum isai thiruppadhigam paadi poy thaaranikku manggalamaam perum thondai valanaadu kadandhu anaindhaar senggan valavan pirandha seer naadu neer naadu." Sekkizhar Peruman refers to the Lord residing in Thiru Aamathur as "Angganar Aamathur Angganar" (the compassionate Lord of Aamathur), because he was like a mother to the cows there. He also refers to Him as "Aamathur Azhagar" (the beautiful one of Aamathur). "Azhagar" is the Lord's name in Thiru Aamathur. The Lord's name here is also Abirameswarar and Azhagiyanathar. The Goddess's name is Mukthambigai. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal bows to the Lord in such a Thiru Aamathur and composes a Thiruppadhigam. Sekkizhar Peruman refers to this Thiruppadhigam as "Thangum Isai Thiruppadhigam" (a melodious hymn that lingers). "Thanguthal" means to remain stable. This is a melodious Thiruppadhigam with a lingering sound. "Thanggalvosai" means that in each line of the song, the first words are repeated twice in a poetic device called "sol porul pinvaru nilai." In this way, the structure of this Thiruppadhigam is such that the sound lingers, making it impossible to sing the verses continuously and quickly. We see this in all the verses, such as "Kandanen Kandanen" (I saw, I saw), "Paaduvan Paaduvan" (I will sing, I will sing), and "Theduvan Theduvan" (I will search, I will search).

Similarly, we see that the sound lingers in the last word of each line. The "Pann" (melody) for this Thiruppadhigam is Kollikkavvanam. "Thangum Isai" also means a Thiruppadhigam that remains eternally in the Lord's feet in a state of Shiva Yoga. The Thiruppadhigam composed by Sundaramoorthy Swamigal for Thiru Aamathur is the 45th Thiruppadhigam in the 7th Thirumurai, set in the Kollikkavvanam Pann. The following are the first, second, third, and tenth verses of this Thiruppadhigam: Thiruchitrambalam.
"Kandanen kandanen kaarigaiyaal than karuththanaay
Aandanen aandanen aamaaththoor em adigalukku
Poondanen poondanen poy endru solluvan kelmingal
Meendanen meendanen vedhavithth allaadha varkatke.
Orndhanen vorndhanen ullaththulle nindra on porul
Serndhanen serndhanen sendru thiruottriyur pukku
Saarndhanen saarndhanen sanggili menthol thadamulai
Aarndhanen aarndhanen aamaaththoor ayyan arul adhe.
Theduvan theduvan semmalar paadhanggal naaldhorum
Naaduvan naaduvan naabikku mele or naal viral
Maaduvan maaduvan van kai pidiththu magizhndhu ule
Aaduvan aaduvan aamaaththoor em adigale.
Aiyyanai aththanai aaludai aamaaththoor annanai
Meyyanai meyyarkku meypporulana vimalanai
Maiyanai maiyani kandathanai vanthondan oorana sol
Poyvondrum indri pulambuvar porkalil servare."
Thiruchitrambalam. Having composed the Thiruppadhigam in Thiru Aamathur, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal crosses Thondai Nadu. This Thondai Nadu is sung by Sekkizhar Peruman as "Thaaranikku Manggalamaam Perunthondai Valanaadu" (the prosperous Thondai Nadu, auspicious to the earth). What does "Mangalam" mean for a country? It means the qualities that bring essential prosperity and glory to that country. The special feature of Thondai Nadu is that the entire country shines with Shiva-consciousness. Sekkizhar Peruman has extensively sung about the holy places of Thondai Nadu in the Puranam of Thirukurippu Thonda Nayanar. Not only that, but Thondai Nadu is also the country where Kanchipuram, where Ammaiyar performed penance and received boons, is located. Therefore, because Ammaiyar performed penance and received boons, Thondai Nadu possesses Kamakottam, which fosters all the dharmas that save the world.

Therefore, this Thondai Nadu becomes a prosperous country auspicious to the earth. "Thondai Nadu Saandrör Udaiththu" (Thondai Nadu is rich in scholars) means it is a country that holds many scholars within itself. That is a unique specialty of Thondai Nadu. In accordance with "Evvazhi nallavar aadavar avvazhi nalla vaazhiya nilane" (wherever good men are, there the land flourishes) and "Ulaginil ellam mangalam tharum malarvilam podhagam" (the flower-like elephant that brings auspiciousness to the whole world), Thondai Nadu possesses such greatness. Having crossed such a glorious Thondai Nadu, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal proceeds to Chola Nadu, which is the birthplace of Kochchenggatchozha Nayanar. Although many great people were born in Chola Nadu, Kochchenggatchozha Nayanar was born with divine grace, knowing his previous state. That is the specialty of his birth. Sekkizhar Peruman describes Chola Nadu as the country blessed with the greatness of Kochchenggatchozha Nayanar, who ruled by building 78 Maadak Kovils (temples with elevated platforms) for Lord Shiva. He refers to it as "Neer Nadu" (water country), which means the Kaveri country. Verse 294: "Annaattin marunggu thiru araththuraiyaich chendru eythi minnaarum padai mazhuvaar virai malarththaal panindhu ezhundhu solmaalai malar kalvaay agil enum thodai saaththi man aarvaththu thiruththondarvudan magizhndhu vaiginaar." Within the boundary of that country, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal reaches Thirunelvaayil Araththurai, a place on one side. That is, Thirunelvaayil Araththurai is a place in Nadu Nadu (central region) before entering the boundary of Chola Nadu. There, he prostrates himself at the Lord's flower-like feet, rises, and offers the garland of Tamil verses beginning with "Kalvaay Agil." Sundaramoorthy Swamigal joyfully resides there with the devotees, full of love. If this place is part of Nadu Nadu, and Sekkizhar Peruman mentions that Sundaramoorthy Swamigal visited this place after stating that he went to Chola Nadu, it implies that Nadu Nadu was under the rule of the Pallavas, who were of the Chola royal lineage, during those days. Therefore, it was considered part of Chola Nadu. Nadu Nadu was ruled by the Munaiyatharaiyar lineage, who were subordinate kings to the Cholas at that time. From Thiru Aamathur, there was a path that went directly southwest through Viluppuram and Ulundurpettai to Thirunelvaayil Araththurai, crossing the Niva River. Thirunavukkarasu Peruman's journey from Thillai to Thirunarayur in the west and then to Seerkazhi, and Thirugnanasambandha Peruman's journey from Thirunelvaayil Araththurai back to the Chola Nadu places, worshipping them, and then to Seerkazhi via Thirunarayur and Thirukaruvariyalur, followed these paths. Here, the point is that Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, on his way from Thiru Aamathur to Thiruvarur, worshipped places like Thirunelvaayil Araththurai, Thiruvaavaduthurai, and Thiruthurutthi. We will see this history. We also see internal evidence of this in Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's Thiruppadhigams, as his own words. The Thiruppadhigam composed by Sundaramoorthy Swamigal in Thirunelvaayil Araththurai is the 3rd Thiruppadhigam in the 7th Thirumurai, set in the Indhalappann (melody). The first, third, and tenth verses of this Thiruppadhigam are as follows: Thiruchitrambalam.
"Kalvaay agilum kadhir maa maniyum kalandhu undhi varum nilavin karaimel
Nelvaayil araththurai needu uraiyum nila velmadhi soodiya nirmalanai
Nalvaayil seythaar nadandhaara uduththaar naraiththaar irandhaara endru
Naanilaththil sellaay kazhugindradhu arindhu adiyen thodarndhen uyyappovadhu or soozhal sollee.
Putraadum aravam arai aarththu ugandhaay punidhaa poruvelvidai oordhiyinaay
Etre oru kan ilan ninnaiyallaal nelvaayil araththurai nirmalanai
Matrel oru pattru ilan emperumaan vandaar kuzhalaal manggai panggine
Attraar piravikkadal neendhi eri adiyen uyyappovadhu or soozhal sollee.
Neervoorum neduvayal soozh puravin nelvaayil araththurai nirmalanai
Theroor neduveedhi nanmaadamali then naavalarkon adiththondan
Ani arooran uraiththana narchamizhin migumaalai orpaththu ivai kattruvallar
Kaarvoor kazhivandu arai yaanai mannar avar aagi or vinmuzhudhu aalbavare."
Thiruchitrambalam. Having composed the Thiruppadhigam in Thirunelvaayil Araththurai, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal stays there happily with the devotees for a few days. Let us continue to contemplate. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 48 - Yeyar Kon Kalikkaama Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Sekkizhar Peruman's Periya Puranam, Part 886, Vampara Vari Vandu Sarukkam, Eyarkon Kalikkaama Nayanar Puranam. Verse 295: "Paramar thiruvaraththuraiyai panindhu poy pala padhigal viravi mazhvidai uyarththaar virai malarththaal thozhudhu ethi uravu neerththadam ponni adaindha anbarudan aadi aravanindhaar amarndha thiru aavadhu thanthuraiyai anaindhaar." Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, having worshipped the Lord's holy place of Thiruaraththurai, praises many holy places where the Lord resides and reaches the holy place of Thiruvaavaduthurai, where He is enshrined. From Thirunelvaayil Araththurai, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal crossed the Niva River near Thiru Yerukkaththampaliyur and then proceeded directly south through Jayankondacholapuram, crossed the Kollidam River near Thiruvaaypadi, and reached the Kaveri River. This is the route Sundaramoorthy Swamigal took from Thirunelvaayil Araththurai to Thiruvaavaduthurai. Not only this route, but there is also another route that seems plausible: from Thirunelvaayil Araththurai, along the northern bank of the Niva River, crossing the Niva River near Koodalaiyattru, and then proceeding directly south, crossing the Kollidam River and the Kaveri River, and reaching near Kanjanur and Kodikka. The places along the first route include Govandapuththur, Vijayamanngai, Kottaiyur, Kudamookku, and others. The places along the second route include Thirupanandhaazh, Thirumanggalakkudi, Kanjanur, and others. This verse mentions that he reached Thiruvaavaduthurai after crossing the Kaveri. Therefore, it is likely that he came via the second route.

He then reaches Thiruvaavaduthurai, a cool place that has the power to cure the heat of birth, along with the devotees who accompanied him and bathed in the Kaveri. Verse 296: "Anggu anaivaar thamai adiyaar edhir kolla pukku aruli ponggu thirukkoiyilinai pudai valam kondu ul anaindhu ganggai vaazh sadaiyaay or kannilen ena kavalvaar ingu enakku aar uravu ennum thiruppadhigam eduththu isaiththaar." Sundaramoorthy Swamigal reaches Thiruvaavaduthurai. The devotees welcome Nambi. He enters the holy place, circumambulates the overflowing temple, and then enters inside. He then composes a Thiruppadhigam, beginning with "Gangai Vaazh Sadaiyaay" (O Lord with matted hair where Ganga resides), lamenting, "I have no eye here, who is my kin?" He composes the Thiruppadhigam such that the theme "Ingu enakku aar uravu" (Who is my kin here?) is embedded within it. "Aar enakku uravu amarargal ere" (Who is my kin, O Lord of the celestials?) is the crown of this Thiruppadhigam. Both Thiruppadhigams composed by Sundaramoorthy Swamigal for Thiruvaavaduthurai are set in the Thakkési Pann. The Thiruppadhigam beginning with "Maraiyavan" (the Brahmin) was composed during a previous visit. This Thiruppadhigam, beginning with "Gangai Vaazh Sadaiyaay," is very moving. This Thiruppadhigam is the 70th Thiruppadhigam in the 7th Thirumurai. The first, second, and tenth verses of this Thiruppadhigam are as follows: Thiruchitrambalam.
"Gangai vaazh sadaiyaay gannanaadhaa kaalakaalane kaamanu gannale
Ponggumaa kadal vidamizhaththaane boothanaadhanai punniyaa punidhaa
Sengganmaalvidaiyaay theliththene theerththane thiruvaavaduththuraiyul
Angganaa ena anjal endru arulaay aariyanakku uravu amarargal ere.
Mannin mel mayanggik kidappene valiya vandhu ena aandu kondaane
Kannilen udambil adu noyaal karuththazhindhu unakke poraiyanen
Thennilayérikkum sadaiyane devane thiru aavaduththuraiyul
Annanale ena anjal endru arulaay aariyanakku uravu amarargal ere.
Vendhalai pirai kondraiyum aravum verimaththamum viravi munmudiththa
Endaimaa malar senjsadaiyanai eesanai thiru aavaduththuraiyul
Andavanaai singgadiyappenn anukka vanthondan aarvaththaal uraiththa
Than thamizh malar paththum vallargal saadhalum pirappum aruppaare."
Thiruchitrambalam. Now, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal departs from Thiruvaavaduthurai and proceeds to Thiruthurutthi. Verses 297 and 298: "Thiruppadhigam kodu paravi panindhu thiruvarulaal poy virippinodum thiruththuruththithanai mevi malargalul aruththiyinaal pukku irainji adiyenmel uttra pini varuththam ena ozhiththu arulavendum ena vanangguvaar paraviye panindha avarkku paramar thiruvarul purivaar viraviya ippini adaiya thavippadharkku veru aaga varamalar vandarai theerththa vadagulaththukkuzhi ena karavil thiruththondardhaam kaiththozhudhu purappattaar." Having sung the Thiruppadhigam in Thiruvaavaduthurai, praised the Lord, and worshipped Him, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal receives His divine permission and proceeds further. He reaches the holy place of Thiruthurutthi with great desire, enters the temple, and worships the Lord's feet with devotion. What does Sundaramoorthy Swamigal say to the Lord in his worship? He asks the Lord to remove the suffering of the disease that has afflicted his body. Therefore, we see here that when he arrived at this place, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal had a new disease, and he appeals to the Lord of Thiruthurutthi to remove it. The local tradition also states that Sundaramoorthy Swamigal suffered from a skin disease. To Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, who thus pleaded, the Lord grants His grace. The Lord instructs him to bathe in the northern pond on the northern side of this temple, saying that it is different from other holy waters and will completely cure this disease that has afflicted him. This holy place has a northern pond on its northern side, distinct from other holy waters. The Lord instructs him to bathe there. The Lord specifically instructs him to bathe in the holy water for this purpose. That is, while bathing for daily rituals and for worshipping the temple is customary, the Lord's command here is to bathe separately in the northern pond to cure this disease. The northern pond mentioned by the Lord is located in the northern courtyard of the Lord's temple within the temple premises. On its bank, there is a separate temple for Nambi. There, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's idol is enshrined in a standing posture with one eye. That is a wonderful idol that melts the heart. It is a great idol worthy of being seen and worshipped. Many devotees worship here and regularly bathe in this pond to cure diseases and be freed from other sufferings. "Theertham" means purity. It came to mean water that purifies. Thus, with the Lord's grace, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, along with the guileless devotees, departs from before the Lord and goes to the northern pond. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's Thiruppadhigam, in which he pleaded with the Lord to remove his suffering from the disease, is not available to us due to our lack of merit. Verse 299: "Mikka punal theerththaththin mun anaindhu vedhamellam thokka vadivaay irundha thuruththiyaar thamai thozhudhu pukku adhanil moozhgudhalum puthiya pini adhu neenggi akkaname mani oli seer thirumeni aayinaar." Sundaramoorthy Swamigal goes before that holy water, the northern pond, worships the Lord of Thiruthurutthi, who is the embodiment of all the Vedas, and enters the holy water. The water, as one of the Lord's eight forms, has the great grace of purifying both inside and out. In this holy place, the Lord, who embodies all the divine qualities mentioned in the Vedas, is seated, manifesting Himself. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal worships Him and bathes in this pond. It is customary to worship and praise the Lord while bathing. We see the themes of Thiruvembavai and Thiruvachagam, such as "Udaiyaan porpaadham eththi irunjunai neer aadelor empavaay" (Praising the Lord's golden feet, let us bathe in the great pond, O maiden) and "Paadha thirampaadi aadelor empavaay" (Singing the glory of His feet, let us bathe, O maiden). Furthermore, here, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal bathes according to the Lord's command. Therefore, remembering and worshipping the Lord becomes a special rule here. We should remember the Thirumurai sayings here, such as "Gangai aadilen kaveri aadilen engum eesan enaadhuvarkku illaiye" (What use is bathing in Ganga or Kaveri if one does not say 'Isan' everywhere?) and that there is no benefit if one bathes in crores of holy waters but has no love for Lord Shiva. The moment he emerged from the bath, the disease that had afflicted Nambi's body disappeared. This disease was not present before but appeared recently. It is a traditional belief that the disease that afflicted Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's body was due to heat. Nambi's body was naturally radiant. Just as the light of a gem is inseparable from it, the radiance of Nambi's body was of that nature. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal was known as "Azhagar" (the beautiful one) because his body possessed the beauty of the Lord's form. We see that when he was playing, King Narasimha Munaiyaraiyar saw Sundaramoorthy Swamigal and was filled with love for him. In Thirukkai, Upamanyu Mamunivar worshipped Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, seeing his radiant form.

Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, who possessed such a radiant body, saw that upon emerging from the holy pond, the disease that had afflicted him had disappeared, and his natural radiant body shone forth. Verse 300: "Kandavargal adhisayippa karaiyeri udaipunaindhu mandu perum kaadhalinaal koyilinai vandhadaindhu thondar edhir minnumaa megam ennum solpadhigam endhisaiyum arindhu uyya ezhu eduththu isaiththaar." Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, to the astonishment of those who saw him, climbed out of the northern pond, changed his wet clothes, and with great love, came to the temple. Those who saw him were astonished. The people who saw Sundaramoorthy Swamigal before he bathed in the holy pond, with the disease afflicting his body, and then saw him after he emerged, radiant and free from the disease, were astonished, wondering what miracle they had witnessed. The devotees also rejoiced. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, after worshipping the Lord inside the temple, came out and composed a Thiruppadhigam before the assembled devotees, beginning with "Minnumaa Megam" (Shining Cloud). Sundaramoorthy Swamigal composed this Thiruppadhigam, saying, "En udambu adum pini idar keduththaanai naan marakkumaaru ennain" (How can I forget Him who removed the suffering of the disease that afflicted my body?).
