This Thiruppadhigam was composed in the third person, referring to the Lord. Therefore, it is understood that this Thiruppadhigam was sung before the devotees. The great ones do not speak to worldly people, only to devotees.

The state of commanding worldly people is different from the state of singing before devotees. The theme of this Thiruppadhigam is that the world should know and be saved by this divine greatness, in accordance with the Thirumandhiram saying, "Naan petra inbam peruga ivvaiyagam" (May the joy I received spread throughout the world). This Thiruppadhigam was composed to praise the immense grace the Lord bestowed upon him. This Thiruppadhigam, sung for Thiruthurutthi and Thiruvelvikudi together, is the 74th Thiruppadhigam in the 7th Thirumurai, set in the Gandhara Pann (melody). The first and tenth verses of this Thiruppadhigam are as follows: Thiruchitrambalam.
"Minnumaa megamgal pozhindhu izhindhu aruvi vedipadak karaiyodum thirai konarndhu etttrum
Annamam kaveri agan karai urai vaar adi inai thozhudhu ezhum anbaram adiyaar
Sonnavaaru arivaaru thuruththiyaar velvikudivullar adigalai sediyanen naayen
Ennai naan marakkumaaru emperumaanai en udambu adum pini idar keduththaanai.
Manggaivor kooru ugandhu eru ugandhu eri maaralaar thiripuram neeru ezhachchettrra
Anggaiyaan kazhal adiyandri mattrra ariyaan adiyavarkku adiyavan thozhuvan arooran
Ganggaiyaar kaveri thuruththiyaar velvikudivullar adigalai serththiya paadal
Thanggaiyaal thozhudhu tham naavin mel kolvaar thavaneri sendru amarulagam aalbavare."
Thiruchitrambalam. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal explicitly states in verses 1, 3, 5, and 6 that his disease was cured. Thus, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, having sung the melodious Tamil verses and not forgetting the Lord's grace, resides there with countless devotees. Let us continue to contemplate. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 49 - Yeyar Kon Kalikkaama Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Sekkizhar Peruman's Periya Puranam, Part 887, Vampara Vari Vandu Sarukkam, Eyarkon Kalikkaama Nayanar Puranam. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal resides in Thiruthurutthi. Verse 301: "Panniraindha thamizh paadi paramar thiru arulmaravaa thenniraindha thondarudan panindhu angun uraindhu egi unniraindha padhipiravum udaiyavar thazh vananggi poy kanniraindha thiruvaroor mun thondra kaangindraar enbadhu thiruppaadal." Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, having sung the melodious Tamil Thiruppadhigam, and not forgetting the immense grace the Lord bestowed upon him, worships the Lord with countless devotees. He resides in Thiruthurutthi, praising the Lord, not forgetting His grace, as he sang "Ennai naan marakkumaaru" (How can I forget myself?). He worships the Lord with countless devotees. "Enniraindha" means countless devotees, and also devotees whose minds are filled with Shiva. "Niraithal" means to fill completely, leaving no room for anything else. They are those who always remember only Lord Shiva and nothing else. We saw that Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, after bathing in the northern pond in Thiruthurutthi and being cured of his new disease, did not go directly before the Lord but sang this Thiruppadhigam before the assembled devotees. Therefore, from there, he joins those devotees and then goes before the Lord to worship. This means that he shared his experience with the devotees, praising the Lord's unique grace, and thus experienced the joy with them. This not only informed the devotees of the divine grace but also increased his joy by sharing it with them. The devotees mentioned here are those who accompanied Sundaramoorthy Swamigal and waited there until he bathed in the northern pond and received divine grace. After he returned from bathing, they saw Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's radiant body and worshipped him. Therefore, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal sings to these devotees, saying, "You see how He removed my suffering, how can I forget myself?" He composed that Thiruppadhigam in a way that praises divine grace. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, who was always filled with the Lord in his heart, was commanded by the Lord on the very day he was taken into His service. From that day, he made it his rule to remember and visit all the Lord's holy places and worship them. Such a Sundaramoorthy Swamigal now sees Thiruvarur, which is filled with the Lord, appearing before him, filling his eyes. He sees Thiruvarur appearing before him, filling his eyes. Thiruvarur is a holy place whose beauty is inexhaustible, a sight that once seen, one cannot turn away from. We see Thirunavukkarasu Peruman praising it in various ways as "Ani Aroor" (beautiful Aroor), "Kulir Aroor" (cool Aroor), "Vizh Aroor" (great Aroor), "Valar Aroor" (growing Aroor), and "Than Aroor" (his own Aroor). Sundaramoorthy Swamigal was always immersed in seeing "Solai Arooranai" (Lord of the groves of Aroor), "Pazhana Thiru Ani Arooranai" (Lord of the beautiful Aroor with fertile fields), and "Thamarai Poygai Arooranai" (Lord of Aroor with lotus ponds) in his mind. Now, he sees such a Thiruvarur before his eyes, filling them completely. Verse 302: "Andru thirunokku ondraal aara kandu inburaar nindru nilamisai veezhndhu nedhuyirththu ner irainji van thondar thiruvaroor mayanggum maalaiyil pugundhu thundrusadai thoovaayaar thamai munnam thozha anaindhaar." Sundaramoorthy Swamigal sees Thiruvarur appearing before him. However, he sees that Thiruvarur with only one eye. Even with one eye, he is not completely happy. Standing at the very place where he saw the town, he falls to the ground, sighs deeply, and worships directly. Falling to the ground and worshipping upon seeing a Shiva temple is the custom of Shiva temple darshan. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, having worshipped in this manner, sighs deeply. This sigh is due to his unbearable sorrow. The sigh is because he, who used to see Thiruvarur with both eyes, is now seeing it with only one eye, and his mind is filled with unbearable sorrow. He stands directly and worships the holy place. It is dusk, when the light fades and darkness enters, a time when the eyes are confused. It is a time that causes confusion in the eyes. Therefore, it is called the time when the eyes are confused, and it is also called the time that confuses the time itself. It is the twilight hour when night and day merge. In that twilight hour, he enters the town of Thiruvarur. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal then goes to the Thirupparavaiyul Mandali temple in Thiruvarur. The Lord enshrined in the Aroor Paravaiyul Mandali temple is known by the name "Thoovaayaar." "Thoo" means to eat, and "Vaayaar" means one who has a mouth. So, "Thoovaayaar" means one who has a mouth that eats. This means that when the great flood of the deluge came upon Thiruvarur, the Lord here swallowed that flood, preventing it from spreading over Thiruvarur and submerging it. Therefore, the Lord enshrined here is known by the name "Thoovaayaar," meaning one who has a mouth that eats the flood. "Paravaiyul Mandali" is the name of the temple.

"Paravai" means sea, and "ul" means to eat without overflowing. Therefore, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal begins this Thiruppadhigam with the Lord's name, "Thoovaayaar." Before worshipping the Lord enshrined in the anthill in Thiruvarur, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal goes to the Paravaiyul Mandali temple in Thiruvarur and worships the Lord there. "Thoovaayaar" can also be interpreted differently from the mythological reason mentioned earlier. If "Thoovaayaar" is split as "Thoo Aayaar," it means one who has become a support. "Mananthooymai seyvinai thooymai irandu inam thooymai thoovaavarum" (Purity of mind, purity of action, purity of both lineages will come through purity) is from Thirukkural. Furthermore, "Thoovaayaar" can also mean one who has a mouth that uttered pure Vedic and Sivagama statements. Verse 303: "Ponggu thiruththondarvudan ul anaindhu pokku irainji thunggu isai thiruppadhigam thoovaayaa endru eduththé ingu emadhu thuyarkalaindhu kankana kaattaay endru angganardham munnindru paadi arunthamizh punaindhaar enbadhu thiruppaadal." Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, along with the overflowing devotees, enters the temple, bows before the Lord, and composes a melodious Thiruppadhigam, beginning with "Thoovaayaa." Sundaramoorthy Swamigal sings before the Lord, with the intention of pleading with the Lord to remove his suffering here and to show him his eyes, and thus offers this garland of rare Tamil verses. "Ingu emadhu thuyarkalaindhu kan kana kaattaay" (Remove our suffering here and show us our eyes) is the theme of this Thiruppadhigam. "Angganar" means one who is compassionate. "Angganmai" means compassion. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal is asking for his eyesight, so he sings, referring to the Lord as "Angganare" (O compassionate one), "Kan Udaiyavare" (O one with eyes), and "Kan Thara Vallavare" (O one capable of giving eyes). The Thiruppadhigam composed by Sundaramoorthy Swamigal is for the Thirupparavaiyul Mandali temple in Thiruvarur. It is the 96th Thiruppadhigam in the 7th Thirumurai, set in the Panjamam Pann (melody). The first and tenth verses of this Thiruppadhigam are as follows: Thiruchitrambalam.
"Thoovaayaa thondu seyvaar padu thukkanggal kaavaaye kandu kondaar aivar kaakkilum
Naavaayaal unnaiye nallana solluverku aavaa en paravaiyul mandali ammane.
Karandhaiyum vanniyum maththamum koovizham parandha seer paravaiyul mandali ammanai
Nirambiya oorana uraiththana paththu ivai virumbuvaar melaiyaar melaiyaar melare."
Thiruchitrambalam. Thiruvārūr Paravaiyul Mandali is the 89th temple on the southern bank of the Kaveri in Chola Nadu. It is known as Thulānāyanār Kovil. This temple is located to the southwest of the Thiruvarur temple's Maha Rath (great chariot) level, on the eastern street, to the east of the Thiruvarur temple. At one time, when the sea rose due to Varuna's command over Thiruvarur, the Lord here swallowed that sea and became enshrined, which is why this place is called Paravaiyul Mandali. The name Thoovaayā Nayanār has colloquially become Thulānāyanār. "Mandali" can be split as "Man Thali," meaning a temple that remained steadfast on this earth, not submerged by the sea. This is the place where Durvasa Muni worshipped the Lord and received boons. The idol of Durvasa Muni is enshrined and worshipped in the Vinayagar temple. The Lord's name is Mandaleswarar. The Lord's form shines like crystal. The Goddess's name is Panjinum Melladiyāl (one with feet softer than cotton). The holy waters associated with this place are Kamalalayam Teertham, Sanga Teertham, Gaya Teertham, Vani Teertham, and Sengazhuneer Odai. Behind this temple are the Thiruneelakanda Nayanar temple and, in the southern sanctum, the Paravai Nachiyar temple. These are the details of the Thiruvārūr Paravaiyul Mandali temple. Let us continue to contemplate. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 50 - Yeyar Kon Kalikkaama Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Sekkizhar Peruman's Periya Puranam, Part 888, Vampara Vari Vandu Sarukkam, Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar Puranam. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, having arrived at Thiruvarur Paravaiyul Mandali, is composing a Thiruppathigam (a devotional hymn). The 304th verse states: "Having worshipped the Lord with the matted locks adorned with the Ganga, he came out and stayed elsewhere, joining with the Thiruthondar (devotees) who were with him. He then approached the Thirumoolattanam (the main shrine) of the Lord who bears the bull banner, knowing it was the time for the matchless Arthajama (midnight) worship." Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, having worshipped the Lord whose matted locks are adorned with the Ganga at Thiruvarur Paravaiyul Mandali, came out and stayed for a while in a secluded spot. It is the custom not to enter the temple outside of worship times. Therefore, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal delayed for a short period and entered during the Arthajama worship. The Arthajama puja is a peerless time of worship where boons are granted as desired. Accordingly, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal arrived at that time before the Thirumoolattanam of the Lord Puttidam Kondanathar (Lord who resides in the anthill) inside the Thiruvarur Poongkoil (flower temple).

The 305th and 306th verses are: "As the prime devotees approached him, he looked at their faces, and with flawless music, he composed the Thiruppathigam, 'Kurugupaya,' inquiring as if he were a stranger, and with affection, he sang it in the Kaikkilai (a poetic theme of unrequited love) genre, and then joined them. He went before the magnificent Thirudevasiriyan (a hall in the temple), worshipped, and with folded hands, bowed to the cloud-laden gopuram (temple tower) and entered. He then worshipped the garland-filled Poongkoil and approached it, falling to the ground with immense love." Seeing and hearing of Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's arrival at the Thiruvarur temple, devotees gathered there. All the devotees came forward to greet him. Looking at their faces, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal composed a Thiruppathigam. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal sang the Thiruppathigam beginning with "Kurugupaya" with flawless music. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, standing like a stranger, looked at the faces of those devotees and sang the Thiruppathigam, addressing the devotees as if to ask, "Can you make them understand?" In this Thiruppathigam, he expresses his sorrow, as if asking birds to convey it. "Ethilar" means "stranger." When Sundaramoorthy Swamigal first came to Thiruvarur, he asked the devotees there, "Which of the six towns is he from, and can you tell me about him?" There is a unity in the diversity within that Thiruppathigam. The Thiruppathigam starting with "Kurugupaya," composed here, was also sung addressing the devotees. Here, we see the beauty of Tamil, where unity and diversity are revealed.

The state of mind filled with anxiety due to not receiving the desired object for many days is called "Esaravu." Sundaramoorthy Swamigal sings this Thiruppathigam in the "Kaikkilai" genre, which expresses love through the sorrow of "Esaravu." "Kaikkilai" refers to the state of mind of one-sided love. With such a sentiment, he sings this Thiruppathigam and joins the devotees. This Thiruppathigam is set in the Kollipann (a musical mode). The 1st, 2nd, and 11th verses of this Thiruppathigam are as follows: Thiruchitrambalam. "O you who can make them understand these things: how to drink, worship, and melt with remembrance for the Lord of Arur, where the juice of dense sugarcane flows by the fields, and where the Kurugu (a type of bird) flies!" "O my flying parrots, O my singing mynahs! Can you make them understand that I cannot forget my Lord, who is like a cruel one, that my bangles do not stay, and that I have no sleep?" "O devotees, sing the praises that Uuran (Sundarar), the true devotee of Singadiyappan, whose heart is set on the Lord, the Father who is worshipped and remembered eternally, whose golden feet are praised!" Thiruchitrambalam. Having thus composed the Thiruppathigam, he joined the devotees and entered the temple.

There, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal first worshipped the magnificent Thirudevasiriyan mandapam (hall). Upon entering the Thiruvarur temple courtyard, the first thing one sees is the Devasiriyan mandapam. It is a place filled with gatherings of devotees. Therefore, in accordance with the custom of first worshipping the devotees, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal first worshipped the Thirudevasiriyan mandapam. Having worshipped thus, he then bowed with folded hands to the cloud-laden gopuram and entered. This is the first gopuram of the main temple, after worshipping the Devasiriyan. This is the place where the Lord appeared before Nambigal (Sundarar) and gave him the opening line for the Thiruthondar Thogai (a hymn listing the devotees): "I am a servant to the devotees of Thillai Vaazh Andhanar (Brahmins of Chidambaram)." Having worshipped that gopuram, he entered and reached the Thiruppoongkoil. He worshipped and approached the Thirumaligai (palace) called Poongkoil, which is adorned with many garlands. The Poongkoil is the palace inside the Thiruvarur temple where Puttidam Kondanathar and Thyagesa Peruman reside. There, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal fell to the ground with immense love.

He fell to the ground, overcome by love. It was not a conscious act of falling, but his body falling to the ground due to his overwhelming emotion. The 307th verse states: "Having fallen and risen, he worshipped with folded hands, stood before the Lord, and wept. With one eye, which had lived, he was not satisfied, and his mind was distraught. He pleaded, 'Lift me from the deep ocean of sorrow, and grant me the other eye, for my lowly desire to be fulfilled!'" As mentioned before, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, who fell to the ground, worshipped and stood before the Lord, weeping. He was not satisfied with seeing with only one eye, which had lived, and his mind was distraught. He pleaded, "Lift me from this very deep ocean of sorrow and grant me my eye, so that my lowly desire may be fulfilled!" Sekkizhar Peruman here refers to the left eye as "lived flower-like eye," as Sundaramoorthy Swamigal had received sight in that eye at Thiruvottriyur to see Thiruekambara, as in "How I, a servant, received the eye to see the form of the Lord." Therefore, he yearned for the full vision of the Lord's form. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, with a melting heart, lamented that seeing the Lord with only one eye was not a complete vision. He pleaded with the Lord to lift him from this deep ocean of sorrow, which he could not comprehend, and to forgive his great sin of breaking his vow, and to grant him his eye.

The 308th verse states: "The king of Thirunavalur, Sundarar, worshipped the Lord residing in Thiruvarur, the nectar of Thirumoolattanam, desiring to drink Him with his eyes, and pleaded for the other eye with passionate longing, and then composed a Thiruppathigam beginning with 'Meela Adimai' (unending servitude)." To drink the Lord, who is un-drunk sweet nectar, with his eyes, he pleaded for the sight of his right eye, in addition to the left eye he had received in Kanchipuram, and then composed a Thiruppathigam beginning with "Meela Adimai." The 309th verse states: "Beginning with 'Meela Adimai,' he sang a fearless Thiruppathigam, in the spirit of servitude and friendship, saying, 'O Lord, who consumed poison to save all the hosts of devas from perishing, and remained steadfast, you will not bear the suffering that comes to your true devotees who lovingly cherish your feet.'" Sundaramoorthy Swamigal worshipped the Lord, who, beginning with "Meela Adimai," had consumed poison to save all the hosts of devas from perishing and remained steadfast. He sang a beautiful Thiruppathigam with the spirit of servitude and friendship, implying, "You will not bear the suffering that comes to your true devotees who lovingly cherish your feet." Although it is the Lord who, according to destiny, causes suffering to His devotees for the purification of their karma, and even though that suffering is caused by Him, He shows compassion, unable to bear the suffering of His devotees when they experience it. Thus, he composed the Thiruppathigam beginning with "Meela Adimai," combining the two qualities of servitude and friendship. The nature of the verses, such as "Vaazhndhu Potheere" (may you live long) and "Neere Pazhippatteer" (you are blamed), are parts where Sundaramoorthy Swamigal expressed both his servitude and his friendship with the Lord.

Thus, the Thiruppathigam composed by Sundaramoorthy Swamigal is a Thiruppathigam for the Thiruvarur shrine. It is the 95th Thiruppathigam in the 7th Thirumurai, set in the Senturutthi pann (a musical mode). This Thiruppathigam is as follows: Thiruchitrambalam. "O Lord of Thiruvarur, if we, your servants, who are your unending slaves and desire no one else, whose hearts burn like an unquenchable fire within, and whose faces are greatly withered, tell you our suffering, will you live on untouched?" "You sell, I am not a pawn, I willingly became your servant. I have done no wrong. You have made me a wretch. Why did you take my eyes, O Lord? You are blamed! If you do not give me the other eye, may you live on untouched!" "O Lord of Arur, where the Anril (a bird) does not cease to reach the groves! Like cows that yield milk when their calves butt them, if your servants, who sing praises eternally, cannot see, and fall into a pit after hitting a hill, may you live on untouched!" "O dweller of Thurutthi, O Lord of Pazhanam, O dweller of Sootrutthurai! You have Thiruvarur as your abode, and your mind is there. If your loving servants tell you their suffering, and you cause them distress and then command them to serve in the next life, may you live on untouched!" "Is this Thiruvarur, with groves where red corals shine? Is this how it is for us, your servants, O my Lord? If your servants, who sing many praises, cannot see, and cry out, 'Is this justice, O my Lord?' may you live on untouched!" "O Lord with the matted locks adorned with the Konrai (golden shower tree) garland, like gold, in Thiruvarur, where the red-legged crane reaches the rice stalks! If your servants, without wealth, become weak and cannot see, and their minds are distressed, may you live on untouched!" "O Lord of Arur, where the female elephant reaches the groves! Is this how it is for us, your servants, O Lord? If you show illusion, show birth, show an unforgetting mind, show the body, and then take away our eyes, may you live on untouched!" "We were born in a family that is like a toddy, like an ocean, like a vessel, like the earth, and like mingled words, and we praise you without despising you. O Lord, you do not know blame! If we, your singing devotees, wander without finding our way, may you live on untouched!" "They say that even separation from a ghost is unpleasant for others. If you desire the fruit, is it not the raw fruit that is considered? Even though we wander like dogs in the middle, and are your servants, if you do not open your mouth, O Lord of Thiruvarur, may you live on untouched!" "Is this Thiruvarur, with groves where red Serundhi (a type of flower) blossoms? You have taken Thirumoolattanam as your abode. We praise you without despising you, whether sitting, standing, or lying down. If we, who have come with suffering, tell you something, may you live on untouched!" "O Lord with the dark throat, eight shoulders, three eyes, and many arts, whose feet are in Thirumoolattanam of Arur! You took my eyes, O Lord! You are blamed! You who have the part of the breasted woman, may you live on untouched!" Thiruchitrambalam. This Thiruppathigam is special because it comes from true experience. Sivakavimani Desikan has said that he knows people who have regained their sight by reciting this. Sekkizhar Peruman then continues to describe the divine miracle that occurred after this Thiruppathigam was composed.

The 310th verse states: "The holy Lord, Puttidam Kondanathar, the Lord of the elements, took pity on the suffering of Vanthondar (Sundarar), which arose from love, and cast his merciful divine gaze. When he granted the cool, flower-like eye, Sundarar opened his eyes clearly, his face blossomed, and he fell at the Lord's lotus feet, his heart overwhelmed with bliss." The Lord Puttidam Kondanathar, the Lord of the elements, took pity on the suffering of Vanthondar (Sundarar), which arose from his incessant loving plea, and cast his merciful divine gaze. When he granted the cool, flower-like eye, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal opened his eyes clearly, his face blossomed, and he fell at the Lord's lotus feet, his heart overwhelmed with bliss. The Lord, who is gracious, had previously caused Nambigal to lose his eye, which was also an act of divine grace, but it was a harsh grace. Now, the Lord's action was a compassionate grace.

The 311th and 312th verses are: "Falling and rising many times, he worshipped and praised greatly. Overwhelmed with joy, he danced and sang, immersed in a flood of bliss. With both eyes, he drank the grace of the rich, clear coral-like form that arose from the golden anthill. Having worshipped and praised for a sufficient time, he circumambulated the golden jeweled temple, out of his good desire, for the welfare of the world, and then, with the virtuous devotees, approached the Thirudevasiriyan, passing through the gate where Brahma and Vishnu await their turn." Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, falling to the ground and rising many times, performed various prostrations (Ashtanga, Panchanga, Thrayanga), sang praises, and was immersed in a flood of bliss. Earlier, he had seen with one eye and was not satisfied. Now, having received the other eye, he drank the Lord with both eyes. He drank the un-drunk sweet nectar with his eyes, thereby immersing himself in the experience of bliss and reveling in it. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal had arrived for the Arthajama worship. Therefore, he continued to praise the Lord until the Arthajama worship ended. After the Arthajama worship, the temple doors would be closed. So, before that, he circumambulated the temple and came out. He came out through the first gate. This is the place where the Lord appeared to Nambigal and gave him the opening line for the Thiruthondar Thogai. This place is called Kodinidam or Ottravayil.

It is the place where the devas (celestial beings) wait, observing the time, to present their grievances. Passing through that gate, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, with the virtuous devotees, reached the side of the Thirudevasiriyan mandapam. He is Nambi, who came for the welfare of the world, so that the southern land, which had performed great penance, might flourish, and the flawless Thiruthondar Thogai might be given. This Nambi, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, who came for the welfare of the world, is now residing beside the Thirudevasiriyan mandapam. We will continue to contemplate. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 51 - Yeyar Kon Kalikkaama Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Sekkizhar Peruman's Periya Puranam, Part 889, Vampara Vari Vandu Sarukkam, Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar Puranam. Sekkizhar Peruman has sung the history of Sundaramoorthy Swamigal receiving his right eye and residing in the Thiruvarur temple. Now, Sekkizhar Peruman sings the continuation of Paravai Ammaiyar's history. Earlier, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal had departed from Thiruvarur to undertake pilgrimages to various temples. Therefore, Paravai Nachiyar experienced sorrow due to separation from Sundaramoorthy Swamigal. In addition to this, Paravai Ammaiyar received another piece of distressing news: Sundaramoorthy Swamigal had married Sangili Ammaiyar in Thiruvottriyur. Therefore, Sekkizhar Peruman will now sing the history, starting from how Paravai Ammaiyar was in distress due to her sulk (oodal) caused by these two sorrows.

Sekkizhar Peruman continues to sing about the history of the Lord going as a messenger twice to appease Paravai Ammaiyar's sulk towards Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, and the history of Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar, which follows this narrative. The 313th verse: "After Nambi (Sundaramoorthy Swamigal) departed, leaving Nankai Paravaiyar (Paravai Ammaiyar), she was weakened by loneliness in her jeweled palace. Days passed, with nights becoming days and days becoming nights, as her overflowing love caused her to wither. After some days had passed..." Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, having departed, Paravai Ammaiyar is weakened by loneliness in her beautiful palace. Due to her loneliness, Paravai Ammaiyar is in distress. All those times, the daytime passes like nighttime, and the nighttime passes like daytime. Due to her separation from Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, she cannot sleep, and thus night becomes day. Tamil literature, in the context of Akapporul (love poetry), speaks of the sorrow that comes at night, such as being associated with the sea and suffering when the moon appears. How does day become night? Without the presence of her beloved, the daytime loses its splendor and light, becoming dark. She does not have the usual human excitement during the day. The thoughts of union and weariness that belong to the night occur during the day for Paravai Ammaiyar, and the wakefulness and going out that belong to the day occur during the night. Therefore, night becomes day, and day becomes night. Thus, the days pass without her realizing the difference between day and night, as she suffers from loneliness and distress. Her overflowing love causes her to wither in distress. The 314th verse: "The truthful news that Thirunavalurar (Sundaramoorthy Swamigal), who is steadfast in righteousness, went to Thiruvottriyur and married Sangiliyar, the woman with full breasts, was conveyed to her by those who came to her." The sorrow caused by Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's departure has been explained so far. Now, Sekkizhar Peruman explains another sorrow that comes to Paravai Ammaiyar. It is the news that Sundaramoorthy Swamigal is properly married to Sangili Ammaiyar in Thiruvottriyur and living there. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, who followed the path of Shiva, a path of righteousness, having received the grace of penance from the Lord, was told by some people that he had properly married Sangili Ammaiyar in Thiruvottriyur. When some people first conveyed this news, Paravai Ammaiyar asked them to investigate the truth of this news. They went to Thiruvottriyur, saw Sundaramoorthy Swamigal and Sangili Nachiyar living as husband and wife, and confirmed to Paravai Ammaiyar that the news she had already received was true. We also see this in the story of Karaikkal Ammaiyar. Worldly wisdom states that one should not simply believe what others say, but should send some trusted people to investigate and confirm the truth, and then proceed with what needs to be done. When Paravai Ammaiyar confirmed the truth of this news, anger came to her, without her even realizing it. It was an anger that arose from her feminine nature. It was not an anger that Paravai Ammaiyar intentionally created. Moral texts state that chaste women should not be angry with their husbands. Therefore, it is not appropriate to be angry with her husband. However, here, anger comes to Paravai Ammaiyar without her even realizing it. It was an anger that could not be distinguished from her sulk. This is because it was an anger that arose from her inability to tolerate that the joy of her beloved, who belonged only to her, was now shared with another. Therefore, unable to bear that anger, Paravai Ammaiyar's body weakens.

In the 315th verse, Sekkizhar Peruman explains how Paravaiyar's actions were when her body was weakened. "She would not sleep on the soft flower bed, nor would she sit comfortably after waking up. She would not sit on the golden flower throne, nor would she stand, nor would she walk around, nor would she go outside. She would not forget the flower-like rain, nor would she remember it. What would she do? She was caught between a sulk that melted her bones and separation." Due to that anger, Paravai Ammaiyar could not sleep on the flower bed. Even though it was a flower bed, Paravai Ammaiyar could not sleep. Those who are weakened usually lie down and sleep. By sleeping, the body rests, and then the weakness disappears, and they regain strength. This is generally what medical texts say. But here, the weakened Paravaiyar cannot sleep. Well, if she cannot sleep, even if she rests on the bed for a while, her body's weakness will disappear. But Paravai Ammaiyar's mind is withered and her heart is burning, so she cannot even sit on the flower bed. Sekkizhar Peruman sings, "Inithu Amarar" (She does not sit comfortably). Well, if she cannot lie down comfortably on the bed, she can sit on the flower throne for a while. But she cannot even sit on the flower throne. Well, if she cannot sit on the flower throne, can she stand up? She cannot even stand up. Well, if she can walk around inside the palace, she cannot even walk around. Can she go outside the palace and change her mind? She cannot even do that. Can she forget Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, who is the cause of all these states? She cannot forget him. Due to anger, his memory cannot be removed. Can she remember Sundaramoorthy Swamigal? Due to anger, she cannot even remember him. Therefore, Paravai Ammaiyar's mind is neither remembering nor forgetting. Therefore, when she cannot do any work, either outwardly or inwardly, what else can Paravai Ammaiyar do? Paravai Ammaiyar was caught between the two states of sulk and separation. This state of being caught between these two states is a unique state where she is neither sulking nor separated, but suffering. It is a state where she cannot be separated due to anger, and she cannot even remember in her mind. It is a unique state between these two. Her state is one that melts the bones. Thus, Sekkizhar Peruman sings this verse. This verse beautifully explains the state of mind of one who is separated, and the state of mind of a heroine who hears that her beloved has married another woman. It is a very high-quality Akapporul (love poetry) verse with a beautiful narrative. We will continue to contemplate. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 52 - Yeyar Kon Kalikkaama Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Sekkizhar Peruman's Periya Puranam, Part 890, Vampara Vari Vandu Sarukkam, Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar Puranam. We have contemplated Sekkizhar Peruman's portrayal of Paravai Ammaiyar's situation. The 316th verse: "As she was distraught with sorrow and helplessness, in those days, Aaroorar (Sundaramoorthy Swamigal), whose head was adorned with the crescent moon, approached the temple. The attendants, who used to go to the palace of the doe-eyed lady, were not allowed to enter and remained outside." As Paravai Ammaiyar was thus distraught with sorrow and helplessness, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, having now received his right eye, was residing before the Poongkoil (flower temple) of the Thiruvarur temple. At the same time, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's attendants went to Paravai Ammaiyar's palace. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's attendants went to Paravai Ammaiyar's palace as usual. However, they were not allowed to enter Paravai Ammaiyar's palace. So, they remained outside. We saw that Sundaramoorthy Swamigal arrived at the Thiruvarur temple during the Artha Jama Puja (midnight worship). Therefore, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal entered Thiruvarur in the evening and arrived at the Thiruvarur temple for Artha Jama. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's attendants also stayed with him for his service until the Artha Jama time. After the Artha Jama Puja was completed and everyone in the town was asleep, the attendants went to Paravai Ammaiyar's palace after delaying for so long.

Paravai Ammaiyar had learned about the events that had taken place in Thiruvottriyur. Therefore, the attendants, having somewhat guessed Paravai Ammaiyar's changed mood, delayed until everyone in the town was asleep and then went to Paravai Ammaiyar's palace. The Thiruthondars (devotees) are always with Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, but the attendants do not always need to be with him. Their job is to perform the necessary services for Sundaramoorthy Swamigal. So, now they go to Paravai Ammaiyar's palace, and since they are not allowed to enter, they stand outside the palace. These attendants, who have the great privilege of serving Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, are not like other attendants in the world. Such glorious attendants are standing outside Paravai Ammaiyar's palace. The 317th verse: "Some of them, seeing their situation, went before the Lord of Thiruvarur and reported, 'Paravai Ammaiyar has learned all the events that took place in Thiruvottriyur. Therefore, we were not allowed to enter her palace and are standing outside.'" The attendants stood before Paravai Ammaiyar's palace, and some of them left and went to Sundaramoorthy Swamigal to inform him of the situation. They told Sundaramoorthy Swamigal that Paravai Ammaiyar had learned all the events that took place in Thiruvottriyur, and that they were not allowed to enter her palace and were standing outside.

The 318th verse: "Hearing that news, Vanthondar (Sundaramoorthy Swamigal), with a distraught mind, wondered, 'What can be done now?' He then sent some learned people, who understood the ways of the world, to Paravaiyar, his beloved, to appease her anger with appropriate words." Hearing this news, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's mind was distraught. He wondered what he should do now. He called some wise people who had learned the ways of the world and understood worldly justice. He asked them to go to Paravaiyar, his beloved, and, knowing her anger, to appease it with good words. These people were knowledgeable about worldly affairs, experienced, and capable of performing tasks. They knew how to approach different people in different situations and how to achieve their goals. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal sent such people to Paravai Ammaiyar. The 319th verse: "Those who went by Nambi's (Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's) grace reached Nankai Paravaiyar's beautiful golden palace. With a manner that would appease her anger, they approached the lightning-waisted lady, who was immersed in the ocean of intense sulk, and said, 'Is this befitting our Queen?' and spoke many words." Those people who went by Nambi's grace reached Nankai Paravaiyar's beautiful palace. They went there with the intention of appeasing her anger. Paravai Ammaiyar was immersed in the ocean of separation, which caused her mind to burn. Those people went before Paravai Ammaiyar and said, "Is this befitting our Queen?" and spoke many words. They addressed Paravai Ammaiyar as "Our Queen," praising her greatness and right, and tried to appease her anger.

This is one of the methods used by learned people. This is called the Sama Upaya (method of conciliation). Among the four Upayas (methods) of Sama, Bheda, Dana, and Danda, this is called Sama Upaya. They spoke such words. The 320th verse: "When they spoke many more words with the method of differentiation, the lady refused and did not change her mind from the anger she held. She said, 'If you speak further about his faults, my life will surely depart.' They too became afraid and left." First, those wise people spoke about the Sama Upaya. After that, they adopted the Bheda (differentiation) method. This is the second type of Upaya. They adopted the Bheda Upaya and spoke in many ways to appease Paravai Ammaiyar's anger. When the Sama Upaya did not work, they resorted to the Bheda Upaya. We see these methods in the Ambuil Paruva (moon section) of Pillaitamil (a genre of Tamil literature). Even though they spoke all these words, Paravai Ammaiyar refused their statements and did not change her mind from the anger she held. Paravai Ammaiyar said to those who came, "If you speak further about his (Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's) faults, my life will surely depart." When she said this, those who came became afraid. We saw that they only used the first two Upayas among the four types of Sama, Bheda, Dana, and Danda. The Upayas used to unite separated lovers are only the first two. Therefore, when they used both and it did not work, Paravai's anger increased, and she said, "If you speak about Sundaramoorthy Swamigal anymore, my life will depart." So, they became afraid and went to Sundaramoorthy Swamigal and told him everything that had happened.

The 321st verse: "When they spoke, he was filled with fear and weariness. He could not find a companion to help him. In his anxious heart, he was immersed in sorrow and could not rise. In the midst of that night, in that ocean of sorrow, the elders who had departed from there reported everything that had happened to Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, the leader of Thirumunaippadi Nadu." When they spoke, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal was filled with fear and weariness. He could not find a companion to help him in that ocean of worry. He was immersed in great sorrow. That anxiety burned Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's heart. "Sindha Kulam" means "Sindhai Aakulam," and "Aakulam" means sorrow. Therefore, "Sindha Kulam" means mental anguish. So, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal was in that ocean of sorrow with mental anguish, unable to find a companion to help him out of that ocean. He was immersed in great sorrow in the middle of that night, that is, midnight. The 322nd and 323rd verses: "Those around him slept. The Artha Jama worship was completed. In the deep darkness, where the world was silent and even ghosts slept, the Lord, whose head was adorned with fragrant Konrai flowers and who wore the crescent moon and a snake, his friend, sat alone, grieving and contemplating. He thought of the Lord's feet, saying, 'Oh, my Lord, I am caught in this trouble, which is a result of my past karma, and Paravai is the cause of it. You must come and save me in this hour. If you come and remove the sulk of that swan-like woman, I will be saved. Otherwise, there is nothing else I can do.'" In that night, everyone around Sundaramoorthy Swamigal was sleeping. It was the time when the Artha Jama Puja was completed. So, it was a time when all the people of the world had stopped their activities and were sleeping. It was a deep dark night when even ghosts slept. The Artha Jama worship in the temple and the daily worship rituals associated with it were all completed, and the temple was closed. So, those who were with Sundaramoorthy Swamigal were also sleeping. It was past midnight. It was a time when even the night, which usually moves, was still. At that time, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal was sitting alone, grieving and contemplating. At that moment, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, the friend of the Lord, who wore the fragrant Konrai flowers on his head and had a snake and the crescent moon, was thinking of the Lord's feet. He thought, "Oh, my Lord, who owns me! You must grant me grace so that I can reach Paravai, who is the cause of this trouble, which is a result of my past karma. If you come in this midnight and remove the sulk of that swan-like woman, I will be saved. Otherwise, there is nothing else I can do." We will continue to contemplate. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 53 - Yeyar Kon Kalikkaama Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Sekkizhar Peruman's Periya Puranam, Part 891, Vampara Vari Vandu Sarukkam, Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar Puranam. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, in the middle of this night, prays to Lord Shiva. He thinks of the Lord's feet, saying, "If you come and remove Paravaiyar's anger, I will be saved. Otherwise, there is nothing else I can do. There is no way for me to be saved." As soon as Sundaramoorthy Swamigal thought of the Lord's feet, the Lord, who cannot bear the suffering of his devotees, appeared to Sundaramoorthy Swamigal. The 324th verse: "The Lord, who cannot bear the suffering of his devotees, and who would not leave his chosen friend's (Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's) wish unfulfilled, appeared before his devotee, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, with the marks of bangles and breast prints on his golden, tender, sprout-like hand, and with feet that even Vishnu could not see, touching the ground." The Lord, who cannot bear the suffering of his devotees, would he not fulfill the wish of Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, his chosen friend? Certainly not. Therefore, the Lord, who has the marks of bangles and breast prints on his beautiful, tender, sprout-like hand, which is the hand of Kamakshi Ammaiyar, and whose feet even Vishnu could not see, appeared before his devotee, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, with those feet touching the ground. The reason for granting such a vision to his devotee, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, is explained in the first part of this verse, which Sekkizhar Peruman has composed as his own statement: "The Lord, who cannot bear the suffering of his devotees, and who would not leave his chosen friend's wish unfulfilled." This part is Sekkizhar Peruman's own poetic statement. The truth that "Sivan knows those who love him" is explained here, as stated in the Gnana Sastras (scriptures of wisdom). Such a Lord, with great compassion, appeared before Sundaramoorthy Swamigal. The 325th verse: "Seeing the Lord appear, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, with unbearable joy, trembled in all his limbs, his hair stood on end with ecstasy. Nambi Aaroorar (Sundaramoorthy Swamigal), with his lotus-like hands folded over his head, fell at the red lotus feet of the Lord, the husband of Ambika." Seeing the Lord appear, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal was filled with unbearable joy. Due to this unbearable joy, all of Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's limbs trembled, and his hair stood on end with ecstasy. Nambi Aaroorar, with his lotus-like hands folded over his head, fell on the ground at the red lotus feet of the Lord, who has Ambika as a part of him. Now, the Lord speaks to Sundaramoorthy Swamigal. The 326th verse: "The Lord, seeing his friend who had fallen and praised him with great devotion, asked, 'What is your sorrow?' Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, bowing, explained his plight, saying, 'I am caught in a fault that I myself initiated, and I am suffering. You must still lift me up and save me.'" The Lord, seeing his friend who had fallen and praised him with great devotion, asked, "What is your sorrow?" Would the Lord, who knows all the inner feelings of Nambigal, not know Nambigal's sorrow? However, the Lord asked this for the sake of worldly understanding. When the Lord asked this, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, bowing to the Lord, explained his plight. He said, "Oh, Lord, I am suffering, caught in a fault that I myself initiated. You must still lift me up from that fault and save me. That is your duty." Sundaramoorthy Swamigal asks the Lord to save him from his suffering, which arose from his love for Sangiliyar, the vow he made, and his breaking of that vow, just as the Lord had saved him before.

Sundaramoorthy Swamigal further says in the 327th verse: "Oh, Lord, you yourself granted me grace in Thiruvottriyur, and I married Sangili, the woman with beautiful eyes. Paravai, the slender-waisted lady, has learned all this and has decided that if I approach her, she will die. What can I do?" Sundaramoorthy Swamigal says to Lord Shiva, "Oh, Lord, you yourself granted me grace in Thiruvottriyur, and I married Sangili. Paravai has now learned all these things and has decided that if I approach her, she will die. Therefore, I do not know what to do." Sundaramoorthy Swamigal pleads with the Lord, implying that since all this suffering came about due to his grace, it is his duty to resolve it. He further pleads. The 328th verse: "Sundaramoorthy Swamigal said to the Lord, 'Oh, Lord, if I am your servant here, and if you are my Lord, a better friend than a mother, then seeing my distraught and weary state, you must go to Paravai's palace this very night and appease her sulk.'" Sundaramoorthy Swamigal says to the Lord, "Oh, Lord," addressing him as "My Master." He says, "If I am your servant here, and if you are my Lord, a better friend than a mother, then seeing my distraught and weary state, you must go to Paravai's palace this very night and appease her anger." Sundaramoorthy Swamigal says, "If it is true that I am your servant, and if it is true that you are my Lord, a better friend than a mother, and if it is true that you are my Master, and also my friend, then you must appease Paravai's sulk this very night." If I say, "If I am your servant," it is enough, because it will be clear that the Lord is his Master. But why does Sundaramoorthy Swamigal say again, "If it is true that you are my Master"? This is because Sundaramoorthy Swamigal was forcibly stopped and accepted as a servant by the Lord himself. Thus, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal speaks in both ways. Can one plead with the Lord in this way, asking him to appease his wife's anger? The answer is that the Lord is both his Master and his friend. Therefore, the right and justification for making this plea and for the Lord to fulfill it are mentioned here, regarding the Lord being both Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's Master and his friend. Therefore, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal expresses all the suffering he is experiencing and pleads with the Lord to go to Paravaiyar's palace this very night and appease Paravaiyar's anger. Now, the Lord grants grace to Sundaramoorthy Swamigal. The 329th verse: "The Lord, who desires only love from his devotees, looked at Nambi (Sundaramoorthy Swamigal), who had stood before him and made a plea, desiring what his devotee desired. He said, 'Cast away your sorrow. We will go as your messenger to Paravai, adorned with golden jewels, this very moment.'" The Lord desires only love from his devotees. Therefore, the Lord, the spiritual master of Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, who desires only love, desired what his devotee, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, desired. The Lord is free from desires and aversions. He desires only love from his devotees for his grace to flow. The Lord will desire what his loving devotees desire. The Thiruvachagam verse that begins with "Vendathakkathu Arivoyini" (You know what is to be desired) beautifully illustrates this. To fulfill the desires of his devotees, the Lord will take them into his heart. If the devotee's desire is aligned with the Lord's grace, then that desire will be fulfilled immediately. This is the teaching of Thiruvachagam. But here, the method and situation are different. The Lord, having decided to fulfill the devotee's desire, himself desired it. This is the idea here. Therefore, this explains the intensity of the devotee's servitude and the Lord's eagerness to fulfill the devotee's wish. Therefore, the Lord grants grace to Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, saying, "Cast away your sorrow. We will go as your messenger to Paravai this very moment."

In response to Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's plea to appease Paravaiyar's anger this very night, the Lord grants grace, saying, "We will go as your messenger this very moment." The 330th verse: "Vanthondar (Sundaramoorthy Swamigal), filled with boundless joy, fell at the Lord's feet, rose, praised him as much as he could, and stood in bliss. He then bowed and pleaded, 'Oh, Lord, go quickly to Paravai, whose teeth are like jasmine buds, and remove her sulk from her cloud-reaching palace.'" When the Lord granted grace, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal was filled with boundless joy. He fell at the Lord's feet, rose, praised the Lord as much as he could, and stood in bliss. Vanthondar, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, again pleaded with the Lord, saying, "Oh, Lord, go quickly and remove Paravai's sulk." Sundaramoorthy Swamigal was filled with boundless joy because he concluded that since the Lord himself had decided to go as a messenger, Paravaiyar's anger would surely be appeased. As it is said, "If the first one is present, is there anything impossible?" Since the Lord had decided, he was certain that the task would be successfully completed. Due to this certainty, he considered his request fulfilled and was immersed in boundless joy. He again bowed to the Lord. We will continue to contemplate. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 54 - Yeyar Kon Kalikkaama Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Sekkizhar Peruman's Periya Puranam, Part 892, Vampara Vari Vandu Sarukkam, Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar Puranam. The Lord goes as a messenger to Paravai Ammaiyar for Sundaramoorthy Swamigal. Sekkizhar Peruman describes in great detail how the entire Sivalogam (abode of Shiva) appears to be present in the Thiruvithi (street) of Thiruvarur as the Lord goes as a messenger. He continuously composes six verses in this section: 330, 331, 332, 333, 334, 335, and 336. "To save the gods, with compassion, he consumed the poison as nectar, the blue-throated one, unknown to one and two. As he went as a messenger to Paravaiyar's palace, whose hair was adorned with flowers where bees resided, to remove the sorrow of his devotee, all the Devas (gods) who were waiting in the Devasiriyan (hall for devotees) worshipped and went. Some who wished to go went, while others remained outside. The Bhoothagananathars (leaders of Shiva's attendants), the immortal sages, and the foremost among the yogis, who were always in close service, went ahead. The Rishis (sages) who came with the great Deva (Nandi) nearby, and Kubera (the god of wealth) and others, praised him joyfully. The streets and the sky were filled with fragrant, rich flowers showering down. As the messenger, sent by the incomparable devotee, walked in the holy street, it was as if the Lord's anklets jingled, calling out to Vishnu and Brahma, 'This is the time to come and worship the lotus feet of the Lord, whom you could not see.' The Lord, whose throat was dark with the poison from the sea, his anklets jingling on his lotus feet, proceeded directly towards Paravaiyar's palace, whose eyes were like blue flowers. As those who had arrived followed him, the snake followed in his matted locks, which contained the roaring, surging waters. The young crescent moon, with its white light, followed near the fragrant Konrai flowers, where bees followed. The Vedas followed, and Vanthondar's (Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's) mind also followed. As he came, the street was filled with the sound of the great elephant, which was louder than the sea. The Lord, who wore the skin of the elephant he had flayed, was surrounded by those who followed him. Thus, in one street of Thiruvarur, the wealthy town where the beautiful Lord rejoices, the entire Sivalogam could be seen." These are the six verses. Let us look at them one by one. The 331st verse: "To save the gods, with compassion, he consumed the poison as nectar, the blue-throated one, unknown to one and two. As he went as a messenger to Paravaiyar's palace, whose hair was adorned with flowers where bees resided, to remove the sorrow of his devotee." The Lord, who, out of compassion for the gods, consumed the Halahala poison as nectar, and thus became blue-throated, is Lord Shiva. He is the one who is unknown to the two, Brahma and Vishnu. His neck is blue due to the poison he consumed. He consumed the very deadly poison as nectar and held it in his throat. Similarly, he goes as a messenger to Paravai Ammaiyar. Worldly people would consider going as a messenger to be a lowly act. But the Lord undertook even this act, which worldly people would consider lowly. Just as he consumed the deadly poison as nectar and held it in his throat, he also undertook this act of being a messenger, which worldly people would consider lowly. This is the implication of the verse. It is because of this act that Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar becomes angry in this Puranam (story) of Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar. We see here that this story arose from that. Sekkizhar Peruman praises that throat, which held the poison, as "Kolamitaru" (beautiful throat). "Kolam" means beauty. What kind of beauty is that blue throat? It is the beauty that saves lives. Such a peerless one, who is unknown to the two, goes as a messenger. He goes as a messenger, in accordance with the statement, "We will go as your messenger." Why? To remove the sorrow of his devotee. The Lord is the one who has Sundaramoorthy Swamigal as his devotee. Therefore, it is the Lord's duty to remove his sorrow. So, we see in this verse the beauty of the idea that the Lord, who consumed poison when the gods pleaded, would not come as a messenger when his devotees pleaded. Now, the next verse. The 332nd verse: "All the Devas who were waiting in the Devasiriyan (hall for devotees) worshipped and went. Some who wished to go went, while others remained outside. The Bhoothagananathars (leaders of Shiva's attendants), the immortal sages, and the foremost among the yogis, who were always in close service, went ahead." In the Thiruvarur temple, at the entrance of the Devasiriyan Mandapam, the Devas always wait, seeking boons. Among those Devas, only some go with the Lord. The others remain outside. This means that those who are worthy of asking boons from the Lord go with him. The others, who are not worthy, remain there without going with him. The Bhoothagananathars, the leaders of Shiva's attendants, who are always in close service to the Lord, go with him. The immortal sages, who have attained immortality through yogic practices, also go with the Lord.

Now, the 333rd verse: "The Rishis (sages) who came with the great Deva (Nandi) nearby, and Kubera (the god of wealth) and others, praised him joyfully. The streets and the sky were filled with fragrant, rich flowers showering down. As the messenger, sent by the incomparable devotee, walked in the holy street." The Lord goes as a messenger. The Rishis who came with Nandi Peruman (the great bull-faced attendant of Shiva) also go with the Lord. Kubera and others, who are friends of the Lord, praise him joyfully. In this verse, "Periyadevar" refers to Thirunandi Peruman. He is the leader of all the Sivaganas (attendants of Shiva) and also the chief of the temple. "Nanbu Nidikkone" in this verse refers to Kubera. He is said to be a friend of Lord Shiva. As all these people go with him, the Thiruvithi of Thiruvarur and the sky are filled with fragrant, rich flowers showering down. The Lord, the messenger sent by the incomparable devotee, walks in the holy street of Thiruvarur. The 334th verse: "It was as if the Lord's anklets jingled, calling out to Vishnu and Brahma, 'This is the time to come and worship the lotus feet of the Lord, whom you could not see.' The Lord, whose throat was dark with the poison from the sea, his anklets jingling on his lotus feet, proceeded directly towards Paravaiyar's palace, whose eyes were like blue flowers." It was as if the Lord's anklets jingled, calling out to Vishnu and Brahma, "This is the time to come and worship the lotus feet of the Lord, whom you could not see." The lotus feet of the Lord, which even Vishnu and Brahma could not see when they searched for them, can now be easily seen, praised, and worshipped if they come here now, at this time, when the Lord is walking as a messenger in the Thiruvithi of Thiruvarur.

Such is the time. Sekkizhar Peruman sings thus. The sound of the anklets helps souls to attain liberation by bringing them closer to the feet of the Lord. Therefore, Sekkizhar Peruman sings thus. Such is the Lord. "This is the time, so make love," says Thiruvachagam, Thiruppandi. As the Lord's anklets jingled, the Lord, whose throat was dark with the poison that arose from the sea, proceeded directly towards Paravai Ammaiyar's palace, whose eyes were like blue flowers. The 335th verse: "As the Lord quickly proceeded, those who had arrived followed him. The snake followed in his matted locks, which contained the roaring, surging waters. The young crescent moon, with its white light, followed near the fragrant Konrai flowers, where bees followed. The Vedas followed, and Vanthondar's (Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's) mind also followed." As the Lord proceeded in the Thiruvithi of Thiruvarur, this verse describes those who followed him and the things that followed him. Those who were mentioned in the verses followed the Lord. As they followed him, the snakes followed in the Lord's matted locks, which contained the roaring, surging waters of the Ganga. Near the young crescent moon on the Lord's matted locks, the bees followed in the honey-filled Konrai garland. The Vedas also followed. Along with these, Vanthondar's mind also followed. The Vedas followed the Lord because they contained the Lord within them. Vanthondar's mind also followed the Lord. Although Vanthondar's body remained near the Devasiriyan Mandapam, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's entire mind followed the Lord. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's mind, his inner self, was always united with the Lord in a state of Sivayoga (union with Shiva), never separating from him. Even though his mind was thus united without separation, here, as if acting in a worldly play, the Lord proceeds, and Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's mind follows. The 336th verse: "The Lord, who flayed the hide of the elephant, which roared louder than the great sea, and wore its skin, was surrounded by those who followed him as described before. As they crowded together, in one street of Thiruvarur, the wealthy town where the beautiful Lord rejoices, the entire Sivalogam could be seen." Our Lord, who flayed the hide of the elephant, which roared louder than the great sea, and wore its skin, was surrounded by all those who followed him as described before. As they crowded together, in one street of Thiruvarur, the wealthy town where the beautiful Lord rejoices, the entire Sivalogam could be seen. Since all the inhabitants of Sivalogam were seen in this street, it appeared like Sivalogam. Even though all of them surrounded him, they only remained up to the street. The Lord alone, in the form of a Sivachariyar (priest of Shiva), is now about to enter the palace. This is the first time the Lord appears in this form. He will appear a second time. At that time, the Lord will appear in his true form, surrounded by his ganas (attendants), and the palace of Paravaiyar will appear like the Kailash, the silver mountain. Sekkizhar Peruman will sing about that there. So, now, as the Lord proceeds in the street, the street appears like Sivalogam. Now, our Lord, as Nambi's messenger, is about to go to Paravaiyar's palace gate. We will continue to contemplate. Thiruchitrambalam.

Thiruchitrambalam. Sekkizhar Peruman's Periya Puranam, Part 893, Vampara Vari Vandu Sarukkam, Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar Puranam.

Chapter 55 - Yeyar Kon Kalikkaama Nayanar

"Gnanam uyya ezhundharulum Nambi thoodhar Paravaiyar kolamani maligai vayil kuruguvar mun kooda thambal angu anaindhar puram nirpa pande thammai archikkum seelamudaiya maraimunivaragi thaniye sendru anaindhar Sundaramoorthy Swamigal." Sekkizhar Peruman states that Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, who incarnated to save this world, was born to bestow the blemishless Thiruthondar Thogai (collection of sacred devotees) so that the great penance of the southern land might flourish. We saw this at the beginning of this Puranam. Sekkizhar Peruman reiterates this here because Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, who was Alalasundarar in the sacred Kailash (Thirukailai) to save this world, fell in love with Ambika's (Goddess Parvati's) companions, Kamalini Ammaiyar and Anithy Ammaiyar. Therefore, the Lord commanded, "You have set your heart on women; be born on this southern earth and unite with those delicate women in the joy of love." According to the Lord's command and that rule, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's incarnation took place on this earth. To bring about this divine command and event, the Lord now came as a messenger. Therefore, Sekkizhar Peruman reminds us of "Gnanam" (the world). As Sekkizhar Peruman sang in the Sakkiya Nayanar Puranam, "Seivinaiyum seivanum seivinaiyum adhan payanum serppanum" (the deed, the doer, the deed's result, and the one who brings them together), the Lord comes as a messenger to bring them together. This is the true nature of the Lord coming as a messenger. This is not only about this event, Nambi's (Sundarar's) story, but we must understand here the truth that the Lord is the one who brings together all the karmic fruits of all beings in this world. Thus, the Lord, as Nambi's messenger, arrives at the beautiful, jewel-adorned mansion of Paravai Ammaiyar. All those who had accompanied the Lord earlier stand outside. Only the Lord reaches the entrance of Paravai Ammaiyar's mansion. In what form does he arrive? He arrives alone in the guise of a Siva Vedhiya Munivar (a sage who follows Saivite rituals). Sekkizhar Peruman sings here, "Pande thammai archikkum seelamudaiya maraimunivaragi" (a sage of virtuous conduct who has worshipped Him for a long time). "Pande" means for a long time. The Lord arrives in the form of a Siva Vedhiya Munivar, an Adisaiva Sivavedhi (a primordial Saivite priest) who performs worship. Sekkizhar Peruman sings this as, "Seelamudaiya maraimunivaragi thaniye sendru anaindhar" (a virtuous Vedic sage, he went and reached alone). Lord Shiva goes as a messenger for Sundaramoorthy Swamigal twice to Paravai Ammaiyar. What is the nature of all this? These are the divine plays of the primordial Lord, performed to enable souls to experience the fruits of their karma and attain salvation. We cannot research and discover the reasons and methods of these events. The nature of karma in living beings is diverse, and living beings themselves are diverse due to karma. Therefore, the nature of the grace bestowed upon each soul is manifold. "Atpal avarkku arulum vannamum aadhi maanbum ketpan pugil alavillai kizhakka venda" (If one attempts to inquire into the manner in which He bestows grace upon His devotees and His primordial greatness, there is no limit; there is no need to question). This is a Thiruppasuram (sacred verse). Therefore, if one asks about the Lord's grace to souls and the Lord's greatness, they are beyond the comprehension and reach of finite minds. Thus, Sekkizhar Peruman's explanation of this Thiruppasuram is that there is no need for research there.

Now, the 338th verse: "Sendru mani vayil kadhavam sezhiya adaitha adhan munbu nindru pavai thiravai endru azhaippa neri men kuzhalarum ondrum thuyiladhu unarndhu ayarvar udai nadhan poosanai sei thundrum puri nul manimarbhar polum azhaithar ena thunindhu." As mentioned earlier, the Lord, in the guise of a Siva Vedhiya Munivar, goes alone and stands before the beautifully adorned gate of Paravai Ammaiyar's mansion. The Lord calls out, "Oh maiden, open the door!" This reminds us of the part "kadaithiravai" (open the door) in Thiruvachagam and Thiruvempavai. Paravai Ammaiyar, as we have seen, is restless inside, unable to sleep even a little. Thus, as Paravai Ammaiyar is awake, this call reaches her ears. She thinks, "It seems to be a sage with a sacred thread adorning his chest, who performs the worship of my Lord, who has called." She only thought it might be a Siva Vedhiya Munivar who performs the Lord's worship, but she couldn't fully conclude that it was Him. This is because she doubted whether such a highly revered person would come and call at this time and place. Due to this doubt, Paravai Ammaiyar comes and opens the door, without knowing that the one who has come is the Lord Himself. Sekkizhar Peruman presents this in the next verse.

The 339th verse: "Padhi madhi vazh mudiyarai payil poosanaiyin panipurivar padhi iravil angu anaindhadhu enna endru payam eydhi padhi umaiyal thiruvuruvil paramar avadhu ariyadhe padhimadhi vanudhalarum padhaithu vandhu kadai thirandhar." The word "padhi" (half) is repeated in this verse, creating a beautiful poetic effect. This state, where the Lord comes as a Siva Vedhiya messenger, signifies that half the purpose of the Lord's message is fulfilled here. The full purpose of this first message was not achieved. To convey this, Sekkizhar Peruman uses the word "padhi" in all four lines, creating a word-play and reiteration of meaning, which is a poetic beauty we see here. Paravai Ammaiyar, thinking it might be a Siva Vedhiya, is filled with fear, wondering why this sage, who performs the Lord's worship, has come here in the middle of the night. Without realizing that the one who has come is the Supreme Lord, who holds Uma Ammaiyar (Goddess Uma) as half of His form, Paravai Ammaiyar, with a forehead like a crescent moon, trembles and opens the door. The great Siva Acharyas (Saivite priests) perform the sacred duty of Shiva Puja (worship of Shiva), which is a virtuous act that bestows the fruit of liberation upon themselves and the world. Paravai Ammaiyar trembles, wondering why such a great person has come in the middle of the night. Without knowing that the one who called her, "Oh maiden, open the door," was Lord Shiva Himself, Paravai Ammaiyar comes and opens the door.

The 340th verse: "Mannum urimai van thondar vayil thoodhar vayilidai munnindrarai kandu irainji muzhudhum urangum pozhudhin kan ennai alum peruman ingu eydhi arulinar enna minnum maninul animarvir eydha venditru en endrar." The Lord Shiva is always a friend to Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, with the right of friendship. The messenger who came here is due to that friendship. This friendship is a two-way relationship. Seeing the messenger standing there, in the guise of a Siva Acharya, Paravai Ammaiyar bows down. She asks him, "Swami, at this midnight hour, when all living beings in this world are asleep, what is the reason for my Lord to have graced us with His presence here?" Paravai Ammaiyar addresses the Lord, who is in the guise of a Siva Acharya, as "Ennai alum Nayagaragiya Iraivare" (Oh Lord, my master). It is a Saivite tradition to consider a sage who worships Lord Shiva, a Siva Vedhiya, a Siva Acharya, as the Lord Himself, and to worship him as such. Therefore, Paravai Ammaiyar addresses him as "Ennai alum Nayagaragiya Iraivare" to indicate that the coming of a sage who worships the Lord is like the Lord Himself gracing them. In truth, the one who has come is Lord Shiva Himself, which is an implicit meaning here.

The 341st, 342nd, and 343rd verses: "Gangai neer karandha vel nee karandhavar aruli seivar nangai nee maradhu seiyin nan vandhadhu uraippadhu enna ang kayal vizhiyinarrum adhanai neer aruli seithal ingu enakku isaiyum agil isaiyalam endru solli ennandhu anaindhadhu enbal innadhu endru aruli seithar pinaiyadhiyum agil am ena piran thamum minnadai madhavai Nambi vara ingu vendum enna nan mudhalarum sala nandru nam perumai enbar panguni thirunallukku pandupol varuvaragi ingu ena pirindhu pogi otriyur eydhi ange sangili thodakka undarukku ingu oru sarvu undo neer kangulin vandhu sonna kariyam azhagidhu endrar." The Lord, who has hidden the great flood of the Ganga in His matted locks, has now come in disguise, hiding His true form. Such a Lord addresses Paravai Ammaiyar, saying, "Oh maiden, if you do not refuse what I say, I will tell you the reason for my coming." Paravai Ammaiyar replies, "If you tell me the reason for your coming, and if it is agreeable to me here, then I may agree to do it." She says, "Ingu enakku isaiyum agil isaiyalam" (If it is agreeable to me here, I may agree). "If what you have come for is agreeable to my mind, then I may agree to do it." "Isaidhal" means to be agreeable to the mind. Even if the mind agrees, some things may not be possible to do. Therefore, Paravai Ammaiyar says, "Even if my mind agrees, if I can do it, then I will do it." Hearing this, the Lord says, "Oh woman with a waist like lightning, Sundaran (Sundaramoorthy Swamigal) must come here." He says, "It is for this that I have come to you." The verse states that Nambi (Sundarar) must come here, as if it were the Lord's request. The Lord is one who has no desires or aversions. Everyone requests things from the Lord, but the Lord never requests anything from anyone. For such a Lord to come here and request Paravai Ammaiyar that Sundaran must come here, we are amazed at the greatness of Paravai Ammaiyar's penance. The Lord thus reveals the greatness of Paravai Ammaiyar's penance. Hearing the Lord say this, Paravai Ammaiyar says, "Very good, is this your greatness?" She continues, "I thought Sundaramoorthy Swamigal would come here as usual for the Panguni festival, but he has left me here and gone to Thiruvottriyur, where he is bound by Sangili (Sangili Ammaiyar). What reason does he have to come here? Is this the matter you came to tell me in the middle of the night? This is very beautiful!" Paravai Ammaiyar says this with a hint of sarcasm. Wherever Sundaramoorthy Swamigal went on pilgrimage, he would always return to Thiruvarur for the Panguni festival. Wherever he went on pilgrimage, he would sing praises of the Lord, receive gold, and come to Thiruvarur for the Panguni festival. This was Swamigal's custom. He joined Sangili Ammaiyar. Sekkizhar Peruman sings in this verse, "Sangili thodakka undarukku" (to the one who is bound by Sangili). "Sangili thodakka undarukku" also means "to the one who is married to Sangili and bound by her." This is a pun (siledai) with two meanings. Sangili Ammaiyar is like a chain that binds, which is a simile. We recall that Sundaramoorthy Swamigal himself had earlier said about Sangili Ammaiyar, "She is the one who binds my soul and beautiful flower." Paravai Ammaiyar asks, "What reason could such a Sundaramoorthy Swamigal have to come here? What attachment could he have?" She says with sarcasm, "For this matter, you, who should not have come at this time, have come here. This is very beautiful!"

The 344th verse: "Nadharum adhanai kettunangai nee Nambi seidha edhangal manathu kolladhu eydhiya veguli neengi no thagavu olitharku andro nunnaiyan vendikondadhu adhalin maruthal seiya adhadhu ena aruli seithar." Hearing this, the Lord says, "Oh maiden, you must not hold in your heart the faults Nambi has committed. You must set aside your anger and make Nambi come here. Was it not to remove this painful situation that I requested you? Therefore, it is not proper for you to refuse." The Lord says, "At least for my sake, you must accept this without refusing."

The 345th verse: "Arumarai munivarana aiyarai thaiyalardham karumam idhaga neer kadaithalai varudhal nundham perumaikku thaguvadhu endral otriyur urudhi petrar varuvadarkku isaiyen neerum pom ena maruthu sonnar." Paravai Ammaiyar addresses the Lord, who has come as a rare Siva Vedhiya, saying, "If this is the action you have undertaken, if this is the reason you have come to my doorstep for this matter, then it is not fitting for your greatness. You have come to my doorstep now for this matter, considering it a task, considering it a superior act. This is not fitting for your greatness," says Paravai Ammaiyar. Therefore, we realize that all these conversations are taking place at the doorstep of the house. So, Paravai Ammaiyar says, "It is not fitting for your greatness, who has the honor of worshipping the Lord, to come here now for this matter." What is the greatness of a Siva Vedhiya? It is the greatness of being an archaka (priest) who touches and worships the Lord. The form in which he came was that of a Siva Acharya who worships the Lord of Thiruvarur. Therefore, it is a great blessing to be born to worship the Lord of Thiruvarur, who resides in the anthill. Such greatness, and not only that, the greatness of being one who touches the sacred form thrice a day, the greatness of being born in Thiruvarur – the Siva Acharya's sacred form possesses many such distinctions. And implicitly, it is also not fitting for the true nature of the Lord hidden within that sacred form and for His greatness. Therefore, Paravai Ammaiyar says, "He, who has taken a vow in Ottriyur and married Sangili, I will not agree to his coming here. You too may leave from here," she says angrily.

The 346th verse: "Nambardham adhanai kettunagayum utkondum meimai tham parisu ariya kattar thani perum thozhanar tham vemporu vetkai kanum thiruvilayattin mevi vambalar kuzhalinardham maruthathai kondu meendar." The Lord, hearing that reply, smiles inwardly. Without revealing His true nature to Paravai Ammaiyar, and engaging in the divine play of witnessing how His unique great friend, Nambi, is suffering with longing, He accepts Paravai Ammaiyar's refusal and returns to Sundaramoorthy Swamigal. We will continue to contemplate. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 56 - Yeyar Kon Kalikkaama Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Sekkizhar Peruman's Periya Puranam, Part 894, Vampara Vari Vandu Sarukkam, Eyarkon Kalikkama Nayanar Puranam. Paravai Ammaiyar did not give her consent even when the Lord went as a messenger. So, now the Lord returns to Sundaramoorthy Swamigal. Sekkizhar Peruman shows us how Sundaramoorthy Swamigal was agonizing during this intervening period.

The 347th, 348th, and 349th verses: "Varavu parthirundha thondar nadharai arivillathen nannudhal pulavi neekki podharath thozhudhen endru pulambuvar paravaiyarai kadhalin isaiu kondu varuvadhe karuthu kolvar poi aval manaiyil nannum unniyar en seitharo nayanar thammai kandal marukkumodhan aya en ayarvu thannai arindhu ezhundharulinar tham seiyizhai thunai theerthu andri meelvadhu seiyar endru vazhi edhirkollach selvar varavu kanadhu meelvar azhivura mayangi nirpar asaivudan iruppar netri vizhiyavar thazhthar endru meelavum ezhvar maran pozhi malar mari vizha odhunguvar punkan." Sekkizhar Peruman describes Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's mental state and actions after sending the Lord as a messenger. After sending the Lord as a messenger, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal is awaiting the Lord's successful return after completing the mission. However, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal laments, "I, the ignorant one, worshipped and pleaded with my Lord to remove Paravai's anger." Sundaramoorthy Swamigal waits there, believing that the Lord will return only after obtaining Paravai Ammaiyar's consent, with her love for him overflowing. The next verse describes the doubts Nambi (Sundarar) had in his mind about the outcome of the message and the conclusion he himself reached. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal thinks, "What did the Lord do after going from here to Paravai? Will Paravai refuse if she sees the Lord? The Lord, who knew my true sorrow, has come. He will return only after resolving Paravai's small quarrel; He will not return without resolving it." Thus, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal thinks. Therefore, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal walks a short distance to meet the Lord, the messenger, who is returning. Seeing that He has not come, he returns. He stands confused and distressed. He sits and rises again, thinking that the Lord with the eye on His forehead is delaying. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal is thus suffering. In the first two verses, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal is thinking in his mind, and at the same time, his body is restless in one place, experiencing the same emotions that Paravai Ammaiyar experienced, as we saw earlier.

The 350th verse: "Paravaiyar thambal Nambi thoodharam pangil pona aravu ani sadaiyar meende ariyumaru anaiyum podhil iravudhan pagalai thondru edhir ezhundhanaiya vitta uravu neer vellam pola ongina kazhippil sendrar." Lord Shiva, who went to Paravai Ammaiyar as Nambi's messenger, returns from there, revealing His true form, and appears before Sundaramoorthy Swamigal. Unlike when He went to Paravai Ammaiyar in the guise of a priest, He now appears before Nambi in His true form. The dark midnight, due to the Lord's arrival, appears with a light like day. A great light thus makes the night appear as day. When Sundaramoorthy Swamigal saw the Lord, the joy that arose in his mind dispelled the sorrow he had experienced before. Therefore, the night becoming day is the truth here. Just as water that had been dammed up flows very fast when the dam breaks, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal went forth with great joy, like water without restraint. The Thiruvachagam verse, "Sirai pera neer pola" (like water without restraint), also states this.

The 351st verse: "Sendru thambiranai thazhndhu thirumugamuruval seiya ondriya vilayattu orar urudhi seidhu anaindhar." As soon as he saw Him, Swamigal went with great joy, thinking that his Lord had returned victorious. He bowed down to the Lord and saw the slight smile on Lord Shiva's face. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, not understanding the Lord's divine play, says to the Lord, "Oh Lord, you have removed Paravai's anger and obtained her consent. Just as you graced me that day by making me your own, so too today you have shown your grace. You have come after removing all of Paravai's sulking." Sundaramoorthy Swamigal bows down. Seeing Sundaramoorthy Swamigal saying this, not knowing that Paravai had refused there, the smile appeared on Swamigal's face. However, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, not knowing the divine play the Lord had undertaken, says this. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal thinks that the Lord, who had told him, "I am going to Paravai as your messenger," has accomplished that task. But in that verse, we see that the Lord only said, "I am going as a messenger to Paravai," and did not say, "I am going to remove Paravai's anger." We also see that subtlety here.
